A narrowed `?T` works as a `T`: in arithmetic, comparisons, conditions, `!` and `-`, casts, indexes, slice bounds, ranges, `switch`, `assert`, `bitcast`, the `bits` builtins, C's variadic arguments, and a conversion to `T` in an argument, a declaration, an assignment or a `return`. In each of those places a `?T` that may be nil gives "`?int` may be nil, so it needs `!!`, `??` or a check against `nil` first".
       - IR: no new kind. A `?T` value is two operands, as a slice is: the first has the type `?T` and holds `T`'s bits, and the second is a `bool`. A local or a parameter takes two slots, `x: ?int` and `x: bool`, and a function's parameter count counts both. A call passes two words for each `?T` argument, and its result is the call, typed `?T`, followed by `second bool`. `ret ?int %3, %4` returns both halves, and a constant pair prints as `nil, false` or `5, true`. `x!!` is a `check-nil bool` of the flag and a `drop`. `x ?? y` is a typed `if` on the flag, with `y` in its `else`. `x == nil` is a `not` of the flag, and `x != nil` the flag itself, after a `drop` of the value. Narrowing reads a `load` of a `bool` slot that follows a `?T` slot as a test of that `?T`.
       - Backends: both read a `?T` as a `T`. On arm64 a `?T` result comes back with the value in `x0` or `d0` and the flag in `x1`, and each half of an argument takes the next register of its class, so a `?f64` takes a float register and an integer register. The C backend passes the two halves as two parameters and returns a `?T` as `struct chibi_<prefix>flagged_<id> { T value; uint8_t present; }`, one struct for each `?T` that a function returns. The reference interpreter returns both halves.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - No implicit unwrap: a `?T` that may be nil is an error wherever a `T` is needed, as a `?*T` is. `x == 5` needs `x` narrowed first, and so does `x == y` with both optional, so equality of two optionals isn't built.
         - `if b` with `b: ?bool` needs `b` checked first, as any other use does. A `?bool` is never true or false by itself.
         - A cast of a narrowed `?T` casts its value: `f64(m)` with a narrowed `m: ?i32` gives an `f64`, and `?i64(m)` gives (i64(m), true). A cast of a `?T` that may be nil gives the error above, except `?T(x)` of the same type, which keeps `x` as it is. `?i64(m)` and `z: ?int = m` with `m: ?i32` that may be nil give "`?i32` and `?int` don't mix without a conversion".
         - `?int(nil)!!` gives "this is always `nil`, so `!!` always traps", as `?*T(nil)!!` does, and `?int(5) ?? y` checks `y` in a branch that never runs and drops its value.
         - The flag's slot is weighed for the arm64 backend's registers as the value's slot is.
       - Tests: the shared programs gain `VALUE_OPTIONALS`, 31 functions, of which the 21 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter, and the arm64 tests call those 21 with 61 expected results, 7 of them traps: a nil `!!`, conversions out of range and overflows. They cover each kind of `?T` as a local, a parameter and a result, `?f32` and `?f64` in float registers, a constant `?f32` result, `?bool` and an enum, nine `?T` parameters that pass on the stack, a `?T` beside a struct passed by value, `??`, `??=` and `!!`, narrowing in loops, after a `defer` and through `and`, and comparisons with `nil` on either side. A Python model of the rules gave each expected result, and the C backend agreed with it on 6,147 results. The checker's tests gain an IR case of 12 functions: assignments and `??`, a `?i32` widened after `??`, enum and float optionals, calls, `??=`, constant pairs, facts known on both sides of a test, casts, a narrowed `?int` returned and used as the right side of `??`, constant `?u64` and `?f64` values folded, `?int(5) ?? y`, a function whose `?int` result is inferred, and `-x` and `!b` of narrowed values. A diagnostics case gives 37 errors in 4 functions, one for each use of a `?T` that may be nil and for each form that isn't built yet, and the `bits` case gains a `?int` argument.
       - Planted bugs: 94, 79 in the checker, 7 in the arm64 backend, 6 in the C backend and 2 in the reference interpreter. A first round of 85 on x86 caught 60, 4 didn't compile until rewritten, and 21 passed. 13 of those got the tests above: a `?i128` local, the folds of constant `?u64` and `?f64` values, a narrowed `?int` returned and used as the right side of `??`, `?int(5) ?? y`, a `?int` in a `bits` builtin, in C's variadic arguments, in slice bounds and in a range, a `bool` parameter after a `?*int`, which narrowing must not read as its flag, the inferred `?int` result, and `vo_mixed`, whose `?T` beside a struct needs a C temporary for each half. 3 showed code that nothing needs, which is gone: a size and an alignment for a `?T` in memory, which isn't built yet, and a flag slot in `add_local`, which only loops over arrays and slices call, whose elements are never a `?T` yet. 3 fail only under qemu, and 2 change nothing that a program can see. A second round of 94 on the final code caught 81 of the 87 for x86: 64 through the checker's tests, 8 through the C backend's, 7 through asserts and 2 through ASan. Under qemu, the arm64 tests caught 3 more: two that miscount a `?T` argument on the stack, at the backend's assert that a call's stack arguments fit, and a flag slot left unweighed, which the backend gives no storage, so `vo_floats(3, 8)` gave -2. Of the arm64 backend's 7, its tests caught 3. Two plants passed every run and got tests: `-x` and `!b` of a narrowed `?T` that gave the value unchanged, now the IR case's `negate`, and a `?f32` result moved as an integer, which left `vo_fhalf`'s constant `0.75` in `x0`. One showed that the nil test of a flag needs no width of its own, so `check-nil` tests all 64 bits again, as it does for a pointer. The last 4 change nothing that a program can see: a C flag declared `int8_t` in place of `uint8_t`, which holds only 0 and 1; float halves of arguments counted as integer registers, which only sends more calls the general way; a call's flag result held as an `i64`; and the order of the two moves of a `?f64` result, which go to different register files.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no value optionals: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 1.4 million more instructions, 0.08%, 1,869.2 million against 1,867.8 million: `binary` 0.8 million, for the test of both operands' types before it unifies them, so that `nil == x` reaches the path for a `?T`; arm64's `emit_function` 0.56 million, for the test of a `?T` at each call's result and each `return`; `check_body` and `check_decl` 0.86 million, for the tests of parameters and results, 0.2 million of it in `is_pair_type`, now out of line; and `walk` with `condition` 0.53 million fewer, now that clang inlines `condition`, whose error path moved out of line, and `assign_to` 0.44 million fewer. The first version cost 24.1 million, 1.29%, because each operator's path tested its operands for a `?T` at its top. Those tests now sit on the paths that a `?T` operand takes anyway: the not-`bool` errors of conditions, `and` and `or`, and the error branches of unary operators and casts. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 1.5 million more, 0.08%, 1,873.1 million, 0.21% more than the C build, against 0.20% before. Its first build ran 3.6 million more, all in `walk`: clang kept `decl` in a register through `walk`'s loop and reloaded it after each call, 3.6 million loads, for the one store when a signature reaches its body. `walk` now reads that declaration from `c->body.decl` there, which costs the C build 20 thousand instructions and saves the translation 2.1 million. The translation keeps 274 `!!`.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, and the pairs above are new uses of `?T` types, `second`, `check-nil` and `ret`. The shared test programs change: `VALUE_OPTIONALS` at the end of `BACKEND_PROGRAMS` (the C grid's function count is now 436), and `ir_reference.c` reads a `?T` as its value type and returns both halves. `check.h` gains `check_is_flag_optional`.
       - Not built yet, each giving "the checker doesn't support this yet" (step 3e.4): a `?T` in memory, as a struct field, an array or slice element, a global, a constant, a parameter default or the target of a `*?T`, and `mem.size_of(?T)` and `&x` of a `?T` local; `x ?? y` where both may be nil; a conditional whose value is a `?T`; `?T` of 128-bit integers, structs, slices and strings; and the random-program generator. An `extern fn` can't pass or return a `?T`: "C has no `?int`".
     - **3e.4 Optionals in memory: built 2026-11-24,** with the layout of the Optionals row of 2026-11-15. The `?T` of step 3e.3 now lives in memory: as a struct field, an array or slice element, the target of a `*?T`, a global, a constant, a parameter or field default, a `..?T` rest parameter, a tuple element and a named result, and `mem.size_of(?T)` and `mem.align_of(?T)` give its size and alignment. In memory a `?T` is laid out as C lays out `struct { T value; bool present; }`: the value at offset 0, the flag byte at offset `size_of(T)`, and padding to `T`'s alignment, so `?bool` and `?u8` take 2 bytes, `?i16` 4, `?i32` and `?f32` 8, and `?int` and `?f64` 16, each aligned as `T`. `nil` is all zero bytes, so zeroed memory and a struct literal that leaves out a `?T` field read as `nil`.
       - IR: no new kind. A `?T` loads from memory as `load-at ?T %p` followed by `second bool`, as a slice does, and stores as `store-pair ?T %p, %v, %f`. Copies of structs and arrays stay `copy`. A local, a parameter and a result stay a pair of slots or registers, as in step 3e.3.
       - Constants: a top-level `?T` constant is `nil` or a present value, which the checker holds as the value's exact number with a mark for present. `const K: ?int = 3`, `K != nil`, `K!!`, `K ?? 7`, `K + 1` and a present constant converted to a wider `?T` fold at top level: `const W: ?int = K8` with `const K8: ?i32 = 3`. In a function body, naming `K` gives the pair (3, true), known present, so `K + 1` needs no check, as a narrowed local doesn't. Constant tables and globals hold the value's bytes and the flag byte: `const T := ?i16.[1, nil, -3]` and `g: P = .{ .c = nil }`. A parameter default such as `x: ?int = 5` passes the pair, and a field default such as `a: ?int = 4` stores it; a default of `0` is a present zero, so zeroing alone doesn't give it.
       - C: an `extern` global can be a `?T`, as `extern chibi_c_maybe: ?i32`, and an `extern fn` or `export fn` can pass and return structs with `?T` fields and `*?T`, laid out as the C struct above. A struct with a `?f32` field isn't a float-only struct for the registers, because C's `bool` makes it not one. A `?T` by value and a `[]?T` still can't cross: "C has no `?int`".
       - Backends: on arm64, `load-at` of a `?T` loads the value with `T`'s width and the flag with `ldrb` at offset `size_of(T)`, and loads the value first when the flag's register is the address's; `store-pair` stores the value with `T`'s width, from a float register for a float, and the flag with `strb`, from `xzr` for a zero constant. The C backend writes `struct chibi_<prefix>flagged_<id> { T value; uint8_t present; }` once for each `?T` that the program uses, before the structs, and declares fields, array elements and `extern` globals with it; a load copies the value with `memcpy` and reads the flag byte, and a store writes both. The reference interpreter reads and writes the same bytes.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A present `?T` constant works as its value in a body, as a narrowed local does: `K + 1` with `const K: ?int = 3`.
         - A `?T` constant converts to a wider `?U` at top level only when it's present, as a `?T` in a body converts only when narrowed: `const M: ?int = N` with `const N: ?i32 = nil` gives "`?i32` and `?int` don't mix without a conversion".
         - `&x` of a `?T` local stays refused. The arm64 backend groups a frame's slots by their size, so the value's slot and the flag's slot aren't laid out as C's struct is, and a `?T` local whose address is taken would need one home for both. Pointers to a `?T` come from fields, elements and globals.
       - Tests: the shared programs gain `STORED_OPTIONALS`, 28 functions, of which the 15 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter, and the arm64 tests call those 15 with 48 expected results, 6 of them traps. They cover every kind of `?T` as a struct field, in arrays and slices, through `*?T` and `&s.f`, in globals, constant tables and defaults, nested structs, copies, `for` over `[]?T`, `..?int` and `..?f32` rest parameters, a tuple `(int, ?bool)` and a named `?int` result, float constants stored beside live float locals and beside float arguments that wait for a call, and a present zero default. A Python model gave each expected result. Both backends' C tests gain an `extern ?i32` global that C and chibi read and write, and `chibi_c_maybes`, which takes a 12-byte struct of `?i32` and `?u8` in two registers and a 20-byte one with a `?f32` by pointer, and returns the 20-byte one through memory. The checker's tests gain an IR case of `?T` fields, `*?T`, a global, constants, a default and `for` over `[]?u8`, a declarations case of constants, tables, globals and defaults with their folds, and a diagnostics case for what stays refused.
       - Planted bugs: 49, 30 in the checker, 10 in the arm64 backend, 6 in the C backend and 3 in the reference interpreter. A first round caught 41, 4 didn't compile until rewritten, and 4 passed. Of those 4, a present `?T` constant that a body reads as maybe nil now fails `so_constants`, which adds a present `?int` constant to an `int`. One showed code that nothing needs, which is gone: a test that wrote zero bytes for a nil constant's value, which is zero already. A plant that built a float constant for a `?f32` field with the integer scratch register's number, so that `fmov` wrote over the float register `v17`, passed until `so_fconst` passed two float temporaries to a call beside a struct literal of float constants. A second round of 49 on the final code caught 48: on x86, 22 through the checker's tests, 9 through the C backend's, 4 through asserts and 3 through ASan; under qemu, the arm64 tests caught 6 through wrong results and 3 through crashes, and the float plant above through `so_fconst`. The one left declares an `extern` `?T` global in the C as its value's type, which changes nothing that a program can see, because the C backend reads and writes a global through its address.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no value optionals: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 0.12 million fewer instructions, 1,869.1 million against 1,869.2 million, all of them in `walk`. The first version cost 6.2 million, 0.33%: 5.9 million in arm64's `emit_function`, where clang inlined the new test of a `?T` in `check_type_align` into the loop that lays out a frame's slots, and 0.26 million in `walk`, where clang called the longer `is_storable` out of line. That test now sits in `compound_align`, out of line, which only kinds from `TYPE_OPTIONAL` on reach, and `is_held` and `is_storable` are `ALWAYS_INLINE`. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 0.58 million fewer, 1,872.5 million against 1,873.1 million, all of them in `walk`, and 0.18% more than the C build, against 0.21% before. The translation keeps 275 `!!`, one more, in the declarations dump of a constant `?T`.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind. `load-at ?T` with `second bool` and `store-pair ?T` now take a `?T` as well as a slice: `T`'s width at offset 0 and one flag byte at offset `size_of(T)`. The shared test programs change: `STORED_OPTIONALS` follows `VALUE_OPTIONALS` in `BACKEND_PROGRAMS` (the C grid's function count is now 451), `ir_reference.c` reads and writes the flag byte, and `check_type_size` and `check_type_align` give a `?T` its C layout.
       - Not built yet, each giving "the checker doesn't support this yet": `&x` of a `?T` local; `x ?? y` where both may be nil; a conditional whose value is a `?T`; equality of two optionals; `?T` of 128-bit integers, structs, slices and strings; a `?T` by value or a `[]?T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`; and the random-program generator. `x ?? y` where both may be nil, a conditional whose value is a `?T` and equality of two optionals came with step 3e.5.
     - **3e.5 Pair values from conditionals and `??`, and `==` of two optionals: built 2026-11-24.** The value of a conditional can now be a pair: a `?T` of step 3e.3, a slice or a string. `if a > b then x else y` with `x, y: ?int` gives a `?int`, and so do `if c then 5 else y`, `if c then nil else x` and `if c then k else y` with `k: i32`, which convert the plain branch as an assignment would. `if c then s else t[1..k]` gives a `[]int`, and `if c then "left" else "right"` a `string`. `x ?? y` takes a `?T` on the right too and gives a `?T`, evaluating `y` only when `x` is nil, so `x ?? y ?? 7` gives an `int`. `x == y` and `x != y` compare two `?T` of the same type: they are equal when both are nil, or when both are present with equal values. At top level, `K == L`, `K ?? L`, `if true then K else L` and conditionals of string literals fold.
       - IR: no new kind. A typed `if` whose type is a pair passes both halves at `else` and `end`, as `ret` does: `else ?int %0, true` and `%6 = end ?int %4, %5`, followed by `%7 = second bool`, as after a `call` or a `load-at` of a pair. `x ?? y` with a `?T` on the right is a typed `if` on `x`'s flag whose then-branch passes `x`'s value with `true`, and whose `end` passes `y`'s halves. `x == y` compares the values with `eq`, keeps `x`'s flag in a hidden `bool` slot, printed `$8`, and gives `(fx == fy) & (!fx | xv == yv)`; `x != y` gives `(fx != fy) | (fx & xv != yv)`. A side that is known nil gives the other side's flag test, as `x == nil` does, and a side that is known present, such as a narrowed local or `?bool(true)`, gives `fy & xv == yv`, or `!fy | xv != yv`. No form reads the value of a nil, so a nil from C whose value bytes are stale equals every other nil.
       - Backends: on arm64, `else` chooses the result's two registers: those that the then-branch's halves sit in when they are pool registers, and free ones otherwise, with the first in a float register for `?f32` and `?f64`. It moves the halves there, through `x16` when they are crossed, as `ret` does, and frees both registers for the else-branch. `end` moves the else-branch's halves into the same two registers and gives the second to the `second` after it. The C backend assigns the second half at `else` and `end` too, and the reference interpreter carries it.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Two nils are equal, a nil and a present value are not, and two present values compare their values, as in Swift and Kotlin. The value of a nil is never read.
         - `x == 5` with `x: ?int` stays refused: "`?int` may be nil, so it needs `!!`, `??` or a check against `nil` first". `x == ?int(5)` compares. Swift and Kotlin accept `x == 5`.
         - A conditional of a `T` and a `?T`, or of `nil` and a `T`, gives a `?T`. Two `?T` branches must have the same type: `if c then y else x` with `y: ?i32` and `x: ?int` gives "`?i32` and `?int` don't mix without a conversion", although a plain `i32` branch converts. In the same way, `y ?? x` gives "`?int` and `i32` don't mix without a conversion", and `y == x` refuses the mix too.
         - A conditional with a constant condition keeps only the branch that it takes, so `p := if debug then x else nil` with `const debug := false` gives "`nil` needs a type here", as it does for pointers. `p: ?int = if debug then x else nil` works.
         - A string literal beside a `string` branch becomes a `string`, and two literals give a `string`.
       - Tests: the shared programs gain `OPTIONAL_BRANCHES`, 21 functions, of which the 13 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter, and the arm64 tests call those 13 with 48 expected results, 5 of them traps, and check that `ob_pick2`, a conditional of slices, moves registers 4 times. They cover `??` chains with a `?T` on the right, conditionals of `?int`, `?f64`, `?f32`, `?bool`, `?u8`, a `?` enum, slices and strings with plain, `nil` and `i32` branches, nested conditionals, a conditional in a `defer` with `continue` and `break`, stores to fields and array elements, constant conditions, `==` and `!=` of each kind against nil, present constants and each other, a `?f64` flag that waits across a call, and a slice's length passed straight to a call. A Python model gave each expected result. Both backends' C tests gain structs of a `?f32` and a `?f64`, passed and returned by value, and a nil from C with stale value bytes that must equal another nil. The checker's tests gain an IR case of each form, with branches that constant conditions drop, a declarations case of the top-level folds, a diagnostics case of what stays refused, and an A/B case that runs the new forms inside a `defer`.
       - Planted bugs: 52, 29 in the checker, 14 in the arm64 backend, 5 in the C backend, 3 in the reference interpreter and 1 in the IR printer. The first round caught 44: on x86, 24 through the checker's tests, 8 through the C backend's and 1 through an assert; under qemu, the arm64 tests caught 3 through wrong results, 3 through asserts and 5 through crashes. 3 didn't compile until rewritten, and 5 passed. Of those 5, two showed code that nothing needs, which is gone: a test of a pair in `settle_value_if` that `may_widen` already makes, and a test in arm64's `is_pair` that kept out 128-bit integers, which never reach the `else` and `end` cases. Three got tests: the IR case of dropped branches, for a plant that kept a dropped branch's second half; the nil from C with stale value bytes, for a plant that compared the values in `!=` without the flag; and `ob_spill`, for a plant that gave the second half the pair's register class, so that a `?f64` flag spilled across a call came back through a float register. A second round of the 6 rewritten or newly tested plants caught 5. The last, which took new registers at `else` instead of the then-branch's, only adds moves, and the move count of `ob_pick2` now catches it.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no conditionals of pairs and no `?T`: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 2.6 million fewer instructions, 1,866.5 million against 1,869.1 million, all of them in arm64's `emit_function`, and checking runs the same 681.4 million. The changed lines run 0.5 million more, for the test of a pair type at each typed `else` and `end`, and the rest of `emit_function` 3.1 million fewer with no work added or removed, as clang allocates its registers differently. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 1.6 million fewer, 1,870.9 million against 1,872.5 million, also all of them in `emit_function`, and 0.24% more than the C build, against 0.18% before. The translation keeps 275 `!!`.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, but a typed `if` of a pair type now passes two operands at `else` and `end`, and a `second` follows the `end`. The shared test programs change: `OPTIONAL_BRANCHES` follows `STORED_OPTIONALS` in `BACKEND_PROGRAMS` (the C grid's function count is now 464), `ir_reference.c` carries the second half through `else` and `end`, and both backends' C tests gain the `Mf` and `Md` structs.
       - Not built yet: a pair value from `switch` arms or labeled blocks, which gives "the checker doesn't support this yet"; `x == 5` with `x: ?T`; `&x` of a `?T` local; `?T` of 128-bit integers, structs, slices and strings; a `?T` by value or a `[]?T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`; and the random-program generator.
   - **3f. Errors;** question 16 decided. Built in six parts: error values, `E!T` results, `try` and `catch` as step 3f.1, written error sets as step 3f.2, their inference as step 3f.3, the errors that a handler deals with leaving a set as step 3f.4, `try` in a function whose result is inferred as step 3f.5, and `defer catch` as step 3f.6.
     - **3f.1 Error values, `E!T` results, `try` and `catch`: built 2026-11-24,** with the codes of the Errors row of 2026-11-11 and the layout and registers of the row of 2026-11-15. `error` is a type of its own, a 16-bit code. `error.not_found` names a code: the first name the checker meets gets 1, the next new name 2, and so on, up to 65,535 names in a program, and 0 means no error. A code can be a local, a parameter, a field, an array element, a global or a constant, and it compares with `==` and `!=` only. A function can return `E!T` or `!T`, where `T` is `void`, `bool`, an integer of up to 64 bits, a float, an enum, a pointer or a `?*T`, and `E` is `error`, the set of every name, until step 3f.2 brings sets. In such a function, `return x` succeeds with `x`, `return error.empty` and `return e` fail with a zero value, `return f(x)` passes on a result of the same type, and a `!void` function succeeds when it returns nothing or reaches its end. `try f(x)` gives `f`'s value, or returns `f`'s error after running the pending `defer`s, in a function that returns `E!U` or `!void`. `f(x) catch v` gives `v` when `f` fails, `f(x) catch { … }` runs the block, which may reach its end only when `T` is `void`, and `f(x) catch e => …` passes the code to the handler, a value or a block, as `e`. Any other use of an `E!T` is an error, "`!int` can fail, so it needs `try` or `catch` first", and so is `try` or `catch` on a value that can't fail: "`int` can't fail, so `try` does nothing".
       - IR: no new kind. `error` is a 16-bit unsigned scalar, and a constant code prints as its name: `ret !int 0, error.negative`. An `E!T` is a pair like a `?T` of step 3e.3, the value and the code: `ret !int %5, 0`, and after a call, `%2 = second error`. A `!void` is one value, the code: `%1 = call !void w(%0)` and `ret !void error.big`. `try` stores the code in a hidden `error` slot that all of a function's `try`s share, printed `$1`, loads it for `ne error %4, 0`, and inside the `if` runs the `defer`s, loads the code again and returns it: `ret !int 0, %8`. `catch e =>` stores the code in the slot `e` and loads it for the test. A `catch` with a value is a typed `if` on `eq error`, whose then-branch passes the call's value at `else` and whose else-branch is the handler, and a `catch` with a block is an `if` on `ne error` around the handler, after which the value is read. Every IR value must be read exactly once, because the arm64 backend frees a value's register at its first read, so a code that is both tested and returned, or tested and named, goes through a slot, as the flag of `x == y` does in step 3e.5. Reading it twice first gave the arm64 backend a stale spill offset, reported as "a function's frame is larger than 16 MB". The arm64 backend keeps the shared slot in a register, so after the call's own moves, `try f(x)` costs a move, a `cmp` and a branch.
       - Backends: on arm64, a function that returns `E!T` returns `T` in `x0` or `d0` and the code in `w1`, as the row of 2026-11-15 chose, and a call takes both halves as a call that returns a `?T` does. A `!void` function returns its code in `w0`. The C backend declares a struct of `T value` and `uint16_t code` for each `E!T` and returns a `!void` as a `uint16_t`, and the reference interpreter carries the code as the second half of the pair.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A `!void` function returns its code in `w0`, where a function that returns a `u16` returns it. The row of 2026-11-15 placed only `E!T`'s code.
         - An `E!T` exists only as a call's result, so each result is handled where the call is. A local, a parameter or a field of type `E!T` gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", and `_ = f(x)` gives "`!int` can fail, so it needs `try` or `catch` first", as Zig refuses to discard an error union. `_ = try f(x)` discards the value.
         - `error` has no zero value, because code 0 means no error: `e: error` needs a value, and so does a struct with an `error` field that has no default.
         - Codes follow the order in which the checker meets the names, so a new name can change other names' codes. Nothing keeps a code across builds yet.
         - `!T` and `error!T` are two types that convert to each other, until step 3f.3 gives `!T` an inferred set. Results of different value types don't convert: `return f(x)` in a function that returns `!i32`, with `f` returning `!int`, gives "`!int` and `!i32` don't mix without a conversion".
         - `try` in a function without `->`, whose result is inferred, gives "the checker doesn't support this yet" until step 3f.5. In the entry file's top-level code, `try` gives "the function returns `i32`, which can't fail, so `try` can't pass an error up". In a `defer`, it gives "`try` can't leave a `defer`", as `return` does.
         - `switch` on an `error` was refused until sets let a switch cover every code, which step 3f.2 built, and arithmetic on codes is refused.
       - Tests: the shared programs gain `ERROR_UNIONS`, 43 functions, of which the 15 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter, and the arm64 tests call those 15 with 85 expected results, 10 of them traps. They cover `try` and `catch` on `!int`, `!void`, `!f64`, `!f32`, `!u8`, `!i32`, `!bool`, an enum, a pointer and a `?*T`; handlers with and without a name, with values and with blocks; nested catches; `try` with pending `defer`s, in loops and in call arguments, which must run left to right; a handler that writes through a pointer; a global, a constant and a struct field of type `error`; and a table of 300 codes, so that codes above 255, and code 256, pass through calls, catches and a result held across a deferred call. A Python model gave 75 of the expected results, and the rest were worked out by hand and checked through the C backend. The checker's tests gain an IR case of each form, among them a `try` under a `defer` that never ends, a declarations case of `E!T` results and of folded comparisons of codes, and a diagnostics case of each new error and of what stays unsupported.
       - Planted bugs: 43, 32 in the checker, 5 in the arm64 backend, 4 in the C backend and 2 in the reference interpreter. The first round ran 39 of 41, as 2 didn't compile until rewritten, and caught 36: on x86, 23 through the checker's tests, 6 through the C backend's, 1 through AddressSanitizer and 1 through an assert; under qemu, the arm64 tests caught 5. Three passed, and two of them got tests: codes cut to 8 bits in the C struct, which the table of 300 codes catches, and an `error` field that gave its struct a zero value, which the diagnostics case catches. The third gave a call's code the type information of a `bool`, which changes no code, because that information only picks the register class, and `bool` and `error` share one. In a second round, the 2 rewritten plants, 2 new ones in the path of `_ = x`, and the plant that numbered each use of a name anew, which only qemu had caught, all failed on x86; the plant that left the code after a `try` unreachable failed only once the IR case of a `defer` that never ends was added.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no errors: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 0.15 million more instructions, 1,866.6 million against 1,866.5 million, and checking 0.23 million more, 681.6 million against 681.4 million. The first version cost checking 1.1 million, because `_ = x`, which the corpus writes 95,490 times, tested for an `E!T` first; one test of the type's kind now sends optionals, `E!T` and slices to an out-of-line function. Of the rest, `walk` runs 0.18 million more with no work added, `check_decl` 0.05 million more to set the slot field, and `peer_type` 0.04 million more for its wider test of the kind. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 0.07 million more, 1,871.0 million against 1,870.9 million, and 0.23% more than the C build, against 0.24% before. The translation keeps 281 `!!`, against 275.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, but `error` is now a 16-bit unsigned scalar, and `E!T` a pair whose `ret` and `call` carry the value and the code, followed by `second error`, while a `!void` is one value. The type kinds are renumbered: `TYPE_ERROR_UNION` now follows `TYPE_OPTIONAL`, which moves `TYPE_SLICE` through `TYPE_VECTOR` up by one. The shared test programs gain `ERROR_UNIONS` after `OPTIONAL_BRANCHES` (the C grid's function count is now 479), and `ir_reference.c` treats `E!T` as a pair.
       - Not built yet: error sets, a named set such as `LoadError!T`, which gave "unknown name `LoadError`", and `|`, which step 3f.2 built; `defer catch`, step 3f.6; an `E!T` local, parameter, field or element, and an `E!T` of a slice, a string, a struct, an array, a tuple, a `?T` of a value or a 128-bit integer; `switch` on codes, which step 3f.2 built; the names of codes at run time, for printing, and error return traces (the Errors row of 2026-11-11); `try` in top-level code; an `E!T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`; and the random-program generator.
     - **3f.2 Written error sets: built 2026-11-24,** with the sets and `|` of the row of 2026-11-10 and the parentheses proposed with it. `error.{ not_found, denied }` is a set, a type that holds the codes of those names, and `const FileError := error.{ not_found, denied }` names it. `FileError | ParseError | error.{ out_of_slots }` is the union of the sets, a name in two sets is one error, and `error`, the set of every name, absorbs any set that it joins: `error | FileError` is `error`. A set made with `|` takes parentheses in a signature, `-> (FileError | ParseError)!Level`. `FileError.denied` names a member, and the set must hold it. A function can return `E!T` for any set `E`, and `catch e =>` gives `e` the callee's set.
       - A set converts to a set that holds each of its names, so `return e`, with `e: FileError`, passes in a function that returns `Wide!int`, and the other way gives "`error.{ not_found, denied }` can't hold `error.eof`", which names the first name that is missing. `error` converts to no written set: "`error` can hold any error, and `error.{ not_found, denied }` can't". An `E!T` converts to `E2!T` by the same rule, so `return f(x)` checks the sets, and `try f(x)` needs the function's set to hold `f`'s.
       - A constant code such as `error.eof` keeps the type `error`, and it must belong to each written set that it meets: in a conversion, as in `return error.eof`, beside `==` or `!=`, as the other value of a value-if, and as a `switch` arm. Each gives "`error.{ not_found, denied }` can't hold `error.eof`". Where two sets meet with no expected type, in `==`, `!=` and a value-if, the result has their union, and a set that meets an `error` that isn't a constant gives `error`.
       - `switch` takes any set. An arm names one code or several, and a range gives "expected an integer type". A switch on a written set handles each of its names or has a `_` arm, else "the switch doesn't handle `error.denied`; add an arm for it or a `_` arm". A switch on `error` needs a `_` arm: "`error` can hold any error, so the switch needs a `_` arm".
       - Mistakes in a set: `error.{ a, b, a }` gives "`error.a` is already in this set at 8:24" at each repeat, with the line and column of the first, `i32 | FileError` gives "`|` joins error sets, not `i32`; a `union` holds one of several types", which points to `union` as the row of 2026-11-10 asks, `i32!int` gives "expected an error set before `!`, found `i32`", and `error.{}` doesn't parse: "expected an error name".
       - A set is a type of the kind of `error`, whose second argument indexes the list of its codes, sorted, in the checker's list buffer. A table keyed by the list's contents gives two sets with the same names one type, whatever the order or the spelling, so `&y`, with `y: E` and `E` written `error.{ a, b }`, passes to a parameter of type `*error.{ b, a }`. Membership is a binary search, and the subset test merges the two lists. A set prints as its names, in the order of their codes, because its type doesn't keep the name it was given: `fn s13(p: bool, x: error.{ not_found, denied }, y: error.{ eof }) -> error.{ not_found, denied, eof }`.
       - IR: no new kind. A set's code is the 16-bit unsigned scalar of an `error`, and the IR prints the set where it printed `error`: `%9 = load error.{ a, b, c } e` and `switch @10 error.{ a, b, c } %9 [error.a => 0, error.b => 1, error.c => 0]`. `==` of an `error` and a set prints `eq error`. The backends need no new code, but three places read the second argument of an `error` type as a sign, which was 0 for `error` and now indexes a set's list: the arm64 backend's `type_info`, the C backend's `scalar_of` and the LowIR translator's `scalar_of` now read every code as unsigned.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A constant code must belong to every written set that it meets, in `==` and in `switch` arms as in conversions, so a test that can never be true is an error.
         - `E.x` names a member and checks it, as Zig's `FileError.AccessDenied` does, and `error.x` still names any code.
         - A set prints as its names, not as the name it was given.
         - Two sets that meet in `==` or in a value-if join into their union even when they share no name, so `a == b`, with `a: FileError` and `b: ParseError`, is never true and isn't an error.
         - `error.{}` doesn't parse. Zig's `error{}` marks a function that can't fail, which chibi writes `-> T`.
         - `!T` and `error!T` hold any error until step 3f.3 infers the set of `!T`, so its messages say `error`.
         - The checks of written sets run while the bodies are checked, because each set they need is written. Once step 3f.3 infers sets, a check that needs an inferred set runs after the set pass, as the row of 2026-11-10 proposes.
       - Tests: `ERROR_UNIONS` gains 4 sets, a struct with a set field and 15 functions, which convert, join and switch on sets, store them in a struct field, an array and through a pointer, and pass them through `try`, `catch` and `return f(x)` between functions with different sets. The 6 of them that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter (the C grid's function count is now 485), and the arm64 tests call them with 29 expected results, one of them a trap. A new program, `WIDE_ERRORS`, writes a set of 33,000 names, so that codes above 32,767 pass through a struct field, a `switch`, `try` and `catch`; its 2 grid functions run in both backends, and the arm64 tests call them with 9 expected results. The expected results were worked out by hand and checked through the C backend. The checker's tests gain a diagnostics case of each new error and an IR case of a `switch` on a set, a `catch` name typed by its set, `==` of an `error` and a set, and a pointer to a set written in another order. The parser's tests gain trees of sets in signatures, with a trailing comma, over several lines and inside a tuple type, and 4 errors.
       - Planted bugs: 31, 26 in the checker, 3 in the parser and 1 in each backend. 28 compiled at first. On x86, 25 of the 27 that run there failed: 20 through the checker's tests, 3 through the parser's and 2 through the C backend's. Under qemu, the arm64 tests caught the plant that read codes as signed. Two passed, and both got tests: a table that gave two equal sets two types, which the pointer to a set written in another order now catches, and a non-constant `error` that met a set in a value-if and kept the set, which a diagnostics case now catches. The other 3 left a function or a parameter unused, which `-Werror` refuses; rewritten to keep the code but skip it, all 3 failed through the checker's tests.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no errors: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 0.27 million fewer instructions, 1,866.4 million against 1,866.6 million, all of it in `walk`, which does no less work. The first version ran 0.28 million more, most of it in `walk`: `NODE_ERROR_NAME`, placed after `NODE_ENUM_LITERAL`, renumbered the 90 node kinds after it, and placed beside `NODE_ERROR_SET`, after the other node kinds, it leaves every old kind its number. Callgrind's exact counts of the C build move by a few hundred: 197 more reads, 313 more writes and 293 more conditional branches. Its cache and branch models, a machine without prefetchers and with a predictor typical of 2004, give 0.8% fewer first-level read misses and 2.4% fewer mispredicts, from the moved code alone, because the first version, with the same branches, gave 0.5% more mispredicts. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 0.18 million fewer, 1,870.8 million against 1,871.0 million, all of it in `walk`, and 0.24% more than the C build, against 0.23% before. The translation keeps 287 `!!`, against 281.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, but a set is an `error` type whose second argument is now nonzero, the index of its codes in the checker's lists, so a backend must not read that argument as a sign: the LowIR translator's `scalar_of` changed in this step. `switch` now takes set types. The shared test programs gain 6 grid functions in `ERROR_UNIONS` (the C grid's function count is now 485), and `WIDE_ERRORS` with `wide_errors_program()`, which builds the 33,000 names, and `c_source_test.c` gains `handles_wide_error_codes`. The parser gains the node kinds `NODE_ERROR_NAME`, `NODE_ERROR_SET` and the marker `NODE_ERROR_SET_START`.
       - Not built yet: inferred sets, for `!T` and for functions without `->`, step 3f.3; the errors that a handler deals with leaving the set, as the row of 2026-11-10 proposes, step 3f.4; `try` in a function whose result is inferred, step 3f.5; `defer catch`, step 3f.6; and the rest of step 3f.1's list: an `E!T` local, parameter, field or element, an `E!T` of a slice, a string, a struct, an array, a tuple, a `?T` of a value or a 128-bit integer, the names of codes at run time and error return traces, `try` in top-level code, an `E!T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`, and the random-program generator.
     - **3f.3 Inferred error sets: built 2026-11-24,** as the row of 2026-11-10 proposes: the bodies are checked without the sets, one pass over the call graph then works out every set, functions that call each other share one set, the checks that need a set run after the pass, and their messages name where an error comes from. A function that writes `-> !T` and has a body gets a set of its own, made of the codes that it returns, the sets of the calls that it passes on with `try` or `return f(x)`, and the sets of the values that it returns as errors. A private function without `->` whose `return`s give an `E!T` gets one too: `fn g16(x: int)`, which returns `error.five` or `g1(x)`, returns `error.{ negative, five }!int`. `try` in a function without `->` still gives "the checker doesn't support this yet".
       - The pass runs once per package, after the bodies. Each inferred set is a variable, an `error` type that names a slot, and each `return` or `try` that reaches it adds an input: a code, a written set, `error`, or another variable. Tarjan's algorithm, with an explicit stack, finds the groups of variables that feed each other, and resolves each group after the groups that it reads, to the union of its inputs from outside the group, sorted and without repeats, or to `error` if an input is `error`. So `g7` and `g8`, which `try` each other, both return `error.{ top, bottom }!int`, and three functions in a cycle share one set the same way.
       - A check that needs an inferred set waits for the pass: a written set that must hold an inferred one, as in `return try g2(x)` in a function that returns `Small!int`, a switch on an inferred set, which must handle each of its names, and a constant code compared with an inferred set or named in an arm, which must belong to it. The messages about an inferred set name the input that brought the error in, found by a breadth-first search from the set through its inputs, so it is one of the nearest: "`error.{ big }` can't hold `error.negative`, which comes from a:4:22", "the switch doesn't handle `error.negative`, which comes from a:4:22; add an arm for it or a `_` arm", "`error.{ big }` can't hold any error, which comes from a:48:12", and "the switch can get any error, which comes from a:48:12, so it needs a `_` arm". A constant code that an inferred set can't hold gives the message of step 3f.2: "`error.{ big, negative }` can't hold `error.zzz`".
       - A value-if of two `error` values, where an inferred set meets another set, gives a new variable fed by both, and a value-if of `E!T` results with an inferred set on either side does too, taking a code or a set from the other side: `(if p then error.same else g1(x)) catch e => switch e { … }` must handle `error.same` and `g1`'s errors. A code that meets an `error` value with an inferred set, in `==` or a value-if, must belong to the set, as with a written set, and a non-constant `error` still gives `error`.
       - `try s4(x)`, in a function that returns `FileError!int`, with `s4` returning `!int` and never failing, no longer gives "`error` can hold any error, and `error.{ not_found, denied }` can't", because `s4`'s set is empty.
       - The declarations dump and the IR's function headers print an inferred set as resolved, in place of `!T`: `fn g2(x: int) -> error.{ big, negative }!int`. A set that gets no input prints `error.{}`: `fn g13(x: int) -> error.{}!int`. A local typed by an inferred set prints it too, `slots: x: int, e: error.{ negative }` and `%4 = load error.{ negative } e`, while the IR's instructions print an inferred `E!T` as `!T`, as before.
       - IR: no new kind, and the backends need no new code: an inferred set's codes are the 16-bit unsigned scalars of any `error`.
       - Fixed on the way, in functions without `->`: a constant, such as `return error.five` or `return 7`, beside `return f(x)` of an `E!T`, wrote `ret !int %1, %0` with operands that held nothing, and `return nil` beside `return p` of a `?int` failed an assert. Both now write a pair, `ret !int 0, error.five` and `ret ?int nil, false`, and the C backend's and arm64 tests run them.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A function that writes `-> !T` and can't fail gets the empty set, printed `error.{}`, and it isn't an error, as Zig accepts a function with an inferred set that never fails. `error.{}` still doesn't parse.
         - A group of functions that call each other shares one set, the union of the group's inputs, as the row of 2026-11-10 proposes. Zig doesn't support an inferred set in recursion.
         - A message names one origin, an input that is nearest to the set, not the path from it.
         - A written set that can't hold an inferred one names the first missing code, as for two written sets.
         - Where an inferred set meets another set in a value-if, the result is a new inferred set, their union, as two written sets join. In a value-if of `E!T` results, that union takes a code from the other side too, while a code beside an `error` value must belong to its set.

The 2 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter (the C grid's function count is now 487), and the arm64 tests call them with 9 expected results. The first version tested every function's result type after the signature's result was settled, and read its kind from the type table: 0.08 million. The test now runs where a written result type is read, and starts with `ret >= NFIXED_TYPES`, which skips the type table for the built-in types that every function of the corpus returns. Its cache and branch models give 0.25% more first-level read misses, most of them in the name table's interning, and 0.17% more mispredicts, the sum of moves of up to 84,000 either way in `walk`, `emit`, `store` and `exact_parse`, from moved code and data alone: the new branch goes the same way for every function of the corpus. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 0.17 million fewer, 1,870.66 million against 1,870.83 million, all of it in `check_decl`, which does no less work, and 0.23% more than the C build, against 0.24% before.      - **3f.4 Handled errors leave the set: built 2026-11-24,** as the row of 2026-11-10 proposes, "as in Zig's narrowed `else` capture, so removing errors needs no syntax". chibi's `switch` has no captures, so it narrows its subject: in `switch e`, where `e` is a local, a parameter or a catch's name with an error type, each arm sees `e` with the set of its labels, and the `_` arm sees `e`'s set without the labels of the other arms. `y := h1(x) catch e => switch e { error.big => 0, _ => return e }` passes on every error of `h1` but `big`: `fn h2(x: int)`, which does that with `-> !int`, returns `error.{ two, negative }!int`, where `h1` returns `error.{ two, big, negative }!int`. `error.big, error.two => return e` passes on only those two.
       - Which names narrow: the optionals' rule of step 3e.2, parameters and `for` variables and locals whose address the function never takes, and only when `switch` is followed by the bare name, and no arm of the switch assigns to it. `switch (e)`, `&e` anywhere in the function and `e = error.two` in an arm leave `e` its declared set in every arm. On `error`, the `_` arm keeps `error`, as a set can't be "any error but these"; the arms with labels still narrow.
       - Each arm checks against its own set: in `error.big => if e == error.negative then 5 else 6`, the constant gives "`error.{ big }` can't hold `error.negative`"; `return e` in the `_` arm needs a written result set to hold only the rest, so `fn h6(x: int) -> error.{ b, c }!int` can handle `error.a` from an `error.{ a, b, c }` and pass on `e`; and a switch on `e` inside an arm needs only the arm's names, so `_ => switch e { error.negative => 2, error.two => 3 }` needs no `_` arm. A value of the narrowed set keeps it: `x := e` in the `_` arm gives `x` the rest, and `return switch e { error.a => error.b, _ => e }` fits `error.{ b, c }` when `e` is `error.{ a, b, c }`.
       - How it works: the `_` arm's set is a set variable of step 3f.3, with one input, the subject's set without the labels of the other arms, which the switch adds when it closes, because a `_` arm may come before other arms. The pass resolves it with the other sets: from a written set or a resolved set without the labels, or `error`. The checks on it wait for the pass, and their messages name the `_` as the origin of an error that comes from a written set, "`error.{ b }` can't hold `error.c`, which comes from a:51:9", and the `return` in the callee for an inferred set, "`error.{ two }` can't hold `error.negative`, which comes from a:6:22". An arm with labels gets their set at once. The checker sets the slot's type to the arm's set at the start of the arm and restores it at the end, so reads in the arm need no new test, and the switch looks for assignments to the name once, when it opens.
       - Functions that call each other through a handler no longer share one set: each gets the smallest set that holds what reaches it. `h17` handles `deep` from `h18` and passes on the rest, and `h18` returns `deep`, `five` and `try h17(x)`, so `h17` returns `error.{ five }!int` and `h18` `error.{ deep, five }!int`, and a caller's switch on `h17(x)` needs only `error.five`. A group without a handler inside it still shares one set, which is the same answer. The pass sees such a group when one of the group's own inputs has labels to remove, and then repeats the union over the group's inputs, as bit sets over the codes that reach the group, with one more bit for `error`, until no set grows; sets only grow, so it ends.
       - IR: no new kind. A load of a narrowed name has the arm's set as its type, as in `%13 = load error.{ negative } e` in the `_` arm, where the slot is `e: error.{ negative, big }`. Every set holds the same 16-bit codes, so the backends need no new code.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The subject narrows, since chibi's `switch` has no captures, by the rule of step 3e.2 for which names narrow.
         - Only a bare name after `switch` narrows; `switch (e)` doesn't.
         - An assignment to the name in any arm turns the narrowing off for the whole switch, where the optionals' narrowing ends at the assignment.
         - A value read from a narrowed name has the narrowed set as its type, as Zig's capture has, where a narrowed optional keeps its type.
         - In a group of functions that call each other, each function gets the smallest set that holds what reaches it, in place of one set for the group, once a handler inside the group removes errors.

     - **3f.5 `try` in a function without `->`: built 2026-11-24,** as the owner's rule of 2026-11-11 asks: in a private function, "leaving out `->` makes the compiler infer the whole return type, both the value type and the errors". A `try` in such a function makes it return an `E!T` whose set is the function's inferred set of step 3f.3, so the `try` adds the callee's set to it: `fn k1(x: int)`, which does `y := try k0(x)` and `return y + 1`, returns `error.{ nine, negative }!int`, as `k0` does, where `try` gave "the checker doesn't support this yet". `T` is the type that every `return` value converts to, by the rule of 2026-11-15, and `void` when no `return` gives a value: `fn k2(x: int)`, which does `_ = try k0(x)` and has a bare `return` and an end that it can reach, returns `error.{ nine, negative }!void`. A caller can `try`, `catch` and `switch` on the result as on any other inferred set: a switch on `k7(x)`, which `try`s `k1` and `k4`, needs an arm for each of `nine`, `negative` and `five`.
       - A code is a failure where the function also returns a value or nothing: a function without `->` that returns a code, or a non-constant `error`, and also returns a value, has a bare `return` or can reach its end, now returns an `E!T` with the code as its error. `fn k4(x: int)`, which returns `error.five` or `x`, returns `error.{ five }!int`, where it gave "`int` and `error` don't mix without a conversion", and `fn k17(x: int)`, which returns `error.five` or reaches its end, returns `error.{ five }!void`, where it gave "the function can reach its end without returning a value of type `error`". A function whose `return`s all give codes, with no `try`, still returns an `error` value: `fn k18(x: int) { return error.only }` returns `error`.
       - Beside a `return` of an `E!T`, such as `return f(x)`, a value that isn't a constant now converts too, where it gave "`i32` and `!int` don't mix without a conversion": a narrower integer widens through the `nop` that the checker reserves before each such `return` (the row of 2026-11-15), and an `error` value becomes the failure. Step 3f.3 converted only constants there.
       - How it works: the checker settles the result after the body, from the `return`s that it collected. `try` emits its `ret` without a type and adds it to them, with the callee's set as its value, and it makes the slot for the code that it passes on, as in step 3f.1, so that slot shows that the function fails. The value type joins the values of the other `return`s, with an `E!T`'s `T` for a `return f(x)`; an untyped integer gives `int` and an untyped float `f32`, as before. If the function fails, the checker makes its set variable and rewrites each `ret` in place: `ret !void 0` for a bare `return` and the end, `ret !void error.three` for a code, `ret !int %5, 0` for a value, with `%23 = convert int %22` in the reserved `nop` when it widens, and `ret !int 0, %10` for the error that a `try` passes on. A function that can't fail settles as before. The path that step 3f.3 gave the old settling for an `E!T` among the `return`s is gone, because every such function now takes the new path, and a labeled block can't give an `E!T` yet; the old settling asserts that its type isn't one.
       - Messages: a bare `return` beside `return`s with values gives "`return` needs a value of type `!int`", as it named `int` before. A value type that an `E!T` can't hold yet, such as a struct, gives "the checker doesn't support this yet" at the first `return` with a value, and `return .red` gives "the enum of `.red` isn't known here; write its name, as in `Name.red`", as it does without `try`. `try` in a `defer` gives "`try` can't leave a `defer`", and `return try k19(x - 1)` in `k19` gives "`k19` depends on itself", as any call of a function without `->` to itself does.
       - IR: no new kind. The pairs are the `E!T` results of step 3f.1, so the backends need no new code.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `try` makes the function return an `E!T`, even when every path fails, as in `fn k31(x: int)`, which does `_ = try k0(x)` and then `return error.done`, and returns `error.{ nine, negative, done }!void`.
         - A code is a failure only beside a value, a bare `return` or an end that the function can reach, or with a `try`. A function that returns only codes returns them as values of type `error`.
         - An untyped float as `T` gives `f32` and an untyped integer `int`, as in a function without `->` that can't fail.
         - A value that isn't a constant converts beside an `E!T` result, as constants did in step 3f.3.
       - Tests: the checker's tests gain a diagnostics case of 32 functions: `try` with a value, with a bare `return` and with an end it can reach, `try` beside codes, a non-constant `error`, a narrower integer, an untyped float and an optional pointer, codes beside values and beside an end, codes alone, callers that `try` and switch on the results, a written set that can't hold one, a bare `return` beside a value, a struct and an enum literal as values, `try` in a `defer` and in a loop, `try` of the function itself, and a `try` inside a value-if; and an IR case of a `!void` and an `!int` function with every kind of `return`. In the case of step 3f.1, the `try` in a `defer` now gives the `defer`'s message, and `d10`, which `try`s a function that can't fail, returns `error.{}!void`. `ERROR_UNIONS` gains 6 functions: `er_tries`, which `try`s `er_div` and returns a code or a value; `er_tried`, which switches on its set with no `_` arm; `er_hops`, which returns nothing and `try`s twice, with a bare `return` between; `er_hopped`, which catches it; `er_widen`, which returns `try er_div(a, b)` or an `i32`; and `er_widened`, which catches it. The 3 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter (the C grid's function count is now 493), and the arm64 tests call them with 16 expected results.

Two passed: a `try` that didn't mark the function as failing, which `k31`, whose only exits are a `try` and a code, now catches; and a `try` that checked the callee's set against the function's inferred result, which nothing can observe, because an inferred result has no set yet, so the check is against `error`, which holds every set, and it records nothing. A second round, of the first of those two and the 2 written again, caught all 3.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no errors and whose functions all write `->`: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 20,000 fewer instructions, 1,866.37 million against 1,866.39 million, all of them in `check_body`, one per function of the corpus, from register allocation alone: the call that settles an inferred result is new, and it sets the result itself. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs as many as before in every function, 1,870.66 million, and 0.23% more than the C build, as before. Two earlier versions each cost one instruction per function. A new field of the checker's state for a body said that a `try` had made the function fail, and `check_body` cleared it for every function, in both builds; the slot for the code that a `try` passes on shows that now, and the field is gone. A settling call that returned the result type, in place of setting it, made clang keep the index of the body's last node in a register in the translation's `check_body`, at one more move per function. Callgrind's cache and branch models give 0.7% more first-level read misses, 39,000, most of them in `main`, where the lexer and the parser are inlined, and in the name table's interning, because the code grew by 2.4 KB and moved the static tables after it by 4,192 bytes, among them the lexer's 128 KB table; and 0.7% fewer mispredicts, 79,000, from moved code alone. The translation has 318 `!!`, as before.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind. Functions without `->` that `try` print their results as `E!T`, and the shared test programs gain 6 functions in `ERROR_UNIONS`, 3 of them on the grid (the C grid's function count is now 493).
       - Not built yet: `defer catch`, step 3f.6; narrowing by `==` and `!=`; and the rest of step 3f.1's list: an `E!T` local, parameter, field or element, an `E!T` of a slice, a string, a struct, an array, a tuple, a `?T` of a value or a 128-bit integer, the names of codes at run time and error return traces, `try` in top-level code, an `E!T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`, and the random-program generator.
     - **3f.6 `defer catch`: built 2026-11-24,** as the owner decided on 2026-11-11: `defer catch s` runs `s` only if the function returns an error from inside the block that holds it, and `defer catch e => s` names the error, where both gave "the checker doesn't support this yet". The four rules proposed with it, not yet decided, are built as proposed. An error leaves through `try`, through a `return` of an `error` value, such as `return e` or `return error.three`, and through a `return` of an `E!T` that holds an error, such as `return f(x)` when `f` fails; the end of a block, `break`, `continue` and a `return` that succeeds don't run it, and an error that `catch` handles never leaves. Plain and `catch` defers run together, the last first. A successful exit runs no `catch` code. A `defer catch` that can never run is an error. In `fn g(x: int) -> !int`, with `defer note(1)`, then `defer catch note(2)`, then `y := try f(x)`, then `defer catch e => if e == error.three { note(3) }`, a `try` that fails runs `note(2)` and `note(1)`, `return error.three` runs `note(3)`, `note(2)` and `note(1)`, and `return y` runs only `note(1)`.
       - Never runs: a `defer catch` in a function whose result can't fail gives "the function returns `int`, which can't fail, so `defer catch` never runs", and so does one in a function that returns an `error` value, with `error` in the message. When the set of a written `!T`, or of a function without `->`, comes out empty, the same message names `error.{}!int`, once the sets are solved: `fn c4(x: int) -> !int`, which only returns `x`, gives it. A `defer catch` inside a `defer` gives "no error can leave a `defer`, so a `defer catch` in it never runs". In the entry file's top-level code, `defer catch` gives "the function returns `i32`", as `try` does there.
       - The name: `e` in `defer catch e => s` is a local of the deferred statement, and it is gone after it. Its type is the function's set: `error.{ a, b }` in a function that writes `-> error.{ a, b }!int`, so `switch e` there needs an arm for each; the set of a written `!T`, which the checker solves with the function's other sets, so `switch e` in `fn c10(x: int) -> !int`, which returns `k0(x)`, needs an arm for `negative` beside `nine`; and `error` in a function without `->`, whose set is settled only after its body. As with `catch e =>`, the name can't be one that a local in scope already has.
       - In a function without `->`, a `defer catch` makes the function fail, as a `try` does in step 3f.5, so its codes are failures: `fn c8(x: int)`, which does `defer catch e => note(9)` and `return error.only`, returns `error.{ only }!void`, where without the `defer catch` it returns an `error` value. A function without `->` whose only other exits return values gets an empty set, and its `defer catch` gives the message above.
       - How it works: each `defer` records whether it is a `catch` defer, and the slot of its name. A `return` with a `catch` defer pending looks at its value before converting it: an `error` value fails, an `E!T` may fail, and any other value succeeds. A constant code, as in `return error.three`, costs no IR: each named `catch` defer stores the constant into its name before its copy. A code known only at run time goes into the slot that `try` keeps its code in (step 3f.1), the copies read it there, and the `return` reads it again, so each value is still read once. For an `E!T`, the copies of consecutive `catch` defers sit inside one `if` on the code, and a plain defer between them closes it: `return f(y - 9)` in `g` tests the code once, runs `note(3)` and `note(2)` inside the `if`, and runs `note(1)` after it. A failing `try` runs the `catch` defers in its failure branch, where its code is in the slot already. The end of a block, `break` and `continue` skip the `catch` defers. A `catch` defer whose end can't be reached stops the copying at a failure, as a plain one does; inside the `if` of an `E!T`, the code after the `if` still runs on success. A `return` with no `catch` defer pending emits the same IR as before.
       - IR: no new kind.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A `return` of an `E!T` runs the `catch` defers when its code isn't 0, with one test for each run of consecutive `catch` defers, so such a `return` pays one test when it succeeds. Every other successful exit pays nothing.
         - The name has the function's set, and `error` in a function without `->`.
         - `defer catch` makes a function without `->` fail, as `try` does.
         - A `defer catch` inside a `defer`, and one in a function whose result can't fail, are errors, as the proposed rule makes one whose inferred set is empty.
       - Tests: the checker's tests gain a diagnostics case of 15 functions: `defer catch` in functions that return `int`, an `error` value, and an `E!T` whose set comes out empty, with and without `->`; one inside a `defer`; `return`, `try` and a declaration as the deferred statement; the name used after the statement, and a name that a parameter already has; a function without `->` that returns only a code; switches on the name in a function without `->`, with a written `!T` and with a written set, and a code that the written set can't hold; callers that catch the results and switch on them; a `defer catch` whose end can't be reached; the name assigned to a smaller set; and an `error` value and an `E!T` that a written set can't hold. An IR case gives `catch` defers around a failing `try`, a constant code, an `E!T` and a plain value; in a function without `->`, in a loop left by `continue`, `break` and `return e`, in a block that ends, and before `return try`; in a `!void` with a plain `defer` between two `catch` defers; with an end that can't be reached; and a function with plain defers only, whose IR is as before. In the case of step 3c, `defer catch` in a function that returns `int` now gives the message above. `ERROR_UNIONS` gains 6 functions: `er_undo`, with plain and `catch` defers that write through a pointer, around a failing `try`, a code and an `E!T`; `er_undone`, which reports the trail; `er_tidy`, without `->`, with a named `catch` defer; `er_tidied`, which switches on its set; `er_rounds`, a `!void` with `catch` defers in a loop and a block; and `er_rounded`. The 3 that take two `int`s run on the grid in both backends against the reference interpreter (the C grid's function count is now 496), and the arm64 tests call them with 15 expected results, which a run of the C backend gave too.
       - Planted bugs: 42, all in the checker. A first round caught 39, all through the checker's tests, 7 of them also through the C grid and 3 by an assert. Three passed. A `return` that held its code in a slot with no `catch` defer pending, which costs work and changes no result, is now caught by the IR case's function with plain defers only. A `return` that held a value that its conversion had refused passed because the case's set mismatch came from an `E!T`, whose value the conversion keeps; the case now also returns an `error` value that the written set can't hold, and the plant asserts. A second round of those 2 caught both. The third can't be observed: the search after each copy for the names that the deferred statement assigns, stopped one node early, skips only the statement's root, and a root is never a name that the next node assigns, because the next node is the `defer` itself.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no errors and no `defer catch`: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte, and every function of the compiler runs as many instructions as before, in the C build and in the translated compiler built with `chibi c --release-fast`. The C build runs 1,866.37 million, 942 more than before, and the translated compiler 1,870.66 million, 822 more, 0.23% more than the C build, as before. 811 of each are in libc's `memcmp`, whose path depends on where its operands sit: the 16 `Defer` records that the checker allocates first grew by 8 bytes each, and the step 3f.5 build with those 8 bytes added, and nothing else, runs the same 811 more. The rest is in the loader and in printing the times. Callgrind's cache and branch models give 0.8% fewer first-level read misses, 45,000, most of them in `main` and the name table's interning, and 0.05% fewer mispredicts, 6,000, from moved code and data alone. The translation has 318 `!!`, as before.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind. `defer catch` emits copies of the deferred statement, stores of the code into slots, and an `if` around the copies on a `return` of an `E!T`, all of kinds that exist. The shared test programs gain 6 functions in `ERROR_UNIONS`, 3 of them on the grid (the C grid's function count is now 496).
       - Not built yet: narrowing by `==` and `!=`; `try` and `defer catch` in top-level code; and the rest of step 3f.1's list: an `E!T` local, parameter, field or element, an `E!T` of a slice, a string, a struct, an array, a tuple, a `?T` of a value or a 128-bit integer, the names of codes at run time and error return traces, an `E!T` across `extern fn` and `export fn`, and the random-program generator.
   - **3g. Globals and constant tables,** the first step after 3d in the self-hosting order below, built in two parts: constant tables (3g.1), then global variables (3g.2), after the owner chose that globals start at compile time (the Language row of 2026-11-19).
     - **3g.1 Constant tables: built 2026-11-19.** A top-level `const` can now be an array or a struct: `const squares := int.[0, 1, 4, 9]`, `const grid := [3]int.[.[1, 2, 3], .[4, 5, 6]]`, `const box := Box.{ .lo = .{ 1, 2 }, .hi = .{ 30, 40 } }`, or a typed form such as `const t: [3]u8 = .[7, 8, 9]`. Elements and fields must be constants: numbers, `bool`, enum values, names of constants, and other constant arrays and structs. A struct constant gets its field defaults and zero for the rest, as `p: Point` does in a body (3d.4). The checker writes each constant's bytes, laid out as in memory and aligned to the type, into one buffer for the package (`Ir.data`), and the constant's value is its offset there. In a function body, naming an array or struct constant emits `data`, the address of its bytes, printed `%0 = data *[8]int squares`. It is a place like `addr`, so indexing, fields, `.len`, `for` loops, copies into locals and arguments work as they do on locals. The arm64 backend puts the buffer in the same mapping as the code, between the function table and the code, and computes an address with `adrp` and `add`. The reference interpreter reads the checker's buffer. A constant struct over 16 bytes passes to a function without a copy (3d.8), because nothing can write it: its `load-at` root is `DATA_ROOT`, which the backend reads as a place that never changes. The declarations dump prints a constant array or struct as a literal: `const deep: Deep = .{ 9, 0.5, .{ 0, 0 }, .[-1, 2, -3], 200 }`.
       - Fixed while building: a nested anonymous literal at top level, such as `.lo = .{ 1, 2 }` inside `Box.{ … }`, couldn't find its type, because the checker resolved the outer struct only when its literal closed; it now resolves a literal's type when the literal opens. Top-level checking read the package's first file to decide whether a literal was an array, so a literal in another file could be misread; it now reads the file of the declaration it checks. Slicing a field of a parameter said "`a` is a loop variable"; it now names the parameter.
       - Tests: a program of 26 constants covers tables of every integer width, signed and unsigned, `f32`, `f64`, `bool` and enums, rows taken over in place, an alias (`const same := grid`), structs with defaults and nested literals, a 24-byte and an 8 KB struct passed by value, a 1.6 MB struct that puts the tables declared before it more than a megabyte from the code, and a 3-byte table that would misalign the next one. Under qemu, 32 functions give the hand-computed results on a grid of inputs and agree with the interpreter, and two more trap on indexes out of range. C calls a function that returns a 24-byte constant, and the test checks that 12 constants are aligned to their types. Checker tests cover the declarations dump, the IR, every new error and a two-file package. The tests catch all 17 planted bugs: `adrp` without its `add`, a wrong `adrp` immediate, the data copied 8 bytes off, field defaults or the zero fill skipped, a field at the wrong offset, rows taken over when they aren't adjacent, items stored before conversion, the literal's type resolved late, the first file's tokens, a copy of a constant argument in the checker or in the backend, unsigned printing, the wrong name in an error, `&` allowed on a constant, the IR printer reading the data root as a slot, and alignment. Two of them passed at first. The alignment check is new, and the backend now reads a slot's mark only for a real slot number: a backend that forgets the data root then copies the argument and trips the copy-area assert, instead of reading another function's slot. The random-program generator doesn't write constant tables yet.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no constant tables: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as 3d.8's. Callgrind counts 0.20% fewer instructions in checking and 0.03% more in code generation. The first version cost 1.24% in code generation, all of it register allocation in `emit_function` after the `data` case was added. Choosing the result register inside the out-of-line address helper, instead of at the case, removed it. Marking the helper `NOINLINE` alone, moving the emitter's new field to the end, or numbering `data` last changed nothing.
       - Found while testing and not fixed: `?int` as a value crashes the checker, with an assert at top level (`const t := ?int`) and a 16 GB allocation in a body (`x := ?int.[1, 2]`). Both were fixed in step 3e.1.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A constant array or struct is read-only. Assigning to it or to its elements or fields, `&` of it, and slicing it are errors that name it (`` `T` is a constant, so a slice can't point into it ``), until rank 10 decides read-only data. So a constant array can't be passed where a slice is expected, `const s: []u8 = .[1, 2]` is an error, and a slice field of a constant can only be empty. `t := squares` gives a local copy that can change.
         - Indexing a constant with a constant index isn't folded: `T[1]` in a body loads from the table, and at top level `T[1]`, `P.x` and `T.len` are still "not supported yet". In a body `T.len` is a constant, but the `data` it names stays in the IR as a dropped value.
         - A package's constants share one buffer of at most 256 MB, and a constant that would pass that is an error. An inner literal's bytes stay in the buffer after the outer literal copies them, except in an array of arrays or structs whose items are adjacent, which the array takes over in place. The waste is at most the size of the inner literals at each depth.
         - The buffer shares the code's mapping, which is writable on arm64 Linux. Read-only pages wait for rank 10.
         - Still errors: a field or parameter default that is an array or struct (`p: Point = .{ 1, 2 }`, `fn g(p: Point = P)`), `const` inside a function body, and a string inside a constant struct (`Str.{ .s = "hi" }`).
     - **3g.2 Global variables: built 2026-11-20.** A top-level `counter := 5`, `origin: Point` or `ratio: f64 = 1.5` in a library file declares a global. Its starting value must be a constant, as a `const`'s must until compile-time execution is built: numbers, `bool`, enum values, names of constants, and constant arrays and structs, so `copy := T` starts as a copy of the table `T`. A global without a value starts as a local does: zero bytes with its field defaults (3d.4). The checker writes the starting bytes of each global that has a value or field defaults into one image for the package (`Ir.globals`), laid out and aligned to its type, and the global's value in the declarations table is its offset there. A global with neither, or with `= undefined`, takes no bytes in the image: the checker counts it in a zeroed part, and once every declaration is checked, it places that part after the image. So `pad: [100000]int` adds nothing to the image. In a function body, naming a global emits `global`, its address, printed `%0 = global *int counter`. It is a place like `addr`, so assignment, compound assignment, fields, elements, `&`, slicing, `.len` and `for` work on a global as they do on a local. A scalar global is loaded where it is named, so `counter * 100 + incCounter()` reads `counter` before the call. A global array or struct read as an argument or a literal item is copied before a later item runs (3d.7), and a global struct over 16 bytes passed by value is always copied (3d.8), because the callee can write the global: `place_root` gives a global no root. The arm64 backend maps the image and the zeroed part together, in a read-write mapping of their own, and copies the image in. The kernel zeroes the rest, so those pages cost nothing until the program touches them. The backend puts a global's address in a register as a 64-bit constant, with `movz` and `movk`, because the mapping can land anywhere. The reference interpreter keeps its own copy of the image. The declarations dump prints a global's type and starting value, `corner: Point = .{ 3, 4 }`, and only the type of a zeroed one, `zeros: [4]int`. The errors for assigning to, or taking the address of, something that isn't a place now say "only a variable" instead of "only a local".
