With this step the generic path of `src/simd.h`, which loads 64 bytes as four `u8x16` and builds the 64-bit mask from four lane masks, can be written in chibi, and a test does so.
       - Tests: one body shows the IR of a struct whose field is an array of two vectors, built by a literal and copied, with a lane written through the field and an element and a lane read with a variable element index. A second body shows that a `[*]u8` field and a slice of `[*]u8` still load the pointer before indexing. One error case gives seven errors: a struct holding a vector as an `extern fn` parameter and as an `export fn` result, a global and a constant that hold vectors, an `extern fn(...)` type whose result holds vectors in an array of structs, a lane of a temporary, and lane 16 read through a field and an element. An older case lost two errors, for a struct parameter with a vector field and a local array of vectors. A second error case passes a struct that contains itself, with and without a vector, to an `extern fn`, and gets the same errors as before this step. A test checks an `extern fn` that takes the first of 64 levels of structs, each holding two of the next, with an `int` or a vector at the bottom; searching every path would take 2^64 steps. The reference interpreter runs 17 calls of 5 functions whose results were computed by hand, and they also run natively and agree: 64 bytes loaded as four `u8x16`, matched against a byte into a 64-bit mask with four `top_bits`, moved on by one vector and matched again, with a bounds trap on a lane; an array of four vectors written in a loop, with one lane written and one read through a nested index, and traps on the element and the lane; a 32-byte struct of a vector and an `int` passed and returned twice; a 16-byte struct of one vector passed and returned in two integer registers; and copies of a struct of four vectors and of an array of two such structs, with a lane written in one copy and read in each. One machine-code check shows that a vector local after an `int` whose address is taken sits at a 16-byte-aligned offset, so it is stored and loaded with no address computed in X8. The tests catch all 25 planted bugs: 10 by the error cases, 2 by the bodies, 4 by the hand-computed calls in the interpreter, 1 by the same calls run natively, 2 by the machine-code check, 2 by the checker's assert that a `[*]` base is not a place, 3 by crashes, and 1 by the test binary's 30-second alarm. Four plants survived the first run. Two returned the address of any field or element followed by `[`, not only a vector's; for a `[*]u8` field that skips loading the pointer, and no test indexed one, which added the second body. Two left 16-byte slots unaligned, with no padding or with padding to 8; arm64 reads unaligned vectors, so every result stayed right, which added the machine-code check. Review then found that the first search for a vector recursed without end on a struct that contains itself, and took exponential time on the 64 levels; that added the two guards, the second error case and the 64-level test, and one plant removes each guard.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which uses no vectors: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as 3i.6's, and checking runs exactly as many instructions. Code generation runs 632,526 more of 462 million, 0.14%. No corpus function keeps a struct, an array or a vector in memory, so the new frame code never runs. All of the difference is in the function that emits each function, into which the frame layout is inlined, and it is spread over that function's main loop, so clang compiled the function differently. Five other forms cost more: tracking the widest alignment as before 0.17%, starting at 16 bytes with padding before each alignment pass 0.46%, not inlining the frame layout 0.49%, a second scan of the slots only when memory slots exist 0.73%, and a separate function for the widest alignment 0.85%.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A struct that holds vectors travels as any other struct of its size, not in V registers as AAPCS64 passes a struct of up to four vectors, so it can't cross the C boundary yet. This keeps one path for structs inside chibi. A `u8x64` argument therefore goes as the address of a 64-byte copy, which the caller skips when the callee can't write the original, where AAPCS64 would use V0 to V3.
         - A call's copy of such a struct sits in the frame's copies area at 8-byte alignment, which arm64 allows for vector loads and stores.
         - Globals and top-level constants that hold vectors are refused, as vector globals and constants are.
       - Not built yet: vector globals and top-level constants, and structs and arrays that hold them; `p.*` of a `*u8x16`; `bitcast`; and structs of vectors in V registers across the C boundary. A struct literal with an array literal inside builds the array in its own slot and copies it into the struct, so a function that returns `.{ .v = .[ a, b, c, d ] }` of four vectors stores 64 bytes, copies them into the struct, and copies them again to the result; writing the literal in place would save both copies.
   - **3j. The C backend,** item 5 of the self-hosting order, in six parts: what the tests' programs use (3j.1); calls into C, `export fn`, callbacks and the entry point (3j.2); vectors and the builtins (3j.3); a driver that builds a program end to end (3j.4); `#line` directives, from source positions that the checker keeps beside the IR (3j.5); and checks that the structs C can see keep the layout of their C headers (3j.6).
     - **3j.1 Functions, control flow, memory and traps: built 2026-11-21.** `src/c_source.c` turns a checked package's IR into C that clang 18 and GCC 13 compile with `-std=c11 -Wall -Wextra -Werror`, apart from the warnings about unused variables and, in GCC, about comparisons that chibi allows and that are always false for the type, such as `x < 0` on a `u8`. The code runs under UBSan without a report. `c_source_prelude` writes what each C file needs once. `c_source_package` writes one package with a prefix on its names, or returns "the C backend doesn't support vectors yet", or the same for calls into C, callbacks and `export fn`, which 3j.2 and 3j.3 add. Everything else that the checker accepts goes through: integers, floats, `bool`, enums and `switch`, labels, pointers, arrays, slices, structs by value, `[*]T`, globals, constant tables, the bit counts, the overflow checks and the hidden context.
       - Each IR value becomes a C local `v<i>`, and each slot a local `s<k>`, all declared at the top of the function. `if`, `else` and `end` become braces. Every other jump is a `goto` to a label after the instruction it targets: `exit` and `case` jump to their `end`, `repeat` to its `loop`, and the switch's cases to the start of their arms. A C `switch` holds only `goto`s.
       - The hidden context is the first parameter, `void *ctx`, and every call passes it on.
       - A struct becomes a C struct with typed fields, in dependency order, each with a `_Static_assert` that C's size and alignment match chibi's, so that 3j.2 can pass structs to C as C does. A slice is `chibi_slice`, `{ uint8_t *ptr; int64_t len; }`, passed and returned by value. Reads and writes go through `__builtin_memcpy` at byte offsets, and copies and zeroing through `__builtin_memmove` and `__builtin_memset`, so the code doesn't depend on C's type-based aliasing, which chibi doesn't have (the Casts row).
       - No checked operation reaches C's undefined behaviour. Checked arithmetic uses `__builtin_add_overflow` and its kin. Wrapping arithmetic and `<<` go through `uint32_t` or `uint64_t`. Shifts, division, conversions from floats and narrowing test their operands first, and `%` by -1 gives 0, as in the arm64 backend. A failed check calls `chibi_trap(kind)`, which the program that links the code defines. The trap kinds moved from `src/arm64.h` to `src/ir.h`, since both backends use them.
       - `#pragma STDC FP_CONTRACT OFF` stops clang from fusing `a * b + c` into one instruction, as rank 3 recommends. GCC ignores the pragma and warns about it, so only clang gets it; GCC doesn't fuse in its `-std=c11` mode.
       - Names: a function is the prefix and its chibi name, the package's constant data and globals are `chibi_<prefix>data` and `chibi_<prefix>globals`, and the prelude's names start with `chibi_`, so a prefix may not overlap `chibi_`. Functions are `static` unless `CHIBI_FN` is defined otherwise; the test defines it as nothing to find them with `dlsym`.
       - Tests: `test/c_source_test.c` writes the C for every program it tests into up to eight files, compiles them in parallel with the C compiler that built the test, with UBSan set to stop at its first report, and loads them as one shared library. The 21 hand-written programs of the arm64 test moved to `test/backend_programs.c`, which both tests read. Each of their 225 functions that take two `int`s and return one, and 4 written for this step, run on a grid of 16 × 16 arguments, and each result or trap must match the reference interpreter's. The 4 cover structs declared after the struct that holds them, `uintptr`, constant data with the bytes `??=` and `??/`, which C11 reads as trigraphs, and the `.ptr` of an empty slice. 60 random programs from the differential generator must also match it, each entry function run six times with random arguments; `CHIBI_DIFFERENTIAL` and `CHIBI_DIFFERENTIAL_SEED` set the count and the seed, as for the arm64 test. 4,500 programs from 30 seeds agreed with clang, and 300 from 2 seeds with GCC. A table checks the message for each of the six places that refuse a program, and a chain of 64 structs that each hold two of the next must emit in linear time; these two tests run in every build. The compiled tests run only in `make test`'s native build, where the Makefile names the C compiler, and take 3.3 seconds. Of 74 planted bugs, the tests caught 73. The one left removes the parentheses around a negative float constant, which only a unary minus would need, and constant folding leaves no unary minus on a constant.
       - Bug found by the tests: `s.ptr` of an empty slice became `NULL + 0`, which C leaves undefined and UBSan reports. An `index` by a constant 0 now emits no addition, as a slice from 0 already did.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, 694,816 lines and 2.79 million IR instructions in 20,000 functions: the C is 105 MB, 151 bytes and 7 lines for each chibi line. Writing it runs 8.10 billion instructions, 2,900 for each IR instruction and 17.5 times the arm64 backend's 462 million; 57% of it is in `fprintf`. clang 18 takes 40 seconds of CPU time on the cloud's x86-64 machine to compile that C at `-O0`, in four parts, for 40.8 MB of machine code, and 354 seconds at `-O2`, for 4.2 MB. Writing the C is under 3% of a `-O0` build, so a faster writer waits until something needs it.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Every IR value is its own C local. clang `-O2` removes them, but they make the C large and `-O0` slow. A later step can fold a value that is used once into the expression that uses it.
         - Indexing a `[*]T` outside its object, and adding a variable to a null `[*]T` even when it is 0, are undefined in C. chibi doesn't check them either, but a C compiler may assume they never happen.
         - The C uses GNU extensions that clang and GCC share: `__builtin_*` functions, attributes, case ranges and empty structs. MSVC can't compile it.
         - `bool` is a `uint8_t` that holds 0 or 1, not C's `_Bool`, so that loads and stores stay byte copies.
     - **3j.2 Calls into C, callbacks, `export fn` and the entry point: built 2026-11-21.** The C backend now writes every program that steps 3h.1 to 3h.5 accept; only vectors still give "the C backend doesn't support vectors yet" (3j.3).
       - An `extern fn` becomes a C prototype under a name of the backend's own, with the C function's symbol given by an asm label: `int64_t chibi_k_c_labs(int64_t s0) CHIBI_SYMBOL("labs");`. The prelude's `CHIBI_SYMBOL` puts the platform's prefix for C symbols in front, `__USER_LABEL_PREFIX__`, which is empty on Linux and `_` on macOS. Under its own name the prototype can't clash with a C library declaration or a compiler builtin: chibi has no `char` or `void *`, so its `memset` takes and returns `uint8_t *`, and clang and GCC reject that as a redeclaration of their builtin `memset`. clang still knows the function by its symbol and may optimize the call, as it did by turning `labs` into an inline absolute value, which is right, since it is the same function. A declaration that nothing calls leaves no reference, so its C function need not exist.
       - Parameters and results keep the C types that 3j.1 gives chibi types: a pointer, a `[*]T` and an `extern fn` are `uint8_t *`, `bool` is `uint8_t`, and a struct is its C struct. C converts arguments and results through the prototype, as for any call. A function with no parameters gets `(void)`, and a variadic one `, ...` after its named parameters. C11 can't declare a function whose only parameter is `...`, so `extern fn f(..)` gives "C11 can't declare an `extern fn` that takes only `..`".
       - A call into C passes no context. A struct argument is copied into a local of its C struct and passed by value, as chibi calls already were, so a C function that writes its copy changes nothing of the caller's, and a constant table is never passed by address. C promotes variadic arguments itself; the checker has already widened `f32` to `f64` and typed every untyped constant (3h.2). A constant gets a cast to its type, as in `(int64_t)(-7)`, because the C literal `-7` is an `int`, and an `int` where the callee reads an `int64_t` with `va_arg` leaves the upper 32 bits undefined.
       - Each chibi function in the callback list gets one adapter with C's signature for its type, which calls the function with the default context and returns its result: `static int64_t chibi_k_adapter_tap(int64_t s0) { return k_tap(chibi_context, s0); }`. The prelude declares `extern void *chibi_context;`, and the program that links the code defines it, as the arm64 host sets the default context with `native_set_context`. `callback` takes the adapter's address, so every use gives the same pointer, and an `extern fn` in the list gives the C function's own address. Adapters come after the prototypes and before the function bodies.
       - An export's adapter has external linkage, and the asm label gives it the chibi name as its symbol, so C calls `step` while the chibi function stays `k_step` and chibi callers call it directly with their context. `export fn main(argc: i32, argv: [*][*]u8) -> i32` needs nothing more: its adapter is the C symbol `main`, of type `int32_t (int32_t, uint8_t *)`, which C's startup code calls as it calls any `main`.
       - Tests: a new test compiles one program of 37 functions that call 5 libc functions and 22 C functions in the test, and checks hand-computed results for inputs 0 to 19, as the arm64 test does. It covers six argument types in one call; C results narrower than 64 bits that C returns with junk in the upper bits; `f32` both ways; 16- and 24-byte structs in and out; a C function that writes its copy of a local and of a constant table; `memset`; `snprintf` with 12 conversions and a format from C, against C's own output; a C function that reads five pairs with `va_arg`, two of them negative `int` constants; a struct before the variadic arguments; `qsort` with two chibi comparators; a C function that calls a callback in a loop; a callback held in a struct field; a callback with seven arguments of seven types, one that takes and returns a 24-byte struct, and one that returns nothing; five exports called by name through `dlsym`, among them a struct both ways and a function with no parameters; an export that chibi calls directly and passes to C; and `main` with three arguments. Every use of a callback gives the same pointer, which is not the chibi function's; an export passed as a callback gives the address that `dlsym` finds; a C function passed on gives its own address; and an `extern fn` that the test doesn't define and nothing calls doesn't stop the library from loading. clang's UBSan checks the type of every call through a pointer, so the test calls adapters through the exact C types that the backend writes, with structs named as the backend names them, `struct i0_W`. The program passes with clang and with GCC compiling the C, in 0.2 seconds more. The table of refusals has one entry for the new message in place of three for the messages gone. On macOS the test links the library with `-undefined dynamic_lookup`, because Mach-O refuses a library whose symbols the program defines, such as `chibi_trap`; no macOS machine has run the C backend's tests yet. The tests catch 23 of 25 planted bugs: 14 by the C compiler's errors, 4 by a wrong result, 2 by UBSan's check of a call's type, 2 by the messages and 1 by a missing symbol. The two left can't be seen on this machine: an adapter that passes a null context, which no chibi code can read until rank 7 gives the context contents, and a `CHIBI_SYMBOL` without the platform's prefix, which is empty on Linux.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which calls no C: the C is byte for byte the same as 3j.1's apart from 7 new lines in the prelude. Writing it runs 0.09% more instructions by callgrind, 8.116 billion against 8.108; `put_call` and its callers are inlined into `c_source_package`, so callgrind can't split the difference further, and at under 3% of a `-O0` build it isn't worth more.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - C functions get prototypes under the backend's names, bound to their symbols by asm labels, which clang and GCC support. Declaring `labs` under its own name would need C's exact types, which chibi doesn't know: chibi's `*u8` may be C's `char *`, `const char *` or `void *`.
         - An adapter calls the chibi function directly, so the C compiler can inline it: at `-O2` clang inlined the function into each of the test program's adapters and dropped every load of `chibi_context`, which no chibi code reads yet. Nothing can replace the function afterwards, as the arm64 backend's `native_replace` does.
         - A C caller compiled with clang's `-fsanitize=function` that calls a chibi callback through a pointer of another C type, such as `int (*)(const void *, const void *)` for a comparator that chibi writes with `uint8_t *`, gets a report although the call is right at the machine level. Compiling the chibi code without that check avoids it, since the check needs a mark that the callee's compiler writes.
     - **3j.3 Vectors and their builtins: built 2026-11-21.** The C backend now writes every program that the checker accepts, apart from an `extern fn` that takes only `..`.
       - The prelude declares the eight vector types as GCC and clang vector types, `typedef uint8_t chibi_u8x16 __attribute__((vector_size(16)));` and so on, so vector values, locals, parameters, results, struct fields and array elements are C vectors with C's 16-byte alignment, which the structs' `_Static_assert`s check. Lanes are read and written at byte offsets through the slot's address with no new code, as in the arm64 backend.
       - A vector constant becomes a compound literal that repeats its lane, `(chibi_u8x16){ 32u, 32u, ... }`, and `splat` repeats its scalar the same way. C also accepts a scalar operand in a vector expression, but not in an assignment or a `return`, and GCC refuses one whose value changes in the lane type, so the backend always writes the whole vector.
       - Lane-wise operations are C's vector operators. Wrapping arithmetic, `-%` and `<<` go through the unsigned vector type with the same lanes and cast back, as scalars go through `uint32_t` or `uint64_t`, since C leaves signed overflow undefined. `>>` on signed lanes is C's arithmetic shift, and a shift's count is checked against the lane width before the shift. C types a comparison's mask as a vector of signed integers, so it is cast to the operands' type. A vector `store-at` copies from the value's address, because GCC refuses `(chibi_u8x16){ v }`, which it reads as lane 0 set from `v`, where clang reads a copy of `v`.
       - `lookup` and `top_bits` call `static inline` helpers. clang 18 and GCC 13 turn neither a loop over 16 lanes into `tbl` or `pshufb` nor a loop over top bits into `pmovmskb`, so each helper picks its instructions by the C compiler's target macros. On arm64, `lookup` is NEON's `tbl` and `top_bits` is the `ushr` and `usra` chain that the arm64 backend emits (3i.5). On x86, `top_bits` is SSE2's `pmovmskb`, `packsswb` with `pmovmskb` for 16-bit lanes, `movmskps` or `movmskpd`, and `lookup` is SSSE3's `pshufb` after a saturating add of 0x70, which sends every index of 16 or more to 0 as `tbl` does. Without SSSE3, which x86-64's baseline lacks, `lookup` is a loop over the 16 lanes; `CHIBI_SIMD_GENERIC` selects the loops everywhere, as it does for `src/simd.h`. A package writes the helpers only if it uses `lookup` or `top_bits`, inside the include guard `CHIBI_VECTORS`, so packages in one C file share one copy and a program without them includes no intrinsics header. The helpers are marked unused, since clang warns about an unused `static inline` function in a `.c` file.
       - Tests: a new test compiles the vector programs that the checker and arm64 tests share (`test/vectors.c`) and two small programs, one with only `lookup` and one with only `top_bits`. For each function whose parameters and result are integers, the test writes a C wrapper that takes four `int64_t` and calls it with its exact C types, because clang's UBSan checks the type of a call through a pointer. Each build runs 175 calls whose results were computed by hand, among them 24 traps, and the grid of 8 lane types, 20 operations and 256 argument pairs, 40,960 calls, against C's own vector arithmetic. On x86-64 it builds three times, with no flags, with `-mssse3` and with `CHIBI_SIMD_GENERIC`, and twice elsewhere. The packages go into three C files so that in each file a small program is the first package to use a helper, which makes a package that leaves out its helpers fail to compile, and a second copy of the `top_bits` program follows the large program, which uses both helpers, so a missing include guard fails too. `-fno-lax-vector-conversions` makes clang refuse implicit conversions between vector types, as GCC does, so a missing cast fails with either compiler. The tests pass with clang and with GCC compiling the C, and add 1.4 seconds to the native build's tests. By hand, the same programs passed on arm64 under qemu, compiled by clang and GCC with NEON and with `CHIBI_SIMD_GENERIC`, and each helper agreed with a scalar model on 200,000 random inputs on every path. 1,200 more random programs from 8 seeds agreed with the reference interpreter. Of 31 planted bugs, the native build's tests catch 28: 15 by the C compiler's errors and 13 by wrong results or traps. One more fails only with GCC, the vector `store-at` written as a compound literal, and one only on arm64, a NEON helper. The one left shifts signed lanes left without the unsigned cast, which clang and GCC compute the same way and UBSan doesn't check on vectors.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no vectors: the C is byte for byte the same as 3j.2's apart from the 8 typedefs in the prelude, and writing it runs 0.001% more instructions by callgrind, 8.1158 billion against 8.1157. The first version ran 0.10% more, because clang stopped inlining `value_name` once it held the vector case; the vector names moved to a function that is never inlined.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The generated C picks its SIMD instructions when it is compiled, by the target macros. C compiled for baseline x86-64 does `lookup` in a loop; `-mssse3` or any later `-march` gives `pshufb`. Choosing at run time would cost a test of the CPU or a call through a pointer per use.
         - The prelude declares the vector types in every C file, since they need no header; only packages that use `lookup` or `top_bits` bring the helpers and their headers.
         - clang and GCC compute signed vector lanes as two's complement and UBSan checks no vector operation, so the casts through unsigned vectors change no result today. They keep the code within what C defines for the scalar operations.
     - **3j.4 A driver that builds a program end to end: built 2026-11-21.** `chibi build -o program a.chibi b.chibi` checks the files as one package, with the first as the entry file, writes the C and pipes it into the C compiler, which builds and links the program. `chibi c` writes the same C to a file or to standard output, and `chibi tokens file` prints the tokens, as `chibi file` did before.
       - The driver is `src/driver.c`. `src/main.c` only calls `driver_run(stdout, stderr, argc, argv)`, which returns the exit status instead of calling `exit`, so the tests call it directly. It reads every file before it lexes any, because the lexer sizes the names table from the total. Lexer and parser errors now name their file: `print_error_at`, `lexer_print_error` and `parser_print_error` take the output and the path, and the parser test and the bench pass `chibi`, so their messages read as before.
       - `chibi build` runs `$CC`, or `cc` when `CC` is unset or empty, through `popen`, as `$CC -std=c11 -O2 -w -o program -x c - -x none` followed by the arguments after `--`. The C goes to the compiler's standard input while it is written, so no C file is left behind. The later arguments override the defaults, as `-- -O0 -g` does, and `-x none` makes the compiler read a `.o` or `.a` among them by its extension, so `-- helper.o -lm` links C code. The driver quotes each path and argument for the shell. `-std=c11` matters for GCC, which in its GNU modes contracts `a * b + c` into a fused multiply-add where the target has one, as arm64 does (rank 3 asks for no contraction; the prelude's pragma covers clang). `-w` hides warnings about the generated C, which the tests compile with `-Werror`. The driver ignores `SIGPIPE` while it writes, so a compiler that exits early, as on a flag it doesn't know, gives "the C compiler failed" with the command rather than a killed `chibi`.
       - Before the C compiler starts, the driver refuses what the C backend can't write (`c_source_unsupported`, now public) and, for `build`, a program without an `export fn main`, the entry point of step 3h.5, or one whose `main` doesn't return `i32`, C's exit status. A program's names get the prefix `p_`, so a chibi `fib` is `p_fib` in the C and in a debugger. Without `-o`, the program takes the first file's name without its directory and `.chibi`, in the current directory, as Zig's `build-exe` does; a file that doesn't end in `.chibi` needs `-o`.
       - `c_source_runtime` writes what the C needs from a program: `chibi_context`, a null pointer until rank 7 gives the context contents, and `chibi_trap`, which flushes every C stream, prints one of six messages, one per `TrapKind`, such as "trap: integer overflow", and calls `abort`, so a debugger stops at the failed check. The tests keep their own `chibi_trap`.
       - Tests: a new test builds a program of two files that calls `puts`, `putchar` and `atoi`, runs it and checks its output and exit status, word for word: Fibonacci of 91 and of 0, and of 92, which overflows, so the program prints its first line, then "trap: integer overflow", and dies by `SIGABRT`. Its output path holds a space and a quote. Another build has no `-o`, runs from a subdirectory and passes `-O0`; another links a C object given after `--`; another writes 400 functions, about 97 KB of C, more than a pipe holds, to a compiler given a flag it refuses. Seven paths give a lexer, parser and checker error, a missing file, the two entry-point errors and the C11 refusal, each checked with its path, line and column, through `build` and, where it applies, through `c`. Code outside the entry file and in it gives its two messages, and ten command lines give the usage or their own error. `chibi c` writes the same C to standard output and to a file, and `chibi tokens test/hello.chibi` matches `test/hello.txt`. The tests pass with clang and with GCC compiling the C, in 0.4 seconds. They catch all 30 planted bugs, two of them after a change to the tests: a runtime that declares `chibi_context` without defining it still links at `-O2`, where clang inlines `p_main` into `main` and drops the context, which nothing reads, so one build now passes `-O0`; and a driver that ignored `-o` would write programs into the directory the tests run in, so every failing build now names its output inside the test's directory.
       - Measured on the timing corpus: the C is byte for byte the same as 3j.3's, and writing it runs the same 8,115,758,755 instructions by callgrind.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The command line: `build`, `c` and `tokens`, with `-o` and the C compiler's arguments after `--`. Builds default to `-O2`, since the C backend is the release backend (the Compiler row of 2026-11-09); development builds go to the arm64 backend.
         - A trap prints its kind and aborts. It names no chibi line; step 3j.5 leaves the line to a debugger.
         - The C reaches the compiler through a pipe and is not kept; `chibi c` writes it when it is wanted.
     - **3j.5 `#line` directives: built 2026-11-21.** The C now carries the chibi file and line of every statement, so the C compiler's messages, `__FILE__` and `__LINE__`, a debugger's breakpoints and steps, and its backtraces name chibi lines.
       - The checker keeps source positions in a side table, as section 5.2 point 7 planned, but with one entry per statement rather than one per instruction that can trap or call. An entry, `IrLine`, holds a token of the function's own file and the end of the instructions it covers, which start where the previous entry's end. Ends count from the function's first instruction, so a function's entries copy unchanged when its code moves out of the buffer for bodies checked on demand. `IrCode` holds the entries, and `IrFunc` gains `first_line` and `nlines`. A statement that writes no IR adds no entry.
       - An entry ends after each statement, at its token; after the condition of an `if` or a `while`, at the condition; after a `switch`'s scrutinee; after each arm, at its `=>`; after a `for` loop's variable; after the back edge of a `while` or a `for`, at `while` or `in`, so a debugger steps back to the loop's line, as in C; and at the end of the body, at its last token. `else` and the end of an `if` or a block take no entry, so they go with the next statement.
       - The backend writes all the C of one chibi line on one physical line, with a space between statements. It reaches the next chibi line with up to 8 empty lines, or with `#line N` when that line is further on or earlier. Each function and each adapter starts with `#line N "path"` at the line of its name, so the functions of one C file can come from any chibi file. The path is a C string in which bytes outside printable ASCII, `"`, `\` and `?` are octal escapes, as in constant data, since `-std=c11` reads trigraphs. A token's line comes from a table of line starts for its file, filled as the backend reaches later positions; an earlier position is found by binary search. An adapter is now one line, `int32_t chibi_p_adapter_main(int32_t s0, uint8_t * s1) { return p_main(chibi_context, s0, s1); }`, so a debugger shows all of it at the function's name. The driver writes the runtime before the package, so `chibi_trap` keeps the lines of the C, `<stdin>` in a build.
       - Tests: a new test compiles a program of two files, the first named `lines/q"s\b??/`, a newline and `é.chibi`, with a `chibi_trap` macro that passes `__FILE__` and `__LINE__` to the test. 31 traps must each report their file and line: in a statement after 9 lines without code; in the conditions and bodies of `if`, `else if` and `else`, of `while` and of `for`; in `switch` arms on the `=>` line and in blocks, and in a `switch` used as a value; in a call written over four lines; in a labeled block; in a `=>` body; in an expression statement; in two functions that the backend writes before the function above them, which calls them; in two statements on one line; in statements at the start of their line, reached forward and backward; and in a bounds check in the second file. A checker test pins the entries of a function with a declaration without a value, a `while`, a `for` and an `if` with `else`: lines 2, 3, 4, 5, 4, 7, 8, 7, 10, 11, 13 and 15. Another reads the table for five functions, three of them bodies of 72 statements checked while the body that calls them waits, and checks each entry's line, that ends rise and that the functions' entries add up to the table. The driver test names a file `b "q\??=.chibi`; `chibi c` must write the runtime before the first `#line`, and `main`'s adapter on one line after `#line 21` and its file's path. The tests pass with clang and with GCC compiling the C. By hand, gdb stops at `break main.chibi:7`, steps through a loop as 7, 8, 6, 7, and shows a trap's backtrace as `chibi_trap (kind=1) at <stdin>:51` under `p_main (...) at main.chibi:17`. Of 46 planted bugs, the tests catch 44. The two left change nothing that a program or a debugger can see: one keeps old entries in the buffer that bodies checked on demand reuse, which costs only memory, and one writes `#line` where empty lines would do, which changes only the C's size.
       - Bug found while building: a labeled block with statements, or a `switch` used as a value, inside a constant crashed the checker, which has no IR outside a function body. Both now give "the checker doesn't support this yet".
       - Bug found by review: the first version counted newlines from the previous lookup in either direction, so its time grew with the square of a file's size when the backend writes the file's functions out of order, as it does with a function whose result is inferred, which is checked and written before the function that first calls it. 20,000 one-line functions, of which the first half each call one of the second half in reverse order, took 0.57 seconds in `chibi c`; with the table they take 0.05, and the C is the same.
       - Measured on the timing corpus:
         - Checking: 542,586 entries for 4.40 million nodes, 0.123 per node, so the package reserves one entry per 8 nodes. Callgrind counts 1.3% more instructions in checking, 714.0 million against 704.7, 17 for each entry; arm64 code generation is unchanged.
         - The C is 15% smaller, 89.5 MB against 104.8, in 40% fewer lines, 2.94 million against 4.87, because a chibi line's statements share one C line. It holds 98,473 `#line` directives: 20,000 that name the file, 68,583 that go back, to a loop's or an arm's line, and 9,890 that skip more than 8 lines. Writing it runs 4.7% fewer instructions, 7.735 billion against 8.116.
         - clang 18 at `-O0` compiles it 3% faster, in 42 CPU seconds against 43.4, to the same 56.4 MB of machine code. With `-g` it takes 16% longer, 109 seconds against 94 on average of two runs each, all of it in clang's IR generation, for an object 6% smaller. The same C without any directive takes 85 seconds, and with only the 20,000 directives that name the file, or with all 98,473 and no file names, 101 to 106: clang's debug locations slow down once a file has any `#line`, whatever their number or names. Builds without `-g` don't pay it.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Positions are per statement. A trap in an argument on a later line of a call written over several lines reports the line of the statement.
         - A trap still prints only its kind; a debugger finds its line in the C's line table. Passing the file and line to `chibi_trap` would add code at every check and keep identical trap calls from merging. Rust and Odin pass a location to each check, and Zig relies on debug information for its stack traces.
         - At `-O2` clang merges identical `chibi_trap` calls in a function, so a debugger may show another check's line; at `-O0` each check keeps its own.
         - A debugger can't stop between two statements on one chibi line, as with `a(); b();` in C.
         - Up to 8 empty lines stand in for a `#line`. More would make the C larger and fewer would add directives; neither changes clang's time.
         - Every build gets the directives, although `-g` builds pay about 16% in clang. A flag could leave them out when someone debugs the C itself.
       - Not built yet: columns, and positions in the arm64 backend.
     - **3j.6 Layout checks: built 2026-11-22,** the first half of the option that the owner's card on the tests' view after the switch recommends, built while the owner decides. The C tests include the compiler's headers for its structs and link the translated compiler's code. Once chibi is the source, a struct whose chibi definition drifts from its header would make the tests read the wrong bytes, and neither compiler would say so.
       - What works: `chibi c --layouts` writes, in place of the program, a C file of `_Static_assert`s: each struct's size and alignment and each field's offset as chibi lays them out, under the struct's and the field's chibi names. Compiled after the headers, it fails the build and names each struct or field that doesn't match, such as `ArenaMark.top`; a field that the header lacks is an error of its own. `chibi build` refuses the option.
       - Which structs: those that C code can reach, found by walking the types of every `extern fn`, every extern global, every `export fn` and every callback, through pointers, optional and many pointers, slices, arrays, function types and the fields of structs. A struct that only chibi code uses isn't checked, since no header needs to declare it. The translated compiler has 59 structs, and 38 are checked. Of the 21 left, 20 are local to one C file, such as the driver's `Options` and the lexer's `BlockMasks`, and no function that C calls reaches `src/simd.h`'s `u8x64`.
       - `make translated` writes the checks for the translated library, which is the tests' view, for each target, and compiles them with `-fsyntax-only` after every header in `src/`. All 38 pass with the names that the translator kept. While the headers are the source of the translation, only a bug in the translator or in the checker's layout can fail them; after the switch they guard headers that people edit by hand.
       - The checker has a new function, `check_member_offset(c, decl, member)`.
       - Tests: the backend pins the checks of a program with 12 structs: one reached only through an `extern fn`, one only through an extern global and one only through a callback; one each through a pointer, an optional pointer, a many pointer, a slice, an array, a function type's parameter and its result, and a field by value; a struct that points to itself and one that points back to the struct that reached it; and one that only a chibi function uses, which must not appear. A second test compiles those checks after a matching header, which must pass, and after one whose field `B.y` is a `uint16_t` rather than a `uint32_t`, which must fail and name `B.y`. The driver test writes the checks of a program with one struct to standard output, and `chibi build --layouts` gives "chibi: unknown option --layouts".
       - Planted bugs: 31, all caught. 25 in the walk and the output: each root, every function as a root, each kind that leads to an inner type, the parameters, the result, the fields, the mark that ends a cycle (caught by ASan when the walk's stack overflows), the mark of a reached struct, the first declaration, every struct in place of reached ones, the `#include`, the size, the alignment, a field's offset taken from the wrong table, the last field and the message; 2 in `check_member_offset`; and 4 in the driver. A plant in the translator that adds a field to the start of `ArenaMark` stops `make translated` at the checks, with four errors that name `ArenaMark`, `pad`, `ArenaMark.top` and `ArenaMark.cur`. Without the checks, the same plant fails only when the tests run: an assert in the translated `arena_restore`, called from a parser test, prints "trap: assertion failed" and names no struct.
       - Measured on the timing corpus: the C build runs 32 more instructions of 1,889 million, and the release-fast translated build 74 fewer; neither runs the new code. The translation gains 18 nil checks, all in `c_source_layouts`. The checks add about 0.15 seconds to each target in `make translated`: 0.09 to write them and 0.05 for clang to read them.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The checks use the chibi names, so a header must name each struct and field as the chibi source does. The C backend's own C names fields by position, `f0` and `f1`, so the checks can't lean on it.
         - Sizes, alignments and offsets, not the types of fields, since a header's `FILE *` and `const char *` are `?*u8` and `[*]u8` in chibi. Function prototypes go unchecked for the same reason.
         - Enums and constants go unchecked: nothing in a function's type leads to them, and chibi can't tell which of its constants a header declares.
         - The roots are what C can call or read, not what a header declares, since chibi doesn't read headers.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `check_member_offset` is new in `src/check.h` and `c_source_layouts` in `src/c_source.h`.
       - Not built yet: the option's other half, which the switch commit does: the 6 inline helpers in the headers that the tests call become calls into chibi.
     - **3j.7 Names for debuggers: built 2026-11-25,** the first part of debug info, which the owner chose on 2026-11-25 to build before the switch, with the x86-64 development backend ([../decisions.md](../decisions.md), Compiler). The C already carried `#line` directives, so with `chibi build -o p a.chibi -- -g -O0` gdb and lldb stop at a chibi file and line and step by chibi lines, but each local was a slot `s<k>`, so `print total` found nothing.
       - What works: the slot of a named local or parameter takes its chibi name in the C, so `print total` and `info locals` show chibi names. A name that a second local of the same function uses, such as the `i` of a second loop, becomes `i_2_`. A name that C or the C's headers could read as something else becomes `name_1_`: C keywords, `ctx`, names shaped like the backend's own `s3`, `v1` and `a1`, names that end in `_` or `_t`, names with no lowercase letter, which a header's macros may take, `linux`, `unix` and `i386`, and names that start with `chibi_`, `CHIBI_` or the package's prefix. Hidden slots and the second half of a pair keep `s<k>`. The backend's own temporaries inside blocks now end in `_` (`r_`, `lo_`, `hi_`, `x_`, `y_`, `product_`, `negative_`, `over_` and `d_`), so no chibi name can meet them.
       - Tests: a program in the C backend's grid, with 2 more functions of two `int`s, names locals `ctx`, `s0`, `v1`, `a1`, `case`, `linux`, `size_t`, `INT64_MAX`, `x_` and `chibi_k`, takes an array result into a local `r`, a 128-bit product into `x`, `y` and `p`, and a slice's bounds into `lo` and `hi`, and declares `i` in three loops. Planted: treating every name as plain fails it, and fails the random programs, whose locals are named such as `v54` and `s4`.
       - Measured on the timing corpus: `chibi c` writes 89.9 MB of C, 2.3% more, in the same time, 0.50 to 0.51 s (pinned to one core, 5 runs each).
       - Not built yet: function names stay `p_sum`, because a C function named after a chibi function could meet a C library builtin such as `abs`; `chibi build -g`, since `-- -g` passes the flag already; and debug info for the JIT backends, through the GDB JIT interface.
   - **3k. What the translated compiler needs from the language,** before the translator of item 5: `bitcast` (3k.1), `size_of` and `align_of` (3k.2), optional pointers with `nil` (step 3e.1, brought forward), string literals (3k.3), conditional expressions (3k.4), string literals inside constant tables and globals (3k.5), the high half of a 64-bit multiply (3k.6), structs and arrays as the values of a conditional (3k.7), `?extern fn` with `bitcast` of C function pointers (3k.8), typed variadics, `xs: ..T` (3k.9), the inlining marks `@inline` and `@noinline` (3k.10), release-fast builds (3k.11), `assert` (3k.12), the target constants that one source for every target needs (3k.13), `--target` for `chibi c` and `chibi build` (3k.14), the built-in packages that hold the builtins (3k.15), asserts that release-fast builds assume (3k.16), and the 128-bit integers `u128` and `i128` (3k.17), in the arm64 backend too (3k.18), in place of `mul_high` (3k.19), character literals (3k.20), arrays as parameters and results (3k.21), arrays in place of the vector types (3k.22), `string` and `cstring` (3k.23), tuples (3k.24), named results (3k.25), overflow checks with two results (3k.26), top-level code with `os.args` (3k.27), 64-byte vectors for the lexer's blocks (3k.28), `@cold` (3k.29), and the lexer's masks on NEON (3k.30, reverted). The translator's output needs them whatever language the translator is written in: [c-corpus.md](c-corpus.md) section 6.4 counts 476 pointer conversions and 25 between pointers and integers, 176 uses of `sizeof` and `alignof`, and 68 lines with `NULL` in `src/`.
     - **3k.1 `bitcast(T, x)`: built 2026-11-21,** as the Casts row of 2026-11-10 decided. It keeps the bits of a value and gives them another type of the same size: `bitcast(u32, x)` of an `f32`, `bitcast(*u64, p)` of a `[*]u8`, `bitcast(u64, p)` of a pointer, and `bitcast(u32x4, v)` of a `u8x16`.
       - Integers, `uintptr`, floats, `*T`, `[*]T` and vectors change into each other when their sizes match; otherwise "`u32` and `u64` differ in size, so `bitcast` can't change one into the other". A slice gives "`bitcast` can't change a slice, because its length would need rescaling", as decided. `bool` and enums give "`bitcast` changes numbers, pointers and vectors, not `bool`", because most of their bit patterns are no value; `u8(Color.red)` and `Color(n)` convert enums, as decided. Arrays, structs and `?*T` give "the checker doesn't support this yet": arrays and structs live in memory, and `?*T` came with step 3e.1.
       - The value needs a type of its own: `bitcast(u32, 3)`, `bitcast(u32, 1.5)` and `bitcast(u32, .red)` give "`bitcast` needs a typed value, as in `u64(1)`", because an untyped constant's bits depend on the type it would take.
       - A `*T` or `[*]T` result checks its address when `T` needs more alignment than the source promises: the source's pointee alignment for a pointer, and 1 for an integer or a float. The Casts row asks for the check in debug and release builds, and chibi had no release-fast mode yet, so every build had it until step 3k.11. The IR adds `check-align` after the `bitcast`; it traps with the new trap kind "misaligned pointer" and passes the pointer on, since each IR value is used once. `bitcast(*u64, p)` of a `[*]u8` checks for 8, and `bitcast(*u8, q)` of a `*u64` checks nothing. A constant address is checked while compiling: `bitcast(*u32, u64(2))` gives "address 2 isn't aligned for `*u32`", and `bitcast(*u32, u64(0))` and `bitcast([*]u32, u64(0))` give "`*u32` is never nil, so this needs a value", as a `*T` or `[*]T` declared without a value does.
       - A constant integer or float folds while compiling, so it can be a package constant: `bitcast(i8, u8(128))` is -128, `bitcast(f32, u32(1069547520))` is 1.5, and `bitcast(u32, f32(0.1))` is 1036831949. NaNs, infinities and -0 are no constants of the checker (step 2), so in a body they become a `bitcast` at run time, and in a package constant they give "the checker doesn't support this yet". A constant address also becomes a `bitcast` at run time, since no pointer is a constant.
       - `bitcast(T, x)` of a `T` gives `x` back. Its result widens as `T(x)`'s does, as every `bitcast` result does, so `y: i64 = bitcast(i32, a + b)` is accepted where `y: i64 = a + b` isn't.
       - On arm64 a `bitcast` between an integer and a float is one `fmov`. Between 64-bit integers and pointers it writes nothing: the result takes over the operand's register, as an address at offset 0 already does, unless the next instruction consumes the value. Smaller integers go through the existing `truncate`, a move or an extension, and a vector `bitcast` moves its 16 bytes. `check-align` is a `tst` with the alignment less one and a `b.ne` to the function's trap, and its result takes over the register in the same way, so `bitcast(*u64, p).*` of a `[*]u8` in `x1` is `tst x1, #0x7`, `b.ne` and `ldr x0, [x1]`. The C backend writes `__builtin_memcpy(&v2, &(float){ v1 }, sizeof v2)` for numbers and pointers, which clang and GCC compile to a move, a cast for vectors, which keeps the bits in both, and `if ((uintptr_t)v2 & 7u) chibi_trap(...)` for the check; `chibi_trap` prints "trap: misaligned pointer". The reference interpreter keeps the bits, masked to the result's width and sign-extended for small signed types.
       - Tests: the checker folds 12 constants, A to L, to values computed in Python, among them -1 from `bitcast(f32, i32(-1082130432))`. 26 diagnostics, each with its position, cover each refusal above, a type where a value goes and the reverse, a function name, an unused result, and five constants the checker can't hold, among them -0 from `bitcast(f32, i32(-2147483648))`, whose sign-extended bits would fold to +0 without the mask to 32 bits. Two IR dumps pin where `check-align` goes and where it doesn't, and that a `bitcast` result widens. A new program of 10 functions runs 33 cases computed by a Python oracle, 6 of them traps, natively on arm64 under qemu: floats both ways in both widths, integers of every width, pointers through integers and back, a misaligned `*u64` and `[*]u32`, a pointer made from a float's bits, and NaN, infinity, -0 and a constant address made at run time. The same functions join the grid, now 239 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter, with clang and with GCC compiling the C, and a vector function joins the vector programs with 3 results. The random programs now write `bitcast` between integers and floats of each width, keeping the floats finite, and through pointers in reads and writes. 6,000 random programs pass natively on arm64 and 4,000 through the C backend. The tests catch 31 of 36 planted bugs. Of the five left, three change nothing a program can see: an `fmov` of 64 bits for 32-bit values, whose upper half no instruction reads in either register file; a float operand fetched through V16 instead of V30, which happens only for a spilled or constant operand, while a `bitcast`'s operand is never a constant at run time and always comes from the instruction before it; and the reference interpreter keeping the upper bits of an `f32` result, whose readers use only its low 32 bits. The other two need register pressure that no test creates: taking over the register of a spilled operand, which the check that `offset_address` already had prevents, and not releasing the operand's register after `check-align`.
       - Bugs found while building: the first version checked the alignment before the `bitcast`, on the source value, so on arm64 a pointer made from an `f64`'s bits was tested in a float register as if it were an integer register, and C converted the float by value. The check now comes after, on the pointer, and a test covers it. The planted bugs found four gaps in the tests, now covered: no constant folded a negative `i32` into an `f32`, nothing checked that a same-type `bitcast` widens, no `[*]T` result was checked at run time, and no `bitcast` at run time had a constant operand.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no `bitcast`: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same. Callgrind counts 0.06% fewer instructions in checking, 713.5 million against 714.0, and 0.04% fewer in arm64 code generation, 462.5 million against 462.6. The first version ran 0.4% more in code generation, because clang inlined the two new emitters into the backend's main loop; they are now never inlined, like the rare `count_bits` and `test_overflow`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `bitcast` refuses `bool` and enums, since most of their bit patterns are no value; `T(x)` already converts enums both ways and checks `Color(n)`.
         - A `bitcast` of an untyped constant is an error, fixed by `bitcast(u32, u32(3))` or just `u32(3)`.
         - `[*]T` from a constant 0 is an error, as `*T` is, since neither may be nil.
         - A float converts to a pointer and back, since the Casts row allows any value of the same size. The alignment check then treats the float's bits as an address.
         - Arrays and structs wait. The Casts row allows any value of the same size, but they would copy through memory, and nothing in the compiler's C needs them.
       - Not built yet: arrays and structs in `bitcast`. A release-fast build without the alignment check came with step 3k.11, and `?*T` with step 3e.1. The LowIR spike in [experiments/lowir-spike](../../experiments/lowir-spike/README.md) doesn't know `bitcast` or `check-align` yet: its translation asserts on an IR kind it doesn't know, so its test now fails on the grid's new functions, and the second hand-off to its thread will list the new kinds.
     - **3k.2 `size_of(T)` and `align_of(T)`: built 2026-11-21,** as sheet 3 recommends ([syntax-options-data.md](syntax-options-data.md) 3): plain predeclared names that take a type, so a local or a package declaration named `size_of` takes their place, as with the bit counts. `size_of(Point)` gives the size in bytes and `align_of(Point)` the alignment, both from the layout the backends already use, which the C backend checks against C's with a `_Static_assert` for each struct it writes.
       - The result is an untyped integer constant, like an integer literal, so it fits wherever its value fits: `n * size_of(T)` with a `u64` count, an array length such as `buf: [size_of(Header)]u8`, a package constant, and `const V: u32 = size_of(f32)`. An `int` result would need `u64(size_of(T))` beside nearly every use in the translated compiler: of the 175 uses of `sizeof` in `src/`, 148 sit on a line that calls an allocator, `memcpy`, `memset` or `qsort`, which take a `size_t`, and 63 multiply a count.
       - Every type with a layout has a size: numbers, `bool`, `uintptr`, enums (the size of their backing type), `*T`, `[*]T`, slices (16 bytes, aligned to 8), arrays, vectors (16 bytes, aligned to 16) and structs. An empty struct takes 0 bytes and aligns to 1. A struct is laid out when `size_of` needs it, so `size_of(Later)` works before `struct Later` is declared, and `struct N { next: *N, pad: [size_of(*N)]u8 }` works because a pointer's size doesn't depend on its pointee. `struct S { a: [size_of(S)]u8 }` gives "`S` depends on itself" and nothing more, also through an array, as in `size_of([2]S)`.
       - Refusals: a value gives "`size_of` takes a type, as in `size_of(u32)`". `void`, unions, error unions, optionals other than `?*T` and structs that hold them give "the checker doesn't support this yet", since none has a layout yet. A size above the largest `int` gives "the size of `[4611686018427387904][2]u8` doesn't fit in `int`"; this also catches the sizes that the layout code saturates at 2^64 − 1.
       - Types as operands: `size_of([4]u32)` and `size_of(*u8)` didn't parse before, because `parse_operand` took a type that starts with `*` or `[` only before a literal's `.{` or `.[`. Such a type is now an operand, as sheet 3 and question 3 of the generics sheet propose, so a type argument such as `List(*Node)` will parse too. A type used as a value gives "expected a value", as `u32` already did, and a constant can now name a composite type, `const Bytes := [3]u16`, as `const A := Point` already could. Both of the parser's hints stay. `x := [4]int{1, 2}` still gives "expected `.{` or `.[` after a type", now only when `{` follows an array type directly. A C-style `*p` gives "use `.*` to dereference" from the checker: the parser writes a `*` that begins an operand as its own node kind, printed `ptr` like any pointer type, and the checker gives the hint for that kind when the pointee is a value. Before a literal's `.{` or `.[` the `*` stays a pointer type, so `*Point.{ .x = 1 }` gives "`*Point` is not a struct, so a struct literal can't build it" and no hint. `x: *MAX` with a constant `MAX` still gives "expected a type", and `*Missing` gives only "unknown name `Missing`". A type alone as a statement now gives "expected a value" instead of "this value is not used". `(*u8)(p)` now parses as the conversion `T(x)`, which refuses a pointer of another type ("`*i32` and `*u8` don't mix without a conversion"); `bitcast` changes pointer types.
       - The result is a constant, so the IR, both backends, the reference interpreter and the LowIR spike didn't change.
       - Tests: the checker folds 20 constants built from 37 queries, among them a padded struct (12 bytes, aligned to 4), a struct declared after its use (48 bytes, aligned to 8), a struct whose array length is the size of a pointer to itself, an enum with an `i32` backing type, an empty struct, a `[2][3]u32`, a constant naming a bare array type, a `u32` constant and the largest size that fits, 2^63 − 1 bytes. A global is sized with `size_of`. An IR dump shows the constants folded into a body, beside a `check-align` from `bitcast` that uses the struct's alignment. 22 diagnostics, each with its position, cover every refusal above, two cycles, a pointer to a constant and an array of such pointers in a struct's member types, the hint in four operand positions, an unknown name after `*`, and a type as a value and as a statement. Parser tests cover the new operands, `*p = 5`, `if a == *p {}`, the hint for `{` and the depth limit on chains of type operands. The tests catch all 29 planted bugs, among them a `size_of` that skips laying out the struct, the cycle reported twice, the size limit off by one or only at saturation, a typed `int` result, the hint given in a type position or after an unknown name, and a literal's type written as an operand. One passed at first: a `*` operand that skipped the depth check, so an operand at the limit went one level deeper. A depth test now puts a `*` operand at the limit.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no `size_of`: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same. Callgrind counts 0.03% fewer instructions in checking, 713.35 million against 713.55, the same in arm64 code generation, and 3.4% fewer in the parser, 237.9 million against 246.3. All of the parser's saving is in register saves on entry to `parse_operand`, whose type paths now keep fewer values alive across calls.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `size_of` and `align_of` take only a type, so a value will need `size_of(type_of(x))` once `type_of` exists. Whether `size_of` takes a value too is sheet 3's question 2.
         - The result is an untyped integer constant, not an `int`.
         - A size must fit in `int`, so offsets and lengths computed from it never overflow a signed 64-bit integer.
         - A `*` that begins an operand starts a pointer type, and the checker gives the dereference hint only there.
         - An array type in operand position can't be followed directly by `{`, so the parser keeps its hint for `[4]int{1, 2}`.
       - Not built yet: `offset_of`, which `src/` doesn't use, and `type_of`. `size_of(?*T)` came with step 3e.1.
     - **3e.1 Optional pointers and `nil`: built 2026-11-21,** brought forward from 3e for the translator's 68 lines with `NULL`, with the rules of the Optionals rows of 2026-11-10 and 2026-11-15. `?*T` and `?[*]T` are pointers that may be nil, with nil at address 0, so they have a pointer's size, alignment and registers. `nil` takes its type from where it goes. `p!!` gives the pointer or traps, in every build. `a ?? b` gives `a`'s pointer, or `b` when `a` is nil, and evaluates `b` only then. `x ??= y` assigns `y` when `x` is nil. Narrowing after `p != nil` came in step 3e.2.
       - A `*T` converts to `?*T` where one is expected, and `?*T(q)` converts explicitly; neither emits IR. A `?*T` can't be used as a pointer: a field, `p.*`, an index, `p + n`, a slice, a conversion to `*T` and an assignment to a `*T` give "`?*Node` may be nil, so it needs `!!` or `??` first". `nil` where a value can't be nil gives "`*Node` is never nil", also in a comparison such as `q == nil` with a `*Node` and in `return nil` from a function that returns one. `a := nil`, `nil == nil` and `nil!!` give "`nil` needs a type here, as in `p: ?*T = nil`". `q!!`, `n ?? 1` and `i ??= 2` on values that are never nil give "`*Node` is never nil, so `!!` does nothing" and its `??` and `??=` forms, and `?*Node(nil)!!` gives "this is always `nil`, so `!!` always traps". A `?*T` compares with `==` and `!=` against `nil`, a `*T` and a `?*T`, and nothing else: `p < p` gives "`?*Node` doesn't support `<`". The result of `p!!` is a value, so `p!! = q` gives "only a variable, a field, an element or `p.*` can be assigned to", while `p!!.* = 5` writes through it.
       - Optional pointers work wherever a pointer does: locals, parameters, results, struct fields, array and slice elements, and globals. Their zero value is nil, so `p: ?*Node` starts nil and a struct of them zero-fills to nil. `size_of(?*T)` and `align_of(?*T)` are 8, and the declarations dump prints `nil`, as in `const P: ?*u8 = nil`. Other optionals, such as `?int` and `?[]u8`, give "the checker doesn't support this yet" as a type and as an operand such as `?int.[1, 2]`; a type that starts with `?` is now an operand, as `*T` became one in 3k.2, so `?*Node(nil)` and `size_of(?*Node)` parse.
       - IR: `p!!` is the new `check-nil`, which traps with the new trap kind "unexpected nil" and passes the pointer on with the type `*T`. `a ?? b` stores `a` in a hidden slot and tests it, as `and` does: an `if` with the result type, the slot loaded as `*T` in the first arm, `b` in the `else`, and the value at `end`. With a constant nil on the left it gives `b`. Copies that a literal or an argument still owes are reserved before the `if`, so they happen whether or not `b` runs (3d.8). `x ??= y` loads the target, compares it with nil, and stores in an `if` without an `else`; a target that isn't a local keeps its address in a hidden slot. Its targets follow assignment's rules: locals, fields, elements, `p.*` and globals, and not parameters, `_` or values. The parser gives a `??=` target its own marker node, so the checker knows what to emit before the right side, and plain assignments pay nothing for it.
       - `bitcast` takes optional pointers. A `*T` or `[*]T` result from anything but a pointer, such as an integer, a float or a `?*T`, gets `check-nil` before `check-align`, so `bitcast(*u32, n)` of a zero `n` traps with "unexpected nil", where 3k.1 let it through. A constant 0 gives `nil` for a `?*T`, as in `bitcast(?*u64, u64(0))`.
       - On arm64 `check-nil` is a `cbz` to the function's trap, and its result takes over the operand's register, as `check-align`'s does. The C backend writes optional pointers as C pointers and `check-nil` as `if (!v1)` and a call to `chibi_trap`, which prints "trap: unexpected nil". The reference interpreter traps on 0.
       - `TYPE_ID_NIL`, the type of a `nil` that has no type yet, now follows `TYPE_ID_ENUM_LITERAL`, so one range check finds both literals that take their type from context. The vector type ids moved up by one.
       - Tests: parser trees for `?` types as operands and an error for `x := ?`. The checker folds the sizes of `?*N` and `?[*]u8` and of a struct with both, and dumps globals and constants that hold `nil`. 29 diagnostics in one function and 4 in another, each with its position, cover every refusal above and show that a `??=` target gives its type to the right side, as `=` does. Three IR dumps pin `check-nil`, `??` with each result type, `??=` on a local, a field and a slice element, a constant nil on the left of `??`, and `nil` beside `and`; a fourth pins where `bitcast` puts `check-nil`. A new program of 15 functions runs 45 cases, 5 of them traps, natively on arm64 under qemu: a linked list built in an array and walked with `!!`, a `!!` that traps, `??` with a pointer and with an optional on the right, chained `??`, `??=` on a local, array elements, `p.*` and a global, comparisons, `bitcast` through integers with a nil that traps, optional fields in a struct passed and returned by value, a pointer whose upper 32 bits are set, and a `??` whose right side changes a struct that a literal must copy first. The 12 functions that take two integers join the grid, now 251 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The random-program generator doesn't write optionals yet. The tests catch 64 of 66 planted bugs. Thirteen passed at first. Nine are now covered by new tests, among them a `??` that doesn't reserve a literal's copies before its branch, which the last backend function catches, and a `??=` whose right side loses the target's type, which changes the errors for `.{}` and `.A`. Two led to simpler code, since `REF_RESULT` means nothing for a pointer and every optional's zero value is all zero bytes. Two change nothing a program can see: the register weight of `??`'s hidden slot, and the `end` that `??` emits after an error, in a function that is never compiled.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no optionals: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as 3k.2's. Callgrind counts 0.02% more instructions in checking, 713.49 million against 713.35, 0.13% more in arm64 code generation, 463.06 million against 462.46, and 0.29% more in the parser, 241.2 million against 240.5.
         - Code generation: `check-nil` made `IR_CALL`'s number even, and clang then tests `kind == IR_CALL || kind == IR_CALL_C` in `result()` with a `mov`, an `and` and a `cmp` instead of an `lea` and a `cmp`: one more instruction for each value. The next IR kind that gives a value changes it back. An early exit for kinds outside `IR_CALL` to `IR_RET` recovered a quarter of it and was left out.
         - Parser: choosing the `??=` marker takes a compare and a conditional move on each assignment, and the `?` operand one compare.
         - The first version cost 0.84% in checking: the checker read each assignment's token to find `??=` (0.4%), and checked for `nil` in `unify`'s path for equal types (0.2%). The marker node removes the first, and an operator on two `nil`s is now reported in `lanewise`, which runs only for operators the type lacks. Numbering the marker next to `&`'s node, so that one range check could cover both, cost 2.9%: the new numbers moved clang's static estimate of how often `walk` calls `call_fn` and `resolve_name` under the 2% at which it treats a call as cold, and it stopped inlining them. The node kinds now keep their numbers, the marker sits beside `??`'s inside the range that `body_marker`'s switch already covers, and a table says which nodes take a place.
       - Fixed: the two crashes on `?int` found in 3g.1. `const t := ?int` is now a type constant, and `x := ?int.[1, 2]` gives "the checker doesn't support this yet".
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `a ?? b` has `b`'s type: `*T` when `b` is a `*T`, and `?*T` when `b` may be nil too.
         - `!!`, `??` and `??=` on a value that is never nil, and a comparison of a `*T` with `nil`, are errors, not warnings.
         - `bitcast` to `*T` or `[*]T` checks for nil, which changes 3k.1.
         - `x ??= y` accepts a `?*T` for `y`.
         - `nil` alone needs a type: `a := nil`, `nil == nil`, and a struct member `p := nil`, whose error points at the member's name as the other member errors do.
         - Optionals that aren't pointers are refused by the checker. The C backend used to compile a `?int` parameter as `uint64_t`.
         - Narrowing comes in its own step, 3e.2, built below.
       - Not built yet: `?T` for other types. `?extern fn` came in step 3k.8, and narrowing in step 3e.2. The LowIR spike in [experiments/lowir-spike](../../experiments/lowir-spike/README.md) doesn't know `check-nil` or optional types yet, and the second hand-off to its thread will list them.
     - **3e.2 Narrowing: built 2026-11-21,** with the rule of the Optionals row of 2026-11-10: after a check such as `p != nil`, `p` is narrowed to its value, as in Kotlin, so code doesn't need `p!!` there. A narrowed `?*T` works as a `*T`, and a narrowed `?[*]T` as a `[*]T`: a field, `p.*`, an index, a slice, `p + n` and `p += n`, and a conversion to `*T` or `[*]T` in an argument, a declaration, an assignment or a `return`. `p!!` on a narrowed `p` emits no `check-nil`. Narrowing never changes a variable's type, so `p == nil`, `p ?? q` and `p ??= q` still work on a narrowed `p`, and `q := p` gives a `?*T` that is narrowed too.
       - Where facts come from: `p != nil`, `p == nil`, `nil != p` and `nil == p`, where `p` is a local. `!` swaps the facts that hold when the condition is true and when it is false. In `a and b`, `b` sees what holds when `a` is true, and the whole holds a fact when true if either side does; `a or b` is the mirror image, so after `if p == nil or q == nil { return 0 }` both are narrowed. An `if` body sees what holds when its condition is true, and an `else` or `else if` what holds when every earlier condition is false. A `while` body sees what holds when its condition is true, so `while p != nil and p.v < 50 { … p = p.next }` needs no `!!`. Assigning or declaring a value that is never nil narrows the variable, as does `p ??= v` with such a `v`, and assigning a value that may be nil ends the narrowing.
       - After an `if` chain: when every branch before the first one that falls through ends in `return`, `break` or `continue`, what holds when their conditions are false holds after the chain. So `if p == nil { return 0 }` narrows `p` for the rest of the block, and so does `if p == nil { return 0 } else { … }`. Without an `else`, the path that takes no branch falls through, so `if p == nil { return 0 } else if q == nil { return 1 }` narrows both.
       - A narrowing ends at the end of the place its fact holds: the branch of an `if`, the body of a loop, an arm of a `switch`, a labeled block that a `break` leaves, the right side of `and` and `or`, and the function. At the top of a `while` or `for`, every variable that the loop assigns loses its narrowing, because the next pass may start with a nil.
       - Which variables narrow: parameters and `for` variables, which can't be assigned, and locals whose address the function never takes with `&name`. Fields and globals don't, because a call or a store through a pointer can change them, so `if n.next != nil { n.next.v }` still needs `!!`. A nested function, checked in the middle of another because its result type is inferred, starts with no facts.
       - The message for a `?*T` used as a pointer is now "`?*Node` may be nil, so it needs `!!`, `??` or a check against `nil` first".
       - How it works: the checker keeps a stack of facts, each the slot number of a narrowed local, and a mark of the stack's height where each place above begins, so leaving a place pops its facts. A byte per slot, in an array that each function allocates on its first narrowing, says whether the slot is narrowed now or can never be; the scan for `&name` runs at most once per function, the first time a local that isn't a parameter may narrow. A condition leaves its tests on a second stack, one word per test of a local against `nil` with the outcome under which the local isn't nil, and `!`, `and` and `or` combine them. The checker finds a test in the IR it has just emitted, an `eq` or `ne` of an optional with a constant nil and a `load` of a slot, so the comparison is checked as before. Reading a narrowed local gives a `?*T` operand with a mark that it isn't nil, whose `load` has the type `*T`; the operations that need a pointer accept that mark. In a function that reads no optional, `if`, `while`, `and` and `or` do none of this.
       - IR: no new kind. A narrowed read is a `load` of the `?*T` slot with the type `*T`, as `??` already loaded its hidden slot in 3e.1, and the backends and the reference interpreter didn't change.
       - Tests: 29 diagnostics in 13 functions, each with its position, show where narrowing holds and where it doesn't: a then-branch that falls through, an `else` that exits after a then-branch that falls through, `and` and `or` in each direction and with the wrong outcome, an `and` outside a condition whose facts end with it, `p != q`, `!` around `and`, `o := q` of an unchecked `q`, a loop that assigns a narrowed variable, a `while` left by `break`, facts after a `for` and a `while`, a reassignment, a local whose address is taken, a field, a `switch` arm, a labeled block, two `if` chains with an exit in the middle, a local in slot 32 or above, and a nested function checked inside a narrowed branch. An IR dump shows no `check-nil` in a function that walks a list with `while nil != p and p.v > 0` and `p!!.v`, returns early when `q` is nil and then converts it to `*Node`, reads a field of a `?*Node` declared from a `*Node`, and advances and indexes a `?[*]u8` inside `if o != nil`. A program gains 9 functions, 7 of which run 32 hand-computed cases natively on arm64 under qemu: a list walked with `while p != nil and p.v < 50`, an `else if` chain and `!(p != nil)`, a narrowed `?[*]int` advanced and indexed, a `?*int` declared with an address and passed as a `*int`, `??=` with an address, narrowing in a `for` with `continue`, a `switch` arm and a labeled block, `or` chains, `b > 2 and p != nil`, a slice of a narrowed `?[*]int`, `!` around `and` in a labeled block that no `break` leaves, and a narrowed branch that calls a function checked in the middle of it. The 7 join the grid, now 258 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The tests catch 57 of 62 planted bugs. Eleven of 64 passed at first. Four are now covered by new tests: a test of two locals read as a test against `nil`, the facts of an `else` that falls through after a `then` that exits, a nested function that keeps its caller's facts, and the per-function array grown too little for slot 32. Two led to simpler code, because the checks they changed, for a hidden local and for an address already taken, repeated what other checks ensure. Five change nothing a program can see: comparisons of other types with zero recorded as tests that nothing reads, a test stack that isn't popped, parameters scanned for `&` although they can't be address-taken, a fact pushed twice, and a not-nil check that also accepts constants, none of which carries the mark.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no optionals: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as 3e.1's. The first version cost 1.37% in checking, 723.25 million instructions against 713.49: clang kept `walk`'s array of node tokens on the stack and reloaded it for every node, where 3e.1 kept it in a register. Bisecting the change found two edits that each moved it there, the new path for assigning to an optional and the narrowed path in `deref`. A smaller form of the first avoided it, but no form of the second did, in line or out of line, and neither did marking `assign` `NOINLINE`. `walk` computed each node's child token at the top of its loop, a compare, a branch and a load for every node, although only 14 cases read it. Computing it in those cases instead, a commit of its own, saves 1.80% in checking, 700.67 million against 713.49. With it, `walk` keeps the array in a register with narrowing too, and narrowing costs 0.43% on top, 703.67 million: 1.4 million in `walk`, 0.5 million each in `check_body`, for the larger `Body` and the `free` of the narrowing array, and in `declare_local`, and 0.3 million in `logical_left`. Code generation is the same, 463.06 million.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Fields and globals never narrow, as Kotlin doesn't smart-cast a mutable property; code copies them to a local first.
         - A local whose address is taken anywhere in the function never narrows, even before the `&`.
         - At the top of a loop, every variable the loop assigns loses its narrowing, even if each assignment is never nil.
         - Narrowing doesn't change a variable's type: `q := p` gives a `?*T`, and `p == nil`, `p!!` and `p ?? q` on a narrowed `p` are not errors.
       - Not built yet: facts after a loop, such as `p` after `while p == nil { … }`; facts after an `if` whose then-branch falls through and whose `else` exits, as in `if p != nil { … } else { return 0 }`; facts that both branches establish, as in `if p == nil { p = &x }`. Step 3c hides the facts that hold where a `defer` is written from its statement. Narrowing covers the optionals the checker supports, `?*T` and `?[*]T`.
     - **3k.3 String literals: built 2026-11-21,** for the translator, because every file in `src/` but `arena.c` has them, in error messages, format strings and tables of names. This step builds the part of the string design that the owner was asked about on 2026-11-21 ([adopt-or-settle.md](adopt-or-settle.md) 2.10) that translated C needs: a literal is an untyped string constant, stored with a trailing NUL in read-only data, so it converts to a C string at no cost. `string` and `cstring` aren't built yet, so a literal converts to `*u8`, `[*]u8`, `?*u8` and `?[*]u8`, which is how the translator writes C's `char *`.
       - Escapes are the ones the lexer already accepts: `\" \\ \/ \b \f \n \r \t` and `\uXXXX`, which the checker writes as UTF-8. Two `\u` escapes that form a surrogate pair, as in `\uD83D\uDE00`, give one code point, and half of a pair gives "`\uD800` to `\uDFFF` must come in pairs, as in `\uD83D\uDE00`". Other bytes are kept as written.
       - Where a literal goes, inside a function: a declaration with a type, an assignment, an argument, a `return`, a field of a struct literal, an element of an array literal, the right side of `??` and `??=`, and a conversion such as `[*]u8("text")`. In C's `..` a literal passes as a `[*]u8`. `const S := "text"` names a literal, and each use converts it where it goes.
       - Refused: `x := "text"` and `x := S` give "a string needs a type here, as in `s: [*]u8 = "text"`". A pointer to anything but `u8`, or a type that isn't a pointer, gives "`untyped string` and `int` don't mix without a conversion", and so does `p == "text"`, even for an optional `p`, because it would compare addresses. Operators, indexing, `!!`, `??` with the literal on the left, conditions and `bitcast` give their usual errors, naming `untyped string`.
       - Not yet at the top level: a literal in a constant with a pointer type, a global, a field's default or a parameter's default gives "the checker doesn't support this yet". A pointer inside constant data or the globals image needs an address that the backends fill in when they place the data, which none of them does yet, so tables of strings wait for that; step 3k.5 built it.
       - IR: no new kind.
