The checker writes each literal into constant data when it checks it, and a conversion emits `data` with the pointer type, so the backends and the reference interpreter didn't change. A table beside the IR keeps each literal's offset and length. The IR dump prints the string, as in `%0 = data [*]u8 "hello"`, and the declarations dump `const S = "text"`.
       - `TYPE_ID_UNTYPED_STRING` follows `TYPE_ID_UNTYPED_FLOAT`, so the fixed type ids after it, `nil`'s and the vectors' among them, moved up by one, and `TYPE_UNTYPED_STRING` follows `TYPE_UNTYPED_FLOAT` among the kinds.

       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A literal converts to `*u8` and `[*]u8` as well as to the future `cstring`, because the translator writes C's `char *` as those.
         - Comparing a pointer with a literal is an error, also for an optional pointer.

     - **3k.4 Conditional expressions: built 2026-11-21,** for the translator, because the compiler's C uses `?:` 19 times ([syntax-options-effects.md](syntax-options-effects.md) 4b). This step builds option F of sheet 4b, which the owner was asked about on 2026-11-21 together with strings and character literals: `if c then a else b`, with the new keyword `then`.
       - Where it goes: wherever an expression of the lowest precedence starts, which is after `:=`, `=`, `return` and a function's `=>`, as an argument, an element of a literal or an index, inside parentheses, as the value of a value-`switch` arm or a `break`, and as either value of another conditional, so `if a then 1 else if b then 2 else 3` chains. At the start of a statement `if` is still the `if` statement, so an arm of a statement `switch` writes `1 => y = if c then a else b`. Inside another expression it needs parentheses: `1 + if c then 2 else 3` and `-if c then 2 else 3` give "put parentheses around an `if` expression inside another expression". The `else` value reaches as far right as it can, as in Carbon, so `if c then -x else x + 1` adds 1 only to the `else` value. `then` and `else` may start a line. A missing `then` gives "expected `then` after the condition", and a missing `else` gives "expected `else`; an `if` expression needs both values".
       - The condition is a `bool`, and only the chosen value runs. The type comes from where the value goes, as for a value `switch`: a declaration with a type, an assignment, a `return`, an argument, an element of a literal, a `break` value, a value-`switch` arm, or the conditional around it. So `n: u8 = if c then 1 else 2` and `k: Kind = if c then .a else .b` work, and so does `g: u32 = if c then 16 else if d then w else 8` with a `u16` `w`. Where nothing gives a type, the two values decide, as the arms of a value `switch` do: an untyped integer alone gives `int` and an untyped float `f32`, a constant takes the other value's type, and a value whose type widens to the other's converts in its own branch, so `if c then x else y` with an `i32` `x` and an `int` `y` is an `int`. An enum literal, `nil` or a string literal then needs a type, with the usual errors.
       - A constant condition picks its value while checking: `const N := if true then 4 else 5` is the untyped constant 4, which can size an array, and in a function the result is the chosen value with its own type. The other value is still checked, so its errors show, but it is compiled into a block that never runs, and its type doesn't join the result's: `if true then x else "s"` is `x`.
       - Narrowing: `if p != nil then p.v else 0` narrows `p` in the first value, and `if p == nil then 0 else p.v` in the second, as the `if` statement does. The narrowing ends with the conditional.
       - Refused for now: a struct, an array or a slice as either value gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", as in a value `switch` (step 3k.7 built structs and arrays), and a call that returns nothing gives "expected a value". A chain of conditionals nests, so it stops at the parser's depth limit of 256 with "nested too deeply", which the `else if` chain of the `if` statement doesn't.
       - IR: no new kind. A conditional lowers to a typed `if`, `else` and `end`, as `??` does, so the backends and the reference interpreter didn't change: `n: u8 = if c then 1 else 2` gives `if @1 u8 %0`, `else u8 1` and `%3 = end u8 2`. A constant condition puts the value it doesn't pick inside `if false`.
       - `TK_THEN` follows `TK_ELSE`, so the token ids after it moved up by one. The parser writes a conditional in postorder as the condition, a `then` marker, the first value, an `else` marker, the second value and the conditional's node, which takes another kind when it is an item of a list, as a value `switch` does.

A program of 16 functions runs 36 hand-computed cases natively on arm64 under qemu, and the 16 join the grid, now 281 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. Two more showed that two checks could never fire, and those checks are gone.

         - The conditional has the lowest precedence and needs parentheses inside another expression, as option F proposes.
         - With a constant condition, the value that isn't picked is checked and compiled into code that never runs, and its type doesn't join the result's. Zig doesn't analyze that value at all.
         - Without a type from where the value goes, a `*T` and a `?*T` don't mix, as in a value `switch`: `if c then p else q` with a `?*Node` `q` is an error, and `r: ?*Node = if c then p else q` works.
         - The values are expressions, so neither can be `return` or `break`, which an arm of a value `switch` can.
         - A chain of conditionals counts against the parser's depth limit.

       - Where a literal goes now, at the top level: an element of an array literal, a field of a struct literal, and a global's value, where the type there is `*u8`, `[*]u8`, `?*u8` or `?[*]u8`. So `const NAMES: [3][*]u8 = .["zero", "one", "two"]`, `kw: Kw = .{ .name = "else", .len = 4, .alias = nil }` and `label: [*]u8 = "label"` work, and so does a named literal, as in `.[WORD, "b"]`. A type that isn't one of the four gives the usual "`untyped string` and `i32` don't mix without a conversion".
       - IR: no new kind. A pointer slot in constant data or in the globals image holds its string's offset in constant data, and two lists beside the IR, `data_relocs` and `global_relocs`, name those slots in order. A backend adds the address where it placed constant data to each listed slot: `ir_relocate` in `ir.h` does it for arm64, which copies both images itself, and for the reference interpreter, which now copies constant data too. The C backend writes an image that has slots as a struct of byte arrays and `const uint8_t *` members, whose initializers are address constants such as `(const uint8_t *)&chibi_data + 552`, so constant data stays `const` and clang can still fold loads from it. An image without slots is written as before, and code now takes both images' addresses with `&`, as in `(uint8_t *)&chibi_data + 16`.
       - The checker keeps each list sorted. Slots arrive in order, apart from the fields of a struct literal written out of order, which insert near the end. Copying an aggregate copies the slots inside it, which a binary search finds; the copy always lies after its source, so the copy never moves a slot it has yet to read.

     - **3k.6 `mul_high(a, b)`: built 2026-11-22, with the option that the owner's card of 2026-11-21 recommends, while the owner decides; removed in step 3k.19, after the owner chose `u128` on 2026-11-23.** It gives the high 64 bits of the 128-bit product of two `u64`. `src/names.h`'s identifier hash multiplies two 64-bit words to 128 bits and XORs the halves, which the translator can now write as `a *% b ^ mul_high(a, b)`. The card's other options were a `u128` type, which needs 128-bit arithmetic in the checker, both backends and the reference interpreter for one line of the compiler, and a hash written in 32-bit halves, which takes four multiplies where arm64 needs one `umulh`.
       - Both arguments must be `u64`, or untyped integer constants that fit in `u64`. Another type gives "`mul_high` takes two `u64`, found `u32` and `u32`". A narrower argument doesn't widen on its own, because the high half of a product that the caller thinks is narrower is likely a mistake, so it needs `u64(y)`. A constant that doesn't fit gives the usual "-1 does not fit in `u64`". Like the other builtins, `mul_high` is a plain predeclared name, and a declaration can take its place.
       - Constant arguments fold with exact arithmetic, so `const E := mul_high(11400714819323198485, 11400714819323198485)` is `7046029254386353128`. The result is a typed `u64`, also when both arguments are untyped.
       - IR: a new kind, `mul-high`, after `mul-wrap`, with the type `u64`. arm64 writes one `umulh`. The C backend writes `(uint64_t)(((unsigned __int128)a * b) >> 64)`, which clang and GCC compile to one `umulh` on arm64 and one `mul` on x86-64; the backend's C already needs GNU C for vector types and the overflow builtins. The reference interpreter computes it the same way.

       - What works: `r := if c then s else t` with struct or array values. Each branch fills one hidden local, and a declaration takes that local as its variable, as it takes a literal's. The local is the first value's own when that value has no name, so `if c then P.{ 1, 2 } else s` builds the literal in place and copies only `s`. A literal, a call or a conditional that is a whole value writes into the local of the conditional around it, so `if a then s else if b then P.{ 1, 2 } else make(3)` writes each value once, straight into `r`, and so does a conditional in the first value. A named place, such as a local, a parameter, a field, an element or `p.*`, is copied once. A struct or array conditional can be a declaration's or an assignment's value, a field's value, an element, an argument, a `return` value, or the base of `.x` and `[i]`, as in `(if c then s else t).y`.
       - Types: the type comes from where the value goes, or else from the two values, which must have the same type, because structs and arrays don't convert. An anonymous literal takes that type, as in `p: P = if c then .{ 1, 2 } else s`, and in the second value it can take the first value's type, as in `if c then s else .{ 3, 4 }`. A named field of a literal now gives its type to a conditional, as the rule of step 3k.4 says for an element of a literal: before, `.n = if c then K else 0` made an `int` that a `u32` field refused.
       - A constant condition gives the chosen value's place, as it gives a scalar's value.
       - Still refused: a slice as either value gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", because a slice is two words, which a typed `if` can't carry, and a slice place has two locals. An anonymous literal as the first value, with nothing to give it a type, gives "the type of this literal isn't known here".
       - IR: no new kind. A struct or array conditional lowers to the statement form of `if`, `else` and `end`, without a type, and each branch stores into the hidden local or copies into it.
       - The checker finds the shared local without a new pass. A literal, a call and a conditional look at the node after their own, which says whether they are a whole value of a conditional, and the conditional's entries on the value stack hold its local. A conditional keeps one more entry there, for the local of the conditional around it.

     - **3k.8 `?extern fn` and `bitcast` of C function pointers: built 2026-11-22,** for the translator, because `src/driver.c` ignores SIGPIPE while it writes to the C compiler and then puts the old handler back: `void (*on_pipe)(int) = signal(SIGPIPE, SIG_IGN);`, then `signal(SIGPIPE, on_pipe);`. `signal` takes and returns a C function pointer that is NULL for `SIG_DFL`, and `SIG_IGN` is the integer 1 cast to a function pointer. Step 3h.3 left such pointers to optionals and `?extern fn`, and step 3e.1 built optional pointers.
       - What works: `?extern fn(...)` is a C function pointer that may be nil, with nil at address 0, so it has a pointer's size, alignment and registers, as `?*T` does. `nil`, `== nil`, `!!`, `??`, `??=` and narrowing work as they do for `?*T`, so after `h != nil`, `h` passes where an `extern fn` goes. An `extern fn` converts to a `?extern fn`, and a function's name where a `?extern fn` goes makes the same adapter that it makes for an `extern fn`, as in `swap_hook(tap)`. A `?extern fn` can be a parameter, a result, a local, a global, a field or an element, for chibi functions and C functions alike.
       - `bitcast` now changes `extern fn` and `?extern fn` values too, into integers and pointers of the same size and back, as the Casts row allows any value of the same size: `bitcast(?extern fn(i32), int(1))` is C's `SIG_IGN`. A result that is never nil checks for nil, as a `*T` result does: `bitcast(extern fn(i32), n)` of an integer or of a `?extern fn` adds `check-nil`, a constant 0 gives "`extern fn(i32) -> void` is never nil, so this needs a value", and `bitcast(?extern fn(i32), u64(0))` is a constant nil. From a `*T` or an `extern fn` nothing is checked. A function pointer promises and needs no alignment, so no `bitcast` to or from one adds `check-align`.

A chibi `fn` type stays out of `bitcast`.

The reference interpreter now gives an `extern fn` value 64 bits; before, it read the index of the type's parameter list as the width, which no test reached, because no function that the reference runs made an `extern fn` value.
       - The checker asks one new question, `is_nilable`: an optional whose type is never nil, which is a `*T`, a `[*]T` or an `extern fn`. The checker works out the answer once, when it makes the type `?T`, and keeps it in the type's second argument, so asking costs one load. `is_nilable` takes the place of `is_optional_pointer` where the checker asks whether it supports an optional: sizes, storing, passing, conversions, `bitcast`, `!!`, `??` and `??=`. `is_optional_pointer` stays where only a pointer works: `.*`, fields, indexes, slices and `+`.
       - Tests: the checker folds the size and alignment of a `?extern fn` and of a struct that holds one, and dumps a type constant, globals and constants that hold `nil`, and a constant nil made by `bitcast`. 13 diagnostics, each with its position, cover a constant 0 and `nil` where an `extern fn` goes, `?fn`, `.*`, an index, a field and `+` on a `?extern fn`, `!!`, `??` and `??=` on an `extern fn`, a `?extern fn` passed where an `extern fn` goes, a `bitcast` of the wrong size, and a `bitcast` to a chibi `fn`. An IR dump of two functions pins where `bitcast` adds `check-nil` and where it doesn't, the adapter made for a `?extern fn` argument, field and `return`, narrowing into an `extern fn` argument, `!!`, `??`, `??=` and `nil` in a literal. A program of 12 functions runs 36 hand-computed cases natively on arm64 under qemu, 2 of which trap on nil, with `?extern fn` values made by `bitcast` in locals, parameters, results, a global, a struct field, array elements and conditionals. Its 11 functions that take two integers join the grid, now 323 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The hand-computed results agree with a model in Python, which agrees with the C backend on all 3,168 points of the grid. A function `hooks`, in the arm64 test and in the C backend's test, hands a chibi function's adapter to a C function that keeps it in a `?extern fn` slot as `signal` does, gets back nil and then the adapter, has C call it, compares it with the same adapter, passes the integer 1 as `SIG_IGN` does and gets the 1 back. The tests catch all 20 planted bugs. One passed at first: a `bitcast` from an `extern fn` to a `*u8` that checked for nil, which the IR dump now covers.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no optionals or function pointers: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as before, and checking and arm64 code generation run the same 696.47 and 463.16 million instructions. The first version ran 0.12% more in checking, 813,480 instructions, all of them from where clang placed blocks around the new test in `is_scalar`: a jump more for each binary operator in `binary`, and a spilled register and jumps in `walk`. Working out `is_nilable` once, when `?T` is made, and testing it before the test for an `extern fn` brings checking back to the same count.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `bitcast` takes `extern fn` and `?extern fn` but not a chibi `fn`, which isn't a value.
         - A function's name on the right of `??` or in a comparison has nothing to give it a type, so `h ?? on` gives "`on` is a function, so it can't be used here", as `f == on` already did. A local with a type works: `f: extern fn(i32) = on`, then `h ?? f`.
       - Hand-off: no IR kind or type id changes. An optional type's second argument, 0 before, is now 1 when its nil is address 0, which is true of every optional that reaches the IR.
       - Not built yet: calls through an `extern fn` value, which chibi has no IR for, and a global that starts as a function's adapter.
     - **3k.9 Typed variadics, `xs: ..T`: built 2026-11-22,** for the translator, because `src/c_source.c` writes its C through `put_line(Source *s, const char *format, ...)`, a C variadic function with a body that it calls 115 times, and chibi can't define a C-variadic function (question 3 of sheet 4e). The step builds option A of sheet 4e in [syntax-options-effects.md](syntax-options-effects.md), which the owner's card of 2026-11-22 about `put_line` recommends, while the owner decides.
       - What works: `name: ..T` as the last parameter of a chibi function. Inside the function the parameter is a `[]T`. A call passes any number of arguments after the named ones, and each converts to `T` as an argument to a parameter of type `T` does: an untyped constant takes `T`, a narrower integer widens, an anonymous literal takes `T` as its type, and a string literal goes where `T` is `[*]u8`. The caller stores the arguments, left to right, into a hidden local array `[n]T` in its own frame and passes a slice of it, so the function compiles once and a call allocates nothing. A call with no arguments for the `..` parameter passes an empty slice of a `[0]T` local. `T` can be any type that a slice holds: integers, floats, pointers, `[*]T`, slices, arrays and structs.
       - The arguments are read in order, as in an array literal, because the call and the array literal now share one function that converts and stores the elements. A struct argument that a later argument changes is copied before the later argument runs: `weigh(p, P.{ bump(&p), 0 }, p)` passes `p`'s old value first and its new value last.
       - A function's type prints its last parameter with `..`, as in `fn(i32, i32, ..i32) -> i32`, and a declaration prints `xs: ..i32`.
       - Errors: `..T` before another parameter, or with a default, gives "`..` must be the last parameter" from the parser, and `xs: ..` gives "expected a type". A function with a `..` parameter whose other parameters have defaults gives "a function with a `..` parameter can't have defaults", because nothing would say whether an argument fills a default or the `..` parameter. Too few arguments give "wrong number of arguments: expected at least 2, found 1", the message of C's variadic calls. An argument that doesn't convert to `T` gives the conversion's error at that argument, and the call then gives no further errors. An `extern fn` or an `export fn` can't take `..T`, because C has no slices: "C has no `[]u8`, so an `extern fn` can't pass or return it"; C's variadic tail stays the bare `..` of step 3h.2. A `..T` whose `[]T` the checker doesn't support yet, such as `..?int`, gives "the checker doesn't support this yet" at the parameter and at each call, as a parameter of type `[]?int` does.
       - IR: no new kind. A call stores each argument with `index` and `store-at`, or `copy` for a struct, into the hidden local, and passes its address and the count as the slice's two words, as a call that passes a slice of a local array does. The function takes the general call path because its last parameter is a slice. The C backend writes a `[0]T` local as `T s[0]`, a zero-length array that clang and GCC accept, as it already did for `x: [0]int`.
       - Tests: the parser reads `..T`, also `..[]u8`, and refuses it before another parameter, with a default and without a type. The checker prints two declarations with `..`, and 15 diagnostics, each with its position, cover too few arguments, arguments of the wrong type, an argument that is a pointer or an array literal, a function with a `..` parameter passed where an `extern fn` goes, defaults, `extern fn` and `export fn`, `..?int`, an unknown element type with and without arguments, and no further errors after a bad argument, in a call and in an array literal. An IR dump pins a call with no arguments for the `..` parameter, one with a widened `u8`, and one with an anonymous struct literal. A program of 17 functions runs 53 hand-computed cases natively on arm64 under qemu, 2 of which trap on a bounds check: no arguments, one, many, more than the 8 argument registers, an index past the end, the order of side effects, structs, nested calls, `u8` and `u32` widened to `u64`, calls in a loop, a value read before a later store, slices, floats, string literals as `[*]u8`, arrays, calls that return structs, a conditional between structs, and a struct changed by a later argument. Its 17 functions join the grid, now 340 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. Of 29 planted bugs, the tests catch 27. The other 2 removed lines that turned out to do nothing, and those lines are gone: a check that the `..` parameter's type is a slice, which is either a slice or the poison type that every later step already ignores, and removing the `..` marker from the checker's stack before it reads the parameters, which skip it anyway. A third passed at first: an array literal that ignored a bad element still reported it but no longer stopped the errors after it, which the last diagnostic test now covers, with the same test for a call. One planted bug, which found the wrong struct to copy early, failed only on arm64, through its hand-computed results, because the grid compares the backends with each other on the same IR.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no `..` parameters: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as before, and arm64 code generation runs the same 463.16 million instructions. Checking runs 0.11% fewer, 695.73 million instead of 696.47, in `convert`, `declare_local` and `check_decl`, which the step didn't change, from where clang places code. With `NODE_REST_TYPE` at the end of the parser's node kinds, after the markers, instead of after `NODE_VARIADIC`, checking ran 0.65% more, all in `walk`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The hidden array is one local for each call, so a function with many such calls has a frame that holds all their arrays at once, as it does for the hidden locals of structs. `put_instruction` in `src/c_source.c` calls `put_line` 44 times.
         - The slice points into the caller's frame, so a function that keeps it after it returns keeps a dangling pointer, as with a slice of any local array.
         - Defaults are refused in a function with a `..` parameter, and `..T` in `extern fn` and `export fn`.
         - A function with a `..` parameter can't be passed where a function goes: its type, such as `fn(i32, i32, ..i32) -> i32`, matches no `extern fn` type, and no type can be written with `..` yet.
       - Hand-off: no IR kind or type id changes. `FN_REST` is bit 34 of a function type's second argument. `NODE_REST_TYPE` follows `NODE_VARIADIC`, so the node kinds after it shift by one, and `DIAG_REST_DEFAULT` follows `DIAG_NOT_VARIADIC`, so the diagnostics after it shift by one.
       - Not built yet: passing an existing slice as `f(..xs)`, which gives the error of a `[]T` where a `T` goes; `..T` in a function type; and `..any`, which waits for `any` (sheet 8).
       - Fixed on 2026-11-22, in step 3t.9: a call to a function with a `..` parameter and no `->`, whose result the checker infers, compiled to nothing, with no error. Three places that test for an inferred result compared the whole second argument of the function's type, which holds the result in its low 32 bits and the flags above them, so `FN_REST` hid the inferred result: a call didn't check the function's body first, the checked body didn't store its result, and the call saw an unknown result and gave the poison type without a diagnostic. The translated `src/c_source.c` showed it: all 115 calls to `put_line`, whose result is inferred, were missing, and clang noticed only because `put_line`'s C returned `uint64_t` with a bare `return`. The tests now compare the low 32 bits, and the stored result keeps the flags. The checker's expected output had shown it too: two declarations with `..` printed `-> (inferred)` where other functions without a result print `-> void`, and they now print `-> void`. The tests add a third declaration whose result is inferred as `int`, a function passed where an `extern fn` goes before its body is checked, and a grid function, `rst_void`, that calls a `..` function with no result and one with an inferred result, both defined after it, with 3 hand-computed cases on arm64; the grid is now 341 functions. Each of the 4 changed lines, put back as it was, fails the tests. The timing corpus compiles to the same IR and machine code, and checking runs 0.003% more instructions, 695.75 million instead of 695.73, for keeping the flags when an inferred result is stored.
     - **3k.10 `@inline` and `@noinline`: built 2026-11-22,** for the translator, because the compiler's C marks 142 functions `NOINLINE` and 12 `ALWAYS_INLINE`, and a translation without the marks loses 3% to 10% of its speed ([translator.md](translator.md#speed-of-the-translated-compiler)). The step builds option 0 of the owner's card of 2026-11-22 about inlining marks, which recommends it and which the owner chose on 2026-11-23. It uses the form that adopt-or-settle 3.8 recommends for attributes, `@name` before a declaration, of which only `@tail` was decided before.
       - What works: `@inline` or `@noinline` before a function with a body, as in `@inline fn twice(x: int) -> int => x * 2`, `@noinline pub fn slow()` and `@noinline export fn entry()`. The mark comes before `pub` and `export`, and it may stand on a line of its own. The C backend writes `__attribute__((always_inline))` or `__attribute__((noinline))` after `CHIBI_FN`, on the function's declaration and on its definition, as `src/common.h` spells the C's marks. An `export fn`'s C adapter has no mark, so an `@inline export fn` inlines into its adapter. The arm64 backend inlines nothing and ignores both marks. The checker's declaration dump prints the mark first, as in `@noinline pub export fn g() -> void`.
       - `inline` and `noinline` are builtin names, interned after `tail` (section 1), so the parser compares a token's id and no keyword is added. They stay ordinary names everywhere else: `inline := noinline` parses.
       - Errors, all from the parser: any other name after `@` gives "expected `@inline` or `@noinline`" at that name, and so does `@tail return f(x)` until `@tail` is built. A mark before anything but `fn name`, after an optional `pub` and `export`, gives "`@inline` and `@noinline` must come before a function with a body": an `extern fn`, a `struct`, a variable, a function literal and a second mark. `pub @inline fn f()` gives "`pub` must come before a declaration". A mark on a function inside a body gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", as `pub fn` there does.
       - Parser and checker: two node kinds, `NODE_INLINE` and `NODE_NOINLINE`, each with the declaration as its one child and the `@` as its token, as `NODE_PUB` holds its declaration. `declare` reads them as it reads `pub` and `export`, into two new declaration flags, `DECL_INLINE` and `DECL_NOINLINE`. The flags no longer fit in a byte, so `Decls.flags` is now `u16`.
       - IR: no change.
       - Tests: the parser reads both marks, before `pub export` and on their own line, and gives each error above at its position, and the node kinds of two declarations pin the new kinds. The checker prints four declarations with and without marks, and a mark inside a body gives its two diagnostics. A C backend test finds both attributes on declarations and definitions in the C for a program of 5 functions, which the arm64 backend runs natively on arm64 under qemu with 12 hand-computed cases, 1 of which traps on overflow: an `@inline` function with a `=>` body, an `@noinline` function that calls it, an `@inline pub fn`, a recursive `@inline` function and an `@noinline` function on a line of its own. Its 3 functions that take two integers join the grid, now 344 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The tests catch all 24 planted bugs, each through a test written for it: the parser's trees, errors and node kinds, the checker's declaration dump and the diagnostics of a nested mark, and the C backend's test of the attributes.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no marks: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as before. Checking runs 695.76 million instructions against 695.75: 50,079 more in `check_decl`, which now reads 16-bit flags, and 39,803 fewer in `check_package`, where clang compiles the longer loop over a declaration's marks differently. Lexing runs 134 instructions more in all, for the two names it interns at the start, and parsing and arm64 code generation run the same. The first version had one node kind, `NODE_ATTRIBUTE`, with the mark's name as its token, and checking ran 0.12% more, 883,167 instructions, all in `walk`, in four places among the node kinds before the lists; after the markers it ran 0.64% more. That cost comes from where clang places code in `walk` when the kinds of the lists and markers move by one, and step 3k.9's `NODE_REST_TYPE`, which moved them by one the other way, had saved 0.11% the same way. Two node kinds move them by two, and checking then runs exactly as before.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `@inline` means always inline, as C's `always_inline`, Zig's `inline fn` and Odin's `#force_inline` do. A hint, as Rust's `#[inline]` and C's `inline` keyword give, isn't built.
         - The mark comes before `pub` and `export`, as Rust writes `#[inline] pub fn`; Zig writes `pub inline fn`. Only one order is accepted.
         - Only a function with a body takes a mark, because an `extern fn`'s body is C's to compile, and only one mark, because the two contradict each other.
         - The C backend doesn't write C's `inline` beside `always_inline`. GCC warns that such a function "might not be inlinable", which `chibi build` hides with `-w`, but with `-DCHIBI_FN=`, as the tests compile, a C `inline` definition would give no symbol to the functions that clang can't inline, such as a recursive one.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `NODE_INLINE` and `NODE_NOINLINE` follow `NODE_EXTERN`, so the node kinds after them shift by two, and the builtin names after `tail` shift by two.
       - Not built yet: `@tail` and its rules (question 8), attributes with arguments, such as `@align(16)`, and attributes on blocks, other declarations and statements.
     - **3k.11 Release-fast builds: built 2026-11-22,** as the Integer overflow row and the Casts row of 2026-11-10 decided, for the translator: the compiler's C has no overflow, shift, range or alignment checks, and the translated compiler ran them in every build ([translator.md](translator.md#speed-of-the-translated-compiler)).
       - What works: `chibi build --release-fast` and `chibi c --release-fast`. The flag sets `release_fast` in the checker, which then writes the unchecked form of each operation that has one, so the IR says what runs and the backends don't change. In a release-fast build:
         - `+`, `-`, `*` and prefix `-` on integers, with their assignment forms, become `+%`, `-%`, `*%` and `-%`, the IR's `add-wrap`, `sub-wrap`, `mul-wrap` and `neg-wrap`. Floats don't change, and a constant expression that overflows is still an error.
         - A shift whose count isn't a constant masks the count to the width first, `and int %1, 63` before `shl int %0, %2`, and a vector's count to the lane width. A constant count out of range is still an error.
         - An integer conversion `T(x)` keeps the low bits. To a type no wider than the source it becomes `truncate`, so `u8(x)` of an `int` 300 is 44 and `u32(x)` of an `i32` -1 is 4294967295. From a signed type to a wider unsigned type, it first widens to the signed type of the target's width, `convert int` and then `truncate u64`, so `u64(x)` of an `i32` -1 is 2^64 - 1, as C's conversion and Rust's `as` give. A constant that doesn't fit is still an error. A conversion that widens is unchanged.
         - `Color(n)` doesn't check that `n` is a member's value, as the Casts row checks it only in checked builds. It keeps the low bits of `n` in the enum's backing type, `truncate Level` or, from a narrower type, `convert Level`. `u8(Color.x)` and the other conversions of an enum to an integer keep the low bits as integer conversions do.
         - `bitcast` to a pointer that needs more alignment doesn't check the address.
       - What still checks in a release-fast build: a zero divisor and the most negative value divided by -1, as the Integer overflow row decided; bounds and nil; a `switch` that no case matches, which an enum made by an unchecked `Color(n)` can now reach, and the end of a function that must return a value; the end of an inclusive slice, whose `hi + 1` would turn `s[0..=hi]` with the largest `u64` into an empty slice if it wrapped; and a float converted to an integer, which has no low bits to keep, and which C leaves undefined out of range.
       - IR: no new kind. A default build writes the IR it wrote before.
       - Tests: the checker's IR dump pins 19 functions in a release-fast build, and the reference interpreter runs 24 hand-computed cases of them, 2 of which trap: wrapped `+`, `-`, `*`, `+=`, `*=` and negation, masked counts of `<<` and `>>`, a constant count left alone, the conversions above, enum conversions without the member check, and division by zero and the most negative value divided by -1. The dump also pins a float left alone, a vector's masked count, a float converted to an integer with its check, and `bitcast` without `check-align`. A program of 6 functions that take two integers runs natively on arm64 under qemu, built release-fast, with 12 hand-computed cases, 3 of which trap, and agrees with the reference interpreter on a grid of 288 argument pairs for each function. The driver builds the same functions with a `main` through `chibi build --release-fast`, with UBSan in the C compiler, and runs them on 3 pairs of arguments: one prints all 6 results, and the others trap on division by zero and on a float out of range. A program that traps on overflow in a default build prints the wrapped result with `--release-fast`. The tests catch all 28 planted bugs: 22 through the release-fast IR dump, 3 that changed default builds through the tests of default builds, 2 in the driver through its test, and 1 in the reference interpreter, which checked the member list for a `convert` to an enum, through AddressSanitizer, as it read past the side array.
       - Measured on the timing corpus: in a default build, the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as before, and the C build runs 0.05% more instructions, 954,256, all in checking: 815,528 in `binary` and 138,704 in `walk`, where clang inlines `unary`, for the test of the flag. The translated compiler, written by `chibi c --release-fast`, runs 2,044.2 million instructions against 2,095.9 million for `chibi c`, 2.5% fewer, and 8.2% more than the C build's 1,888.9 million.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The spelling `--release-fast`, after Zig's `-O ReleaseFast`. chibi has no separate debug and release builds yet, so a default build is the checked build of both.
         - A signed integer converted to a wider unsigned type keeps the value modulo 2^N, as C does, and doesn't check. Sheet 2 calls every conversion that can lose a value a narrowing ([syntax-options-data.md](syntax-options-data.md), options I to L), so the Casts row's release-fast rule covers it.
         - A float converted to an integer checks in every build. Without the check, the C backend's conversion would be undefined out of range, and keeping it defined, as Rust's saturating `as` does, costs compares too.
         - The end of an inclusive slice checks in every build, as part of the bounds check.
         - The arm64 backend still compares a masked shift count with the width, a compare and a branch that can't trap; the C backend's compiler removes them, and on arm64 and x86-64 a 32-bit or 64-bit shift masks its count in hardware, so the `and` could go too.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. The checker has a new field, `release_fast`, which a zero-initialized `Checker` leaves off.
       - Not built yet: separate debug and release builds, and turning the checks off for one function, which the Casts row allows. `assert` came with step 3k.12.
     - **3k.12 `assert`: built 2026-11-22,** for the translator: the compiler's C has 112 asserts, which the C build compiles out with `NDEBUG`, and the translation wrote each as `if !c { abort() }` ([translator.md](translator.md) rule 13), which runs in every build and cost it 3.1% of its instructions on the corpus. The step builds option 0 of the owner's card of 2026-11-22 about asserts, which recommends it, while the owner decides, in a commit of its own so that another option can replace it. On 2026-11-23 the owner chose option 1, an assert that release-fast builds assume, and step 3k.16 replaced this step's release-fast rule and its `if false` blocks.
       - What works: `assert(c)` with a `bool` condition, as a statement. A default build evaluates `c` and traps with "assertion failed" when it is false. A release-fast build checks `c` for errors but doesn't evaluate it, so `assert(f())` doesn't call `f`, as C's `assert` under `NDEBUG`. `assert(true)` writes nothing in either build, and `assert(false)` traps when it runs in a default build.
       - `assert` is a builtin name, interned after `align_of` (section 1), so a package declaration or a local named `assert` hides it, as it hides the other builtin functions.
       - Errors: "wrong number of arguments: expected 1, found 2" at `assert`; "expected `bool`, found `int`" at a condition of another type, and "expected a value" at a type or at a call that gives no value; "`assert` is a function, so it can't be used here" for `h := assert`; and "expected a constant" for an `assert` in a constant's value. `assert` gives no value, so `a := assert(b)`, `_ = assert(b)` and `assert(assert(b))` give "expected a value".
       - IR: a new kind, `assert`, after `case`, which takes a `bool`, gives no value, and traps with the new trap kind "assertion failed" when the `bool` is false. In a release-fast build the checker writes `if false` when it meets the name `assert`, and `drop` and `end` after the condition, as `false and x` skips `x`, so the backends need nothing new: the arm64 backend skips the block and the C compiler removes `if (0u) { ... }`. A block left empty, by a constant condition or a condition with errors, is taken back out. The C backend writes `if (!v2) chibi_trap(9);`, and the arm64 backend a `cbz` to the function's trap stub, after a `cset` where the condition is a compare.
       - The arm64 backend's loop over the trap kinds at the end of each function now collects the kinds the function uses into a mask and writes their stubs from it, in the same order. With an eleventh kind clang stopped unrolling the old loop, and code generation ran 0.17% more instructions on the corpus, 40 more for each of its 20,000 functions.
       - Tests: the checker's error test gives each diagnostic above at its position. The IR dump pins 3 functions in a default build and in a release-fast build, and the reference interpreter runs 6 hand-computed cases of them in a default build, 4 of which trap, and 4 in a release-fast build, where none of them traps: conditions that compare, a `bool` parameter, a call with a side effect under `and`, `assert(true)`, and `assert(false)` under an `if`. A release-fast dump of a body with 5 asserts that have errors, beside one without, pins its blocks. A program of 4 functions that take two integers runs natively on arm64 under qemu with 10 hand-computed cases, 5 of which trap, and its functions join the grid, now 348 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The release-fast program of step 3k.11 gains a function whose result shows whether its asserts ran: built release-fast, arm64 runs it under qemu and the driver's program prints its result, and the driver also builds the program without the flag, where it traps with "assertion failed". The tests catch all 25 planted bugs: 15 in the checker, 11 through its IR dumps and 4 through its error test; 1 in the reference interpreter through its runs; 3 in the C backend through the grid and the driver's test; and 6 in the arm64 backend under qemu, 3 of which, in the new mask of trap kinds, leave a trap's branch unpatched, so the first test that traps hangs until the suite's 30-second alarm stops it. In a first round 3 of the 25 passed: an `assert` of an unknown name, which left its block open in a release-fast build, the text of the new trap, and a register that the arm64 backend didn't free after the test, which costs spills but no wrong results. The release-fast dump of errors, the driver's default build and a count of the stack stores in a function whose second assert has an `or` now catch them.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no asserts: the IR and the machine code are byte for byte the same as before. The C build runs 243,098 fewer instructions, 0.01%: checking runs 450,498 fewer, all in `walk`, and code generation 207,223 more, in `emit_function`, for the new `case` and the eleventh trap kind. A first version closed the block of a release-fast `assert` whose arguments have errors inside `call`, which clang inlines into `walk`, and checking ran 1.84 million instructions more, from where clang placed code; the test now sits in a function of its own that only a call with errors reaches.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The spelling `assert(c)`, C's, as a builtin rather than a library function, because a release-fast build must not evaluate the condition. Zig's `std.debug.assert` is an ordinary function that the optimizer may assume, Rust's `assert!` checks in every build, and its `debug_assert!` only in debug builds.
         - A release-fast build still checks the condition for errors, as Rust's `debug_assert!` does, where C's `NDEBUG` drops the text, so an assert can't break in one kind of build only.
         - No message argument, as in C. The trap prints "trap: assertion failed", and the `#line` directives of step 3j.5 give a debugger the chibi line.
         - A constant `assert(false)` traps when it runs, not while compiling, as in C, and the code after it stays reachable for the checker.
         - The checker doesn't narrow from a condition: after `assert(p != nil)`, `p` still needs `!!`. In a release-fast build, narrowing would trust a condition that nothing checked, which is option 1 of the card.
         - The arm64 backend materializes a compare with `cset` before the `cbz`, where `if` branches on the compare's flags.
       - Hand-off: `IR_ASSERT` follows `IR_CASE`, `TRAP_ASSERT` follows `TRAP_NIL` and `NAME_ASSERT` follows `NAME_ALIGN_OF`, so no earlier IR kind, trap kind or builtin moves; the ids of other names move by one. The LowIR spike doesn't know `assert` yet, and the grid's new program uses it.
       - Not built yet: the card's other two options; an assert checked while compiling, as C's `static_assert`, which the translator leaves out; and a message.
     - **3k.13 Target constants: built 2026-11-22,** for the switch: the compiler's C chooses code by target with `#if`, and one chibi source for every target needs a way to write those tests ([translator.md](translator.md#one-source-for-every-target)). The step builds option 0 of the owner's card of 2026-11-22 about target code, which recommends it, in a commit of its own so that another option could replace it; the owner chose it on 2026-11-23.
       - What works: `target_os` and `target_arch` are predeclared constants of two predeclared enums, `TargetOs`, with the members `linux`, `macos` and `windows`, and `TargetArch`, with `arm64` and `x86_64`. `if target_os == .macos and target_arch == .arm64 { … }` is an ordinary `if` with a constant condition: both arms are checked on every target, and the arm that the target rules out emits no code, so a call in it to an `extern fn` that only macOS has links on Linux, at any optimization level. The constants work wherever constants do: in `const PAGE: u64 = if target_os == .macos and target_arch == .arm64 then 16384 else 4096`, in a global's value, in `u8(target_os)`, in a `switch`, and in a variable of type `TargetArch`.
       - The checker checks for one target, the host by default. A new `Checker` field, `target`, holds the member name of each constant, and `check_init` sets it from `TARGET_OS_HOST` and `TARGET_ARCH_HOST` in `src/check.h`, which test the C compiler's macros as `CONVENTION_HOST` does. The two enums are declared on first use, after the program's own declarations, so a program that doesn't name them checks, dumps and compiles as before.
       - The nine names are builtins interned after `assert` (section 1), so a package declaration named `target_os` or `TargetArch` hides the builtin, as it hides the other builtins, and `linux` alone is an unknown name.
       - Errors, from the existing checks: "`TargetOs` has no member `armm64`"; "`TargetOs` and `TargetArch` don't mix without a conversion"; "expected a function or a type" for `target_os()`; the assignment error for `target_arch = .arm64`; "`untyped integer` and `TargetOs` don't mix without a conversion" for `w: TargetOs = 1`; and "the switch doesn't handle `.x86_64`" for a `switch` that misses a member.
       - C backend: when a program reads `target_os`, its C holds, after the prelude, `#ifndef __linux__`, `#error "chibi wrote this C for target_os linux"` and `#endif`, with `__APPLE__` for macOS and `_WIN32` for Windows, and the same for `target_arch` with `__x86_64__` or `__aarch64__`, so a C compiler for another target refuses the C. A program that reads neither writes C that builds for any target, as before. An `if` with a constant condition writes only its live arm, in a plain block, as the arm64 backend already did. Before, it wrote `if (0u) { … }`, which clang keeps at `-O0` when the arm holds the label of a loop, so the calls in a ruled-out loop had to link; GCC drops such an arm. The backend now asserts that its brace depth stays above the function's.
       - IR: no new kind. The checker folds the constants, as it folds any enum constant.
       - Tests: the checker's IR dump pins a program of 8 declarations for Linux on x86-64 and for macOS on arm64, and its declaration dump pins the two enums; the error test gives each diagnostic above at its position; and a program that declares its own `target_os` and `TargetArch` shows them hiding the builtins. A program of 3 functions that take two integers, two of which call a function that no library has in an arm that only Windows takes, one of them in a loop, runs as arm64 code under qemu with 3 hand-computed cases and joins the grid, now 351 functions, that both backends run against the reference interpreter. The C backend's test pins the guards and the C of two functions for Linux on x86-64, checks that a program that reads only `target_arch` gets only its guard and a program that reads neither gets none, and compiles C written for Windows, which the C compiler refuses with the guard's message. The driver builds a program at `-O0` that calls a missing function in a ruled-out loop and returns a code made from both constants, and runs it.
       - Planted bugs: 23, all caught. Of 12 in the checker, the dumps and the error test caught 6, the C backend's guard test 1 (no `target_read`), ASan 2 (no memo of the enum, no room for the two declarations), an existing assert 1 (the enums marked unchecked), and the guards themselves 2: a host target set to the wrong OS or arch made the C compiler refuse the grid's C. Of 11 in the C backend, the guard test and its pinned C caught 6, the grid's C compiler 1, ASan 1, and the new depth assert 3. Before that assert, those 3 wrote endless indentation until the test's alarm.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which reads no target constant, the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 906 more instructions of 1,889 million, and the release-fast translated build 1,622 more, still 3.0% more than the C build.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The names: `TargetOs` and `TargetArch`; `arm64`, as the backend's file, `x86_64`, as the owner wrote it, `macos`, as Zig's, and `windows`, which only `O_BINARY` in `src/driver.c` needs.
         - The proposal had the C test the C compiler's macros in place of the constants, so that one C file builds for any target. The checker folds the constants instead, because a `const` or a global needs their value while compiling, and because clang keeps a dead arm at `-O0` when the arm holds a label. The guard makes C written for one target refuse another.
         - There is a guard for each constant that the program reads, so a program that reads only `target_arch` builds on any OS.
         - Only `if` rules code out. A `switch` on a target constant emits every arm, so the calls in its arms must link on every target.
         - On a host that is neither macOS nor Windows, `target_os` is `linux`, and on one that isn't arm64, `target_arch` is `x86_64`. The guard refuses the C on such a host when the program reads the constant.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `NAME_TARGET_OS` and the eight names after it follow `NAME_ASSERT`, so no earlier name moves; the ids of other names move by 9. The checker has the new fields `target`, `target_types` and `target_read`.
       - Not built yet: `--target` for `chibi c` and `chibi build`, which the arm64 build of the compiler needs once chibi is its source, and dead arms of a `switch` on a constant. `--target` came with step 3k.14.
     - **3k.14 `--target`: built 2026-11-22,** for the switch: once the compiler's chibi source reads `target_os` and `target_arch`, `make translated` must write the arm64 translation's C on an x86-64 machine, and step 3k.13's checker folds the constants only for the host.
       - What works: `chibi c --target arm64-linux` and `chibi build --target arm64-linux` check the program for that target, named by the arch and then the OS, each a member of `TargetArch` or `TargetOs`, joined by a hyphen. Without the option the target is the host, as before, and `--target` for the host writes the same C as no option.
       - The checker has a new function, `check_target(c, k, text, len)`, which sets target constant `k` to the member with that name and reports whether there is one. The driver splits the option at its first hyphen and calls it for the arch and for the OS after `check_init`.
       - `chibi build --target` doesn't pass a target to the C compiler: the arguments after `--` name it, such as `-- --target=aarch64-linux-gnu` for clang, and step 3k.13's guards make the C compiler refuse C written for another target.
       - Errors: "chibi: --target needs a target, such as arm64-linux" when the option ends the command, and "chibi: unknown target linux-arm64; a target is an arch and an OS, such as arm64-linux" for a name that isn't a target.
       - `make translated` writes the arm64 translation's C with `--target arm64-linux`, so the arm64 bootstrap check compares C written for arm64 on x86-64 with the C that the arm64 build writes for itself. The two are the same today, because the translation reads no target constant yet.
       - Tests: the driver checks a program with two constants that overflow `u8` only on arm64 or only on Windows, for 4 targets, so each error shows which constant the target folded; 8 names that aren't targets; the guards of C written for arm64 on Linux, x86-64 on Windows and arm64 on macOS; that `--target` for the host writes the same C as no option; and that a program built with it runs to the host's code. The usage test shows the option in both commands, and a new row gives the error for a missing target.
       - Planted bugs: 13, all caught by the new driver tests: 8 in the driver (the option's name, copying it to the program, taking its value, the check for a missing value, the order of arch and OS, the length of either part, and going on after an unknown target) and 5 in `check_target` (the range of names, only its first name, a prefix match, no store, and a store into the other constant). A plant that drops `--target` from the Makefile survives until the translation reads a target constant.
       - Measured on the timing corpus: the C build runs 30 more instructions of 1,889 million, and the release-fast translated build 24 fewer.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The spelling `--target arch-os`, in the order of Zig's `-target aarch64-linux` and clang's `--target=aarch64-linux-gnu`, with chibi's member names, so `arm64` and not `aarch64`. Odin and Go name the OS first.
         - No vendor or ABI part, such as clang's `-gnu`, because the checker needs neither.
         - `chibi build` leaves the C compiler's target to the arguments after `--`, because the driver doesn't know how the C compiler spells it, and GCC needs a cross compiler rather than a flag.
         - The driver reports an unknown target after the files are parsed, because the member names come with the lexer's name table.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `check_target` is new in `src/check.h`.
       - Not built yet: merging the two translations with target constants, which the switch commit does.
     - **3k.15 Built-in packages: built 2026-11-23,** as the owner chose that day on the card about how builtins are named: a built-in package, as Odin's `base:intrinsics` and Go's `math/bits`, in place of the plain predeclared names that the card recommended ([decisions.md](../decisions.md), the built-in packages row of 2026-11-23).
       - What works: `import "std/bits"`, `import "std/mem"` and `import "std/simd"` at the top level of a file let that file call the builtins of steps 3i.1, 3i.2, 3i.5, 3k.2 and 3k.6 as members of a package, as in `bits.trailing_zeros(x)`. `bits` holds `trailing_zeros`, `leading_zeros`, `count_ones`, `mul_high`, `add_overflows`, `sub_overflows` and `mul_overflows`; `mem` holds `unaligned_load`, `unaligned_store`, `size_of` and `align_of`; and `simd` holds `lookup` and `top_bits`. A call checks, folds and emits as the plain call did, so `const A := bits.count_ones(7)` is the constant 3. `assert`, `target_os` and `target_arch` stay predeclared.
       - The checker: the three package names are builtins interned after `x86_64` (section 1), and `src/lexer.h` now lists the members of each package together. `declare` records each `import` in a new table, `imports`, which holds the import's token for each file and package, so an import holds only for its own file. Where a name resolves to a package that its file imports, `resolve_builtin` checks that a member of that package and a call follow, and `member_access` turns the package and the member into the builtin, so the builtins' own checks run unchanged. A package name hides as other builtins do: a local, a parameter or a declaration named `bits` takes its place, as the shadowing row of 2026-11-12 allows.
       - Errors, each at the token that is wrong:
         - "`trailing_zeros` is in the package `bits`; write `bits.trailing_zeros` after `import "std/bits"`" for a plain name, and the same for a member of another package, as in `bits.unaligned_load`;
         - "the package `bits` has no member `nothing`";
         - "`bits` is a package, so it can't be used here" for a package name without a member;
         - "`count_ones` is a function, so it can't be used here" for a member that isn't called, also on the left of `=`;
         - "unknown name `simd`" for a package that the file doesn't import, also when another file imports it;
         - "no built-in package has the path "std/fmt"" for a path in `std/` that names no package, and the old "the checker doesn't support this yet" for any other path;
         - "this package is already imported at 1:8";
         - "`mem` is already declared at a:7:4" at an import whose name a declaration in any file of the package takes, and the declaration keeps the name.
         - The builtins' own diagnostics name them with their package, as in "`bits.count_ones` takes an integer, found `bool`".
       - IR and backends: no change.
       - Translator ([translator.md](translator.md) rules 9, 10, 13, 23 to 26 and 29): the translation writes each builtin with its package and starts each chibi file with an import of each package that its code uses. In the compiler's translation, 11 of the 14 files import one to three packages, and 280 calls gain their package, 195 of them `mem.size_of`. A local or parameter named like a package that its function uses gets a trailing `_`, which renames 3 of the compiler's 40 locals and parameters named `bits`. The builtins' names left the translator's reserved names, so the lexer's own function `trailing_zeros` keeps its name.
       - Tests: every test program that calls a builtin now imports its package. The checker's error test gives each diagnostic above at its position, beside a parameter named `bits` that hides the package. A program of two files shows that an import holds only for its own file and clashes with a declaration in the other file. The parser's test parses `mem.size_of(*Node)` as a call of a field. From the same ASTs and sources, the compiler's translation for each target changes only in the 280 calls, the imports, the 3 renamed locals and parameters, the lexer's `trailing_zeros`, and one struct literal that its longer line now splits. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 44, all caught. Of 31 in the checker, the tests of programs that import caught 29, ASan 1 (a table of imports with one entry for each file), and the release-fast IR dump of asserts 1 (`assert` sent down the path of unknown names). Of 13 in the translator, `make translated` caught 11, where chibi refuses the compiler's translation, and a sample of 6 functions in 3 C files caught 8: it prints the same as clang's build of the C under ASan and UBSan, and it catches the 2 that the compiler's translation hides, a pointer difference and an overflow check without the import of their package, because each file of the compiler that has one also has another call into the same package. The other 5 use calls that the sample lacks or has only beside another call into the same package.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which calls no builtins: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 28,580 more instructions of 1,889 million, and the release-fast translated build 10,909 more, still 3.0% more than the C build.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The packages and their paths: `std/bits` for the bit counts, the overflow checks and `mul_high`, as Go's `math/bits` holds its bit counts and 128-bit multiply; `std/mem` for the unaligned loads and stores, the sizes and the alignments, as Zig's `std.mem` holds `readInt` and `writeInt`; and `std/simd` for the table lookup and the lane mask, where the mask functions of arrays as vectors will go.
         - `assert`, `target_os` and `target_arch` stay predeclared, as Go keeps `len` and `panic`.
         - An import holds for its own file, as in Go and Odin, may sit anywhere at the top level, and is no error when the file doesn't use it, unlike Go.
         - When a declaration takes a package's name, the error goes on the import, and the declaration keeps the name.
         - A path outside `std/` stays unsupported until chibi has packages of its own code.
         - The error for a plain name says which package holds it and how to import it, because code written before this step uses the plain names.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `NAME_BITS`, `NAME_MEM` and `NAME_SIMD` follow `NAME_X86_64`, so the ids of the names after them move by 3, and `NAME_LOOKUP` and `NAME_TOP_BITS` now follow `NAME_ALIGN_OF`. The checker has the new field `imports`. The test programs that the LowIR spike shares, in `test/backend_programs.c`, `test/vectors.c` and `test/c_source_test.c`, now import their packages.
       - Not built yet: an import under another name, which the parser refuses; an import inside a function body, which the checker refuses as unsupported; and the members that the owner's other answers of 2026-11-23 bring: the lane masks of arrays as vectors, and the two-result overflow checks that replace `add_overflows` and its kin once rank 12 brings tuples.
     - **3k.16 Asserts that release-fast builds assume: built 2026-11-23,** as the owner chose that day on the card of 2026-11-22 about asserts: option 1, an assert that the optimizer may assume in release-fast builds, as Zig's optimizer assumes `std.debug.assert` in its release-fast builds, in place of option 0, the removal that step 3k.12 built and the card recommended ([decisions.md](../decisions.md), the assert row of 2026-11-23).
       - What works: a release-fast build now evaluates an assert's condition, as a default build does, and lets the backend assume that it holds instead of testing it. A false condition is undefined behavior in a release-fast build, where step 3k.12's build went on as if the assert weren't there. `assert(f())` now calls `f` in every build. A default build is unchanged and traps with "assertion failed". The diagnostics of step 3k.12 are unchanged.
       - IR: a new kind, `assume`, after `assert`, which takes a `bool` and gives no value. A release-fast build writes `assume` where a default build writes `assert`: after a condition that isn't constant, and as `assume false` for `assert(false)`; `assert(true)` writes nothing in either build. The `if false` blocks of step 3k.12 are gone, so the checker no longer writes an `if` when it meets the name `assert`, and a call with errors no longer has a block to close. The C backend writes `if (!v2) __builtin_unreachable();`, which clang and GCC use as a fact. The arm64 backend, which has no optimizer, frees the condition's register and writes nothing, so it now computes conditions that it used to skip. The reference interpreter traps on a false `assume` with "assertion failed", as on `assert`, so a test program whose assumptions don't hold fails its test instead of running into undefined behavior.
       - Tests: the release-fast IR dumps of step 3k.12 now pin `assume` where they pinned `if false`, `drop` and `end`, and the dump of 5 asserts with errors shows that they leave no block behind. The reference interpreter runs 5 release-fast cases, 2 of which trap on a false assumption. The release-fast program of step 3k.11 now keeps every assumption it makes on the inputs that the tests give it, since a broken one would be undefined behavior in a native build, and gains two functions, one with an assert that has an `or`: arm64 runs them under qemu against the reference interpreter and hand-computed results, and a count of the stack stores in one of them shows that the backend frees the condition's register. The C backend writes that program release-fast with 5 `__builtin_unreachable()` and no trap of "assertion failed". The driver builds and runs it release-fast under UBSan, which reports an assumption that the C breaks as "execution reached an unreachable program point", and its default build now traps in the new function. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 10, all caught. The checker's 4, among them `assume` and `assert` swapped and a constant `assert(false)` left out, through its IR dumps; the C backend's 4, 3 through the new test and an inverted test through UBSan in the driver's test; the reference interpreter's 1 through its runs; and the arm64 backend's 1, a register not freed after `assume`, through the count of stack stores. In a first round that plant passed, because it costs spills but no wrong results.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no asserts: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. Without the test for a call with errors in `call`, which clang inlines into `walk`, checking in the C build ran 1.72 million instructions more, from where clang placed code, as in step 3k.12's first version; `call` now tests for a call of a function first, marked likely, and the C build runs 134,998 fewer instructions than before, 0.007%. The release-fast translated build runs 3.5 million fewer than at step 3k.15, 2.8% more than the C build, and 4.7 million fewer, 0.24%, than the same translation built with step 3k.12's removal. The translation has 113 asserts, 15 of them `assert(false)`. Those 15 are worth 7.7 million instructions: without them, the default arm of the arm64 backend's main `switch` lets clang assume nothing, and it keeps the range check of the `switch`'s jump table, two instructions for each IR instruction. The other 98 cost 3.0 million: 1.6 million for the facts and 1.4 million for evaluating the conditions. The C build keeps the range check too, because `NDEBUG` removes its `assert(false)`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A false assumption is undefined behavior only in a release-fast build. A default build checks every assert, as Zig's Debug and ReleaseSafe builds do.
         - `assert(false)` becomes `assume false`, which tells the C compiler that the code there can't run, as Zig's `unreachable` does in its release-fast builds. The code after it stays reachable for the checker, as in step 3k.12.
         - The arm64 backend computes the condition and drops it: it has no optimizer to use the fact, and the condition may call a function.
         - The reference interpreter traps on a false assumption, so that the tests catch a test program that breaks one.
         - The checker still doesn't narrow from a condition: after `assert(p != nil)`, `p` still needs `!!`, now in every build.
       - Hand-off: `IR_ASSUME` follows `IR_ASSERT`, so the IR kinds after it move by one, and only release-fast builds write it. The LowIR spike knows neither `assert` nor `assume`.
       - Not built yet: an assert that checks in every build, option 2 of the card, and turning assumptions off for one release-fast build.
     - **3k.17 `u128` and `i128`: built 2026-11-23,** as the owner chose that day on the card of 2026-11-21 about the high half of a 64-bit multiply: 128-bit integer types, as Zig, Rust and Odin have and as C has `unsigned __int128` on 64-bit targets, in place of step 3k.6's `mul_high`, which the card recommended ([decisions.md](../decisions.md), the `u128` row of 2026-11-23). This step builds them in the checker, the reference interpreter and the C backend. The arm64 backend refuses them until step 3k.18, and `bits.mul_high` stays until the translator writes the identifier hash with `u128`.
       - What works: `u128` and `i128` work as the other integers do: arithmetic that traps on overflow, wrapping arithmetic, division and remainder with the same traps, shifts by a count of any integer type, the bitwise operators, comparisons, `T(x)` to and from every integer and float type with range checks, `bitcast` to and from the 16-byte vectors, the bit counts and overflow checks of `std/bits`, `mem.unaligned_load` and `mem.unaligned_store`, ranges in `for`, struct fields, arrays, constant tables, globals, and the parameters and results of chibi functions. A constant holds any value that fits in 128 bits and folds with exact arithmetic, as other constants do, so `const A := bits.count_ones(u128(0xFFFF) << 100)` is 16. A release-fast build wraps, masks a shift count to 7 bits and narrows by truncation, as it does for the narrower types.
       - Refused with "the checker doesn't support this yet": a 128-bit integer as an index or a slice bound, as a pointer offset, as the value of a `switch`, as the backing type of an enum or the tag type of a union, as an argument to C's `..`, and wherever C code meets it: in the parameters and results of `extern fn`, `export fn` and C function types, and in `extern` globals, also inside a struct or an array. A chibi global may hold one.
       - The checker: `i128` follows `i64` and `u128` follows `u64` among the fixed types and the builtin type names, so each table that lists the integers lists them in order. A 128-bit constant takes two entries in its function's constant table, the low 64 bits and then the high 64 bits, both with the 128-bit type, and an operand names the first. `exact_fits` now takes widths up to 128 bits, with the big-number case in a function of its own so that the common case stays small enough to inline, and the new `exact_bits_high` gives the high 64 bits of an exact integer. A conversion whose operand is masked by a constant needs no range check only when the mask's high half is 0. The IR dump prints 128-bit constants and data in decimal. `ir_const` tests for a 128-bit type only after it grows the table, so that the function still dereferences the table before its first branch and translator rule 6 checks the pointer once, on its initializer: tested first, the type cost the translation 9 more `!!`.
       - IR: no new kinds. The integer kinds take 128-bit operands.
       - The C backend: the prelude names GCC's and clang's `__int128` as `chibi_u128` and `chibi_i128`. A constant that fits in 64 bits is a cast of a 64-bit literal, and any other is written as `(chibi_u128)HIGH << 64 | LOW`. Division checks for the 128-bit minimum divided by -1, a shift checks a 128-bit count as a 128-bit value, the bit counts work on the two 64-bit halves, range checks compare with 128-bit bounds, and a `bitcast` from a vector is a cast, because GCC reads `(chibi_u64x2){ v }` as a vector whose first lane is `v` and refuses it. A signed 128-bit multiply that checks for overflow, and `bits.mul_overflows` on `i128`, multiply the magnitudes with `__builtin_mul_overflow` on `chibi_u128` and compare the product with the limit for its sign: clang turns the signed form into a call to `__muloti4`, which compiler-rt has and libgcc lacks, so on arm64 Linux, where clang links libgcc, a program that multiplies `i128` values didn't link. The other checks take `__int128` in `__builtin_add_overflow` and its kin, and division and float conversions call `__divti3`, `__floattidf` and their kin, which both runtimes have.
       - The reference interpreter: each value has a high word beside its low word, as vectors already had, and a 128-bit instruction runs in a path of its own with `unsigned __int128`. Parameters, results and calls pass 128-bit values as they pass vectors. A signed multiply checks for overflow with the full 256-bit product, a method of its own, because LLVM turns the division test of a multiply into the same `__muloti4` call.
       - The arm64 backend refuses a function with a 128-bit integer, with "the arm64 backend supports only integers up to 64 bits, floats, bool, pointers, arrays, slices and vectors so far".
       - Translator ([translator.md](translator.md) rule 10): `i128` and `u128` join the reserved names, so a C name `u128` gets a trailing `_`. The compiler's C has none, so its translation changes only where the C changed. A translator sample with a parameter named `u128` and a local named `i128` fails if either name leaves the reserved names.
       - Tests: the checker's error test gives each refusal above and each constant out of range at its position. A declarations test prints 128-bit constant tables, globals and struct fields, and a body test dumps the IR of folds, of conversions from an enum and of operations with two-entry constants. The reference interpreter runs a program of 26 functions on 108 inputs, whose results a Python script computed, with exact rounding for floats: among them the full products of 64-bit values, 128-bit division and remainder, conversions to and from `f32` and `f64`, the bit counts, products at the limits of `i128`, and a loop over a `u128` range. Two programs XOR a value with 40 128-bit constants, one of them after one more constant, so that in one of them a 128-bit constant starts at the last free entry of the constant table. A release-fast program of 8 functions pins wrapping conversions and masked shift counts in its IR dump and runs on 16 inputs. The C backend's grid gains a program of 20 functions, now 371 functions, that run at 288 points each against the reference interpreter under UBSan. The grid's C gives the same 5,760 results with clang and GCC, on x86-64 and on arm64 under qemu, and a Python model of the 20 functions agrees with all of them; the C of the signed multiply also agrees with the compilers' own overflow test on 2 million inputs. A text test pins that a bitcast between a vector and a 128-bit integer is a cast and that a signed 128-bit multiply doesn't reach `__builtin_mul_overflow` with signed operands. The arm64 test refuses a function that computes with `u128` and one that takes an `i128`. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets. The first `make test` on arm64 found the `__muloti4` call, in the reference interpreter.
       - Planted bugs: 85, all caught. Of 29 in the checker, its IR dumps and error test caught 23, the interpreter's runs 2, UBSan 2 and ASan 2. Of 6 in the exact numbers, the checker's tests caught all 6. Of 22 in the C backend, the grid caught 17, UBSan 2 and the text test 3, among them the two that send a signed multiply back to `__builtin_mul_overflow`, which x86-64 can't catch at run time. Of 28 in the reference interpreter, its runs caught 20, the grid 6, the vector runs 1 and UBSan 1. In earlier rounds three passed: the bitcast through a vector compound literal, because clang accepts both forms, which the text test now catches; a 128-bit constant counted as one entry of the constant table, from which the loop over a `u128` range came; and a table that grows too late for a 128-bit constant that starts at its last free entry, which the two programs of 40 constants now catch under ASan.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no 128-bit integers: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 16.5 million fewer instructions, 0.88%: `operand_ir` now ends in a call to `ir_const_128` for a 128-bit constant, and clang now tests whether the operand is a constant before it saves registers, so an operand that isn't constant skips about 12 instructions. The release-fast translated build runs 1.2 million fewer, 0.06%, and 3.7% more than the C build, against 2.8% at step 3k.16, because the translation's `operand_ir` doesn't change this way. The translation has 775 `!!`, 4 more, all in code that the corpus doesn't run.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The names `u128` and `i128`, as Zig, Rust and Odin spell them.
         - No 128-bit integer crosses into C until the arm64 backend passes them, although the C backend could pass them now.
         - A 128-bit integer can't index, offset a pointer, select a `switch` arm, back an enum or tag a union. An index or an offset never needs more than 64 bits, and the others wait for a program that needs them.
         - A shift takes a count of any integer type, which a default build checks against 128 and a release-fast build masks to 7 bits.
         - The C backend relies on `__int128`, which GCC and clang have on every 64-bit target that chibi's C supports.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kinds. `TYPE_ID_I128` follows `TYPE_ID_I64` and `TYPE_ID_U128` follows `TYPE_ID_U64`, so the ids of `u8` to `u64` move by 1 and those after `u64` by 2; `NAME_I128` and `NAME_U128` do the same among the names, so the ids of the names after `NAME_U64` move by 2. A 128-bit constant takes two entries of the constant table, low then high, and `check_is_int128` in `src/check.h` tells such a type. Chibi globals and constant data may hold 128-bit integers, 16 bytes aligned to 16.
       - Not built yet: 128-bit integers in the arm64 backend (step 3k.18), the identifier hash written with `u128` in place of `bits.mul_high`, and 128-bit integers in C interfaces, as indexes, as `switch` values, as enum backing types and as C variadic arguments.
     - **3k.18 `u128` and `i128` in the arm64 backend: built 2026-11-23,** the rest of the `u128` row of 2026-11-23 apart from the identifier hash: the arm64 backend compiles every use of the 128-bit integers that the checker accepts, so the translator can now write `src/names.h`'s identifier hash with `u128`, and `bits.mul_high` can go.
       - What works: all that step 3k.17 built in the C backend, with the release-fast forms: arithmetic that traps on overflow, wrapping arithmetic, division and remainder with their traps, shifts by a count of any integer type, the bitwise operators, comparisons, `T(x)` to and from every integer and float type with range checks, `bitcast` to and from the 16-byte vectors, the bit counts and overflow checks of `std/bits`, loads and stores, struct fields, arrays, constant tables, globals, and the parameters and results of chibi functions. A conditional whose value is a vector or a 128-bit integer works too; before this step, the backend's assert stopped at one, and the reference interpreter refused it.
       - Where a value lives: as a vector does, in a 16-byte home on the stack, or in `v29` when the next instruction reads it. The bitwise operators, loads, stores, moves, `bitcast` and conditionals use the vector instructions. Arithmetic loads the halves into `x16`, `x17`, `x8` and free registers, and writes the result's halves to a new home with one `stp`.
       - Calls: a 128-bit argument or result takes one `q` register, as a vector does, or 16 bytes aligned to 16 on the stack once the 8 `q` registers are used. The checker's counts of stack arguments and of the registers that a call uses now count it so. AAPCS64 passes `__int128` in a pair of `x` registers, but no 128-bit value crosses into C yet (step 3k.17), so only chibi functions see this convention.
