       - Division and remainder, and a signed multiply that checks for overflow, call a routine that the backend writes once in each function that needs it, after its `ret`, and calls with `bl`. The caller passes the addresses of the operands' homes in `x16` and `x17` and of the result's home in `x8`, whose low 2 bits give the mode, remainder and signed, since homes are aligned to 16. The routine saves the registers that it uses, `x0` to `x7`, `x9` and `x10`, so the caller's values stay in their registers across the call. Division uses `udiv` when both high halves are 0, and otherwise 128 steps of shift and subtract; after k steps the remainder is less than 2^k, so it never needs a 129th bit. The signed multiply sets the flags to NE when the product overflows, and the caller traps or gives `bits.mul_overflows`'s result. An unsigned multiply that checks for overflow is inline, and a wrapping multiply is 5 instructions.
       - Conversions to a float convert the low half with `ucvtf` when the high half is 0. Otherwise they shift the value left until its top bit is set, fold the bits below the top 64 into one sticky bit, convert the top 64 bits, and multiply by a power of two, so the result rounds once, as C's does. Conversions from a float check the range, take the high half from the float times 2^-64 and the low half from the float minus the high half times 2^64, which are exact, and negate the result for a negative `i128`.
       - Tests: the reference interpreter's 128-bit program, now 42 functions on 174 inputs whose results a Python script computed, runs on arm64 under qemu with the same results, and so do the release-fast program and the two programs of 40 constants. The C backend's grid of 20 functions moves to the programs that every backend runs, and the arm64 backend agrees with the reference interpreter at its 288 points each. A program of 20 functions tests the shapes, 14 of them at the grid's points and 11 on 34 inputs with results from Python: division and products in leaf functions with many locals, 128-bit values around calls, homes more than 512 bytes from `sp`, ten 128-bit arguments with two on the stack, a 128-bit argument after 8 floats and between integers, a struct with a 128-bit field, conditionals and switches whose value is a vector or a 128-bit integer, and a chain of float conversions. Counts of `bl` show one call for each division, remainder and signed multiply, and counts of `mov` show that a leaf function's integer parameters stay in their registers after a 128-bit parameter. The arm64 test no longer refuses `u128`. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - The reference interpreter: a shift by a 128-bit count whose high half isn't 0 now traps, as the C backend's does, also in vector lanes; it read only the low half. A conditional whose value is a vector or a 128-bit integer runs.
       - Planted bugs: 113, all caught. Of 106 in the arm64 backend, the 128-bit program's runs caught 84, the release-fast program's runs 2, the grid 8, the shapes' results from Python 5, the shapes at the grid's points 4, the counts of `mov` 1, the backend's assert 1, an `and` sent to the arithmetic of halves, and an earlier test 1, which stopped at a trap from a range check that refused almost every float. The 2 in the checker, a 128-bit argument counted as an integer in `note_stack_args` or in `call_general`, stop at the backend's assert that a call's stack arguments fit in the function's count. Of 5 in the reference interpreter, the shapes at the grid's points caught 3 and its own runs 2. In a first round of 108, 15 passed. 13 now fail the tests added for them, among them products whose two high halves aren't 0, the carries and limits of the signed multiply, a shift by a parameter's count, `sxtw` of a negative `i32` and the `mov`s of a leaf function's parameters. 2 were in code that no input reaches, which is gone: a jump past the `else` of a conditional whose value is a vector, which always has an `else`, and a 129th bit of the remainder in division. A later plant in the switch arm of such a conditional's end passed too, because the checker makes a switch with a value into a block, so that arm is now an assert. In the last round, a missing check of a negative value converted to `u128` stopped only at the test's 30-second alarm, because a loop up to `u128(-1)` ran on; `to_unsigned` now traps first.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no 128-bit integers: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 3.0 million fewer instructions, 0.16%, all in the arm64 backend. As first built, it ran 10.0 million more: clang no longer inlined the range check of a conversion from a float, which `narrow` now shares with the conversion to a 128-bit integer (4.5 million), and clang kept less of `emit_function`'s main loop in registers, which cost two more memory accesses for each IR instruction (5.5 million). `check_float_range` is now `ALWAYS_INLINE`, and the main loop reads the function's count and first instruction into locals once, where clang reloaded them through `fn` after every store through the code cursor. That change alone saves 1.8 million at the parent commit. The release-fast translated compiler runs 4.5 million fewer instructions, 0.23%, also all in the arm64 backend, and 3.6% more than the C build, against 3.7% before. Clang stopped inlining `save_for_call` into the translation's `emit_function`, which cost 2.1 million, so `save_for_call` is `ALWAYS_INLINE` too; the C build already inlined it. The translation has 775 `!!`, as before.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - 128-bit arguments and results go in `q` registers between chibi functions, not in AAPCS64's pairs of `x` registers. A 128-bit value that crosses into C must use the pairs.
         - Division and the checked signed multiply are routines inside each function that uses them, so the backend needs no shared runtime code. They take 64 and 51 words of code in each such function.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kinds. The checker's `note_stack_args` and `call_general` count a 128-bit argument as a vector: one `q` register, or 16 bytes aligned to 16 on the stack.
       - Not built yet: the identifier hash written with `u128` in place of `bits.mul_high`, which is next, and what step 3k.17 refuses.
     - **3k.19 `bits.mul_high` removed: built 2026-11-23,** the last part of the `u128` row of 2026-11-23. The translator now writes `src/names.h`'s identifier hash with `u128` ([translator.md](translator.md), the entry for step 3k.19), so step 3k.6's `bits.mul_high(a, b)` goes, with its name, its diagnostic and the IR kind `mul-high`. A program writes it as `u64(u128(a) * u128(b) >> 64)`; the product of two `u64` always fits in `u128`, so `*` can't trap.
       - The mask rule for 128-bit values: `T(x & mask)` emits `truncate` when the mask fits `T`, but for a 128-bit `x` the checker read the mask's low 64 bits as a signed number, so a mask such as `0xFFFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF` counted as negative, and `u64(x & 0xFFFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF)` emitted a checked `narrow`, which step 3k.17's test pinned. The translator writes the C's narrowings as `T(x & mask)` (rule 3), and the hash's two halves are two of them. A mask on a 128-bit value now fits when its high 64 bits are 0 and its low 64 bits fit `T`. On a narrower value, a mask with its top bit set still doesn't fit, because the value may be negative.
       - Tests: the five constants of `bits.mul_high` are now written with `u128` and fold to the same values. `mh_grid` and `mh_hash` stay among the programs that every backend runs, written with `u128`; `mh_hash` is the hash as the translator writes it, and the arm64 backend agrees with C's `unsigned __int128` on 24 by 24 inputs and at the extremes. An IR test shows `u64(x & 0xFFFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF)` and `int(x & 0x7FFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF)` as `truncate` and `int(x & 0xFFFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF)` as `narrow`. A new lexer test checks that the hash stored for every name equals `names_hash` in C, so `make translated` now checks the translated hash against the C one; before, a wrong translation would only have slowed the name table. The token dump's ids after `count_ones` move down by one. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 9 in the mask rule, all caught: 8 by the IR tests and 1, which tests `bits > 64` in place of `bits >= 64`, by UBSan as a shift by 64. In a first round, the plant that drops the sign test of a mask on a narrower value passed. Only a signed value converted to `u64` reaches that test, and no test had one, so an IR test now shows `u64(x & -2)` for an `int` `x` as `narrow` and `u64(x & 0x7FFF_FFFF_FFFF_FFFF)` as `truncate`.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no `mul_high`: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 0.6 million fewer instructions, 0.03%, 0.5 million of them in `result`, which step 3k.6's new kind had made 0.5 million slower: `IR_CALL`'s number is odd again. The release-fast translated compiler runs 4.1 million fewer, 0.21%, 4.0 million of them in the lexer's `intern_names`, which now computes one 128-bit product where `a *% b ^ bits.mul_high(a, b)` made clang compute two, the cost that step 3t.7 measured in C. It runs 3.4% more instructions than the C build, against 3.6% before.
       - The arm64 backend writes the hash in 40 instructions, against 3 for `a *% b ^ bits.mul_high(a, b)`: a 128-bit value lives in a 16-byte home, and the backend doesn't know that the high halves of `u128(a)` and `u128(b)` are 0. No build of the compiler uses the arm64 backend yet; the C and translated builds go through clang, which writes one `mul` on x86-64, and `mul` and `umulh` on arm64, as before.
       - Hand-off: `mul-high` is gone, so every IR kind from `shl` on moves down by one, and so does every builtin name after `count_ones`. The LowIR spike's `lowir.c` loses its `mul-high` case in the same commit.
       - Not built yet: a 128-bit product of two widened 64-bit values as `mul` and `umulh` in the arm64 backend, and what step 3k.17 refuses.
     - **3k.20 Character literals: built 2026-11-23,** with option A of sheet 4c ([syntax-options-effects.md](syntax-options-effects.md) 4c), which the owner adopted on 2026-11-23 with strings and the conditional expression (the strings row of 2026-11-23). `'a'` is an untyped integer constant, the code point of the one character it holds, so `c - '0'`, `c == '\n'` and `b: u8 = 'A'` need no conversion, and `e: u8 = '€'` gives "8364 does not fit in `u8`", as `8364` would.
       - Escapes: strings and character literals share them, and the set gains C's `\0`, `\xNN` and `\'`, as sheet 4c recommends. `\0` is the byte 0, and a digit after it gives "a digit can't follow `\0`; use `\x00`", so `"\012"` can't mean C's octal. `\xNN` takes exactly two hex digits, where C takes all that follow, and gives one byte in a string, so `"\xFF"` holds the byte 0xFF where `"ÿ"` holds the two bytes of its UTF-8. `\'` is an apostrophe, which a string may also hold as written. In a character literal, `\xNN` gives NN, and `\uXXXX` or a surrogate pair gives the code point.
       - What a literal holds: one byte below 0x80, one escape, or one UTF-8 sequence. `''` and `'ab'` give "a character literal holds one character". Bytes that aren't one valid UTF-8 sequence, such as a stray continuation byte, a cut or overlong sequence, an encoded surrogate or a value above U+10FFFF, give "a character literal must be valid UTF-8", and half of a surrogate pair gives the strings' error.
       - Lexer: the block lexer resolves `'…'` spans in the loop that resolves comments, as sheet 4c proposed, so `'"'` doesn't open a string and `'\n'` doesn't put a backslash outside a literal. A table lookup per 16 bytes, which shares its low-nibble mask with the whitespace lookup, marks the blocks that hold an apostrophe or a backslash. Only those blocks go to `rare_literals`, out of line, which builds the exact masks; every other block runs the code it ran before, without the backslash test. A character literal that crosses a block or meets a line end goes to the scalar path, as a string does. The token kind `CHAR` follows `STRING`, and the node kind `char` follows `string`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `c := 'a'` gives `c` the type that `c := 97` gives (sheet 4c's question 4).
         - `\uXXXX` stays, with surrogate pairs above U+FFFF; `\u{…}` isn't built (sheet 4c's question 3).
         - `\xNN` takes exactly two digits, and `\0` refuses a digit after it instead of reading C's octal.
         - A literal may hold a raw tab or another control byte, as a string may.
       - IR: no new kind. A character literal is an exact integer, so the IR and the declarations dump show its number.
       - Tests: 20 new lexer edge cases, each at 128 offsets, against the reference lexer, which now reads `'…'` and the new escapes: character literals beside strings and comments, `'"'`, `'//'`, apostrophes inside strings and comments, a run of 22 literals across a block boundary, a literal and a string longer than a block, and each way that a literal can fail. The random programs' pieces gain 10 character literals, `"don't"`, a string with the new escapes, and a comment with an apostrophe. 11 lexer errors check their messages and positions. In the checker, 11 character constants and a string with each new escape fold to hand-computed values, among them the highest code points of two, three and four UTF-8 bytes. 17 diagnostics cover empty and long literals, each way that UTF-8 can be broken, broken surrogate pairs, and constants that don't fit their type. An IR test shows `c >= '0' and c <= '9'` and `c - '0'` on a `u8` with their numbers. `hello.chibi` gains `initial := 'G'`, which the token dump shows as `CHAR`. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 41 in the lexer and the checker, 36 caught: 21 by the lexer's edge cases, 13 by the checker's cases, 1 by the parser tests, whose source no longer lexed, and 1 by ASan. 5 give the same output. 3 of them only send more blocks to the scalar path, which the tests can't see: an apostrophe left in a comment that continues from the last block, a literal's span without its closing quote, and `\'` missing from the block lexer's table of escapes. On the translated compiler's sources, the span costs the lexer 0.22% more instructions, and the others less. `escape_len` that steps 3 bytes over `\xNN` scans the last hex digit as a plain byte, and a UTF-8 lead of `0xC0` or `0xC1` gives a value below 0x80, which the overlong check refuses anyway. Three tests came from planning the plants: a second byte that isn't a continuation byte but has its top bit set (`'\xc3\xc3'`), two low surrogates (`'\uDC00\uDC00'`), and a continuation byte as the first byte (`'\xbf\xbf'`). The overlong tests use the highest overlong values, `E0 9F BF` and `F0 8F BF BF`, so that one bound for all lengths fails them. The translator's 7 plants are in its entry in [translator.md](translator.md).
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no character literals, apostrophes or backslashes: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 2.0 million fewer instructions, 0.11%. The lexer runs 2.9 million fewer, because its main path no longer tests for backslashes, and `walk` 0.9 million more, because one new node kind changes the parity of the kinds after it; with a second, unused kind, `walk` runs 0.2 million fewer instead. As first built, with the apostrophe mask and its loop on the main path, the lexer ran 15.1 million more, 0.81%. On the translated compiler's own sources, 1.9 MB with 286 lines of character literals, the lexer of this step runs 2.9% fewer instructions than that first version. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 2.2 million fewer instructions on the corpus than before, 0.11%, and still 3.4% more than the C build.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `TK_CHAR` follows `TK_STRING` and `NODE_CHAR` follows `NODE_STRING`, so the token kinds from `TK_IDENT` on and the node kinds from `NODE_TRUE` on move up by one, and `DIAG_CHAR_COUNT` and `DIAG_CHAR_UTF8` follow `DIAG_SURROGATE`. The test programs that the LowIR spike shares don't change.
       - Not built yet: `string` and `cstring`, after arrays as vectors.
     - **3k.21 Arrays as parameters and results: built 2026-11-23,** the first part of arrays as vectors (the vectors row of 2026-11-23), where today's `u8x16` parameters become `[16]u8`. An array travels as a struct that holds its elements does since 3d.6 and 3d.8, under rank 8's copies: up to 16 bytes in one or two integer registers, one to four `f32` or `f64` in float registers, and anything larger as the address of a copy, which the caller skips when nothing the callee can reach can change the original; a large result comes back through memory that the caller provides. The checker's paths for struct arguments, results and returns now take arrays too, so an array argument is a `load-at [N]T` of its address, with its copy root when it goes by address, an array result goes to a hidden local after the arguments, as in `call [3]int make(1) -> $5`, and `ret [N]T` takes an address. The reference interpreter copies an array parameter on entry and writes an array result into the caller's slot, as it does for structs. The C backend wraps each array type that a signature uses in a struct, `struct chibi_<prefix>array_<type>`, because C can't pass an array by value, and copies through it as it copies structs. The arm64 backend needed no change: it already kept arrays in memory, and `check_struct_regs` already counted the floats inside arrays.
       - The rules are the ones for structs: a parameter's elements can't be assigned, `ok(a)[0] = 5` gives "a call's result can't be assigned", `&f(x)[i]` points into the hidden local, `extern fn` and `export fn` still refuse arrays because C has no array values, an empty array can't be passed yet, as an empty struct can't, and a labeled block or a `switch` can't give an array, as it can't give a struct.
       - Tests: an IR test passes arrays where 3d.8's test passes structs, `take(s: [3]int, t: [3]int)` and `make(a: int) -> [3]int`, called with a local, a struct field, `p.*`, a call's result and a `for` variable, and pins the same roots and the same copy area, 48 bytes. A second IR test passes `[2]u8` in a register through `if c then a else b` and indexes a call's result. A diagnostics test assigns into array results, as the one for structs does, and the test that refused array parameters now checks them. The backends share a new program of 35 functions, `ARRAY_ARGS`. On arm64 under qemu, C calls 12 of them with structs that wrap the arrays, so the arrays go where AAPCS64 puts such structs: `[3]u8` in one integer register, `[11]u8` in two, `[4]f32` and `[2]f64` in float registers, `[5]int` by address with its result through `x8`, `[2]AP` of structs, `[2][3]u8`, a mix of all of these, arrays on the stack after 8 integer arguments, and `[11]u8` and `[2]f64` when one register of their kind is left, which sends them and every later argument of that kind to the stack. 15 functions of two integers agree with the reference interpreter on a grid of inputs, on arm64 and through the C backend: small arrays rotated twice, chains and nests of large results, a callee that changes a local copy of its argument, one array passed twice, a peer of a conditional, recursion, stack arguments, floats, arrays of structs, a struct field set from a call, a parameter type and a result type that no other function has, an inferred result with two returns, and two arguments whose original changes during the call, through a pointer argument and through a global. The shared 128-bit program gains one more, which passes `[1]u128` in two registers and `[2]i128` and `[3]u128` by address. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 24, in the checker, the C backend and the reference interpreter, 23 caught: 13 by the C backend's grid, 5 by the checker's IR and diagnostics tests, 3 by ASan, 1 by the checker's assert and 1, which miscounts an array's registers in the stack area that a call needs, by the arm64 backend's assert under qemu. The 24th gives the same program, with each array result also declared as an unused C local. Three tests came from survivors: the inferred result with two returns, the parameter type that no function returns, and the calls where an array needs two registers and one is left.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which passes no arrays: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte, and the C build runs as many instructions as before, 1,866.9 million. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler also runs as many as before, 1,930.5 million, still 3.4% more than the C build.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, but arrays take the shapes that structs have: `load-at [N]T` as a call's argument, with a root when it goes by address; an array result in a hidden local after the arguments, with no value; `ret [N]T` of an address; and parameter slots of array type. Of the test programs that the LowIR spike shares, only the 128-bit program changes, which the spike skips; with its new function the grid has 372 functions of two integers. `ARRAY_ARGS` stays out of `BACKEND_PROGRAMS` until the spike passes arrays.
       - Not built yet: the rest of arrays as vectors (3k.22): lane operators on arrays of numbers, lane masks from `std/simd`, and the end of the eight vector names.
     - **3k.22 Arrays in place of the vector types: built 2026-11-23,** the rest of arrays as vectors (the vectors row of 2026-11-23). The eight predeclared vector types, such as `u8x16`, are gone. The arrays with the same lanes, `[16]u8`, `[16]i8`, `[8]u16`, `[8]i16`, `[4]u32`, `[4]i32`, `[2]u64` and `[2]i64`, take their operators: `+%`, `-%`, `*%`, `&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, unary `-%` and shifts by a scalar work lane by lane, and a scalar beside an array stands for every lane, as in `v | 0x20`. Lane masks come from six functions in `std/simd`: `simd.eq`, `simd.ne`, `simd.lt`, `simd.le`, `simd.gt` and `simd.ge` take two arrays of one type, or an array and a scalar on either side, and give an array of that type with each lane all ones or zero. `mem.unaligned_load([16]u8, p)`, `mem.unaligned_store`, `simd.lookup`, `simd.top_bits` and `bitcast` take the arrays where they took vectors. The vector types stay inside the checker as the types of IR values, so the IR has no new kind: an array of lanes lives in memory, as the card said it would, an operator reads it with one `load-at u8x16`, and a result goes into its array with one `store-at u8x16`, straight into the variable, element, struct field or conditional that receives it, or into a hidden local where an array is needed, such as a call's argument or a `return`. Diagnostics write the vector types as arrays, and the IR dump keeps `u8x16`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` on an array of lanes give "`[16]u8` doesn't compare lane by lane with `==`; use `simd.eq`", because `==` on two arrays gives one `bool` (the Matrices row of 2026-11-10). That `==` isn't built yet, so on any other array it gives "the checker doesn't support this yet". The checked `+`, `-`, `*` and unary `-` give "`[16]u8` supports only the wrapping `+%`, `-%` and `*%` so far", as they did on vectors, and `!` gives "expected `bool`, found `[16]u8`".
         - On other arrays of numbers, such as `[3]int`, `[4]f32` or `[32]u8`, the lane operators give "the checker doesn't support this yet", because the backends have no lanes for them yet. On arrays of anything else and on structs, they give "`[2]bool` doesn't support `+`", and `!` on any other array gives "`[3]u8` doesn't support `!`".
         - An array of lanes that an operator, `simd.eq` and its siblings or `simd.lookup` reads is read where it is written, as step 3d.7 reads literals' items: when a call after it can change it, its load goes into the four `nop`s that the checker reserves before the call. So `v +% bump_first(&v)[1]` adds `v` as it was before `bump_first` ran.
         - A conditional, a labeled block or a `switch` whose value is an array of lanes carries a vector, so `q: [2]u64 = p: { if a > 3 { break p w *% 3 }; break p 9 }` splats `9`, and a branch that names an array loads it. A branch whose array has other lanes than the other branch gives "`[8]u16` and `[16]u8` don't mix without a conversion". A function without `->` whose `return`s give an array of lanes returns that array, as in `fn twice(v: [16]u8) { return v +% v }`.
         - An array literal whose items are all number constants is now one `data` block and one `copy`, `%0 = data *[2]int .[1, 2]` then `copy [2]int %1, %0`, at any length, where it was one store per item. `u8.[32, 0, …]` replaces step 3i.3's vector literal, and a literal must still have one item for each lane. Each function of the vector grid has a 16-item table, which now takes a copy in place of 64 instructions, so the grid's arm64 code is 22% shorter.
         - An array of lanes passes and returns as step 3k.21's arrays do, in two integer registers. Step 3i.6's vector parameters and results in V0 to V7 can no longer be reached. The arm64 backend's code for them stays, because 128-bit integers pass through it (step 3k.18), and step 3t.17 removed the reference interpreter's. Calls that pass arrays of lanes grew: `call_spread`, which passes ten, takes 121 instructions on arm64 in place of 77, and the vector program of the shared tests 5% more.
         - Not supported yet, each with "the checker doesn't support this yet": a constant or a global of an array of lanes set from a scalar, `const C: [16]u8 = 5` (a constant from a full literal works); conversions between arrays of lanes and from a scalar, `[8]u16(a)` and `[16]u8(y)`, of which `bitcast` covers the first; indexing an operator's result, `(a | y)[0]`, which needs a local first; and an inferred result whose `return`s mix a constant with an array of lanes.
         - The names `u8x16` and the seven others are free for user code, and the translator no longer renames C names that match them.
       - The arm64 backend gives a 16-byte array of integers a 16-byte-aligned slot, so one `ldr q` or `str q` with an offset reaches it. An `addr` of a local whose next instruction loads or stores it as a vector emits nothing, and that load or store uses the slot's offset. A vector stays in V29 when the next instruction that reads it follows `addr`, `field` or `index` instructions, as in a store into an element. The C backend and the reference interpreter didn't change.
       - Tests: every test that named a vector type now uses arrays and `simd.eq` and its siblings, among them the vector programs that the reference interpreter, the arm64 backend under qemu and the C backend share, the grid of 8 functions with 20 operators each, and step 3i.6's calls from C, which now pass `[16]u8` inside a struct, in two integer registers as AAPCS64 passes such a struct; the 18 checks of the registers that vector calls used are gone. The shared program `bit_ops` writes `bitcast([2]u64, n)`. The IR tests show 16 constant literals as `data` and `copy` where they showed stores. The shared vector program gains 4 functions with 17 hand-computed runs: `either`, conditionals that give arrays and operators' results; `held`, a conditional, a labeled block and a `switch` typed `[16]u8` whose branches name arrays; `early`, an array read by an operator, by `simd.eq` and as `simd.lookup`'s table before a call that changes it; and `rows`, `+%=`, `^=` and `=` on elements of a `[2][16]u8`. A new IR test pins `+%=` through an element with its hidden pointer, `=` of a whole array as one `copy`, a struct field stored from an operator's result, an inferred `[16]u8` result and an empty literal. Diagnostics tests cover each message above. On arm64, the code-shape tests of the vector program keep every count of `ldr q`, `str q` and the other instructions that they check, and its code is 7 instructions shorter than with vector locals. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 52 in the checker and the arm64 backend. A first round caught 27, 22 passed, and 3 didn't compile until rewritten. For the survivors, the shared vector program got `held`, `early` and `rows`, the checker got the new IR and diagnostics tests, and the arm64 tests got two: `one_row`, whose operator result goes into an element of a `[1][16]u8` with no spill, and `bytes_mask`, moved to be the first function that the backend compiles, where a fold marker left at its first value, 0, would take the load from `%0` for a folded `addr`. They catch 15 of the 22, and the 3 rewritten plants are caught too, one of them only after the IR test gained `r[0] = x`, a whole array assigned as one `copy`. 2 survivors showed dead code, which is gone: a load before `+%=` that the operator makes anyway, and a test for places among a literal's constant items, which conversion refuses anyway. 5 can't change what the compiler does: the right operand of a binary operator and the operand of a unary one never find a reserved load, because nothing runs between them and their operator, and every vector load or store after an `addr` reads that `addr`, and each value is used once, so the fold's test of its operand and its resets after the load and the store never decide anything. All 8 bugs planted in the translator fail its new sample ([translator.md](translator.md)).
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no vectors and no array literals: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 1.0 million fewer instructions, 0.05%, 0.5 million of them in `emit_function` and 0.4 million in `binary`. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 6.6 million more, 0.34%, and 3.8% more than the C build, against 3.4% before. The lexer's loop over 64-byte blocks runs 26 more instructions per block, 6.8 million in all: 17 more copies between vector registers, 4 more spills, 6 more reloads and one more `pxor`, against 2 fewer loads of constants. Every other kind of instruction in the loop runs as often as before. The translated lexer's `[16]u8` helpers now take and give their arrays through hidden locals and a struct, which clang turns back into vectors, but clang then allocates the loop's vector registers differently. With the hidden locals typed as vectors in the translated C, or with a vector field in the struct, the loop is the same, instruction for instruction. The translation has 784 `!!`, 5 more, all in checker code that the corpus doesn't reach: 6 in the new `load_lanes`, on the body's code, and one each in `fill_from_data` and `materialize`, against 3 in code that left.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind, but the IR that vectors make changed shape. A local of an array of lanes is an `addr` and `load-at u8x16` or `store-at u8x16` of an array slot, where a vector local was a slot of its own; a constant array literal of numbers is `data` and `copy`; and no call passes or returns a vector. `DIAG_LANE_COMPARE` follows `DIAG_WRAPPING_ONLY` and `DIAG_LANE_COUNT` is gone, and among the names, the eight vector types are gone and `NAME_EQ` to `NAME_GE` follow `NAME_TOP_BITS`. Of the test programs that the LowIR spike shares, `bit_ops` writes `bitcast([2]u64, n)`.
       - Not built yet: `string` and `cstring`, next, and what the rules above leave unsupported.
     - **3k.23 `string` and `cstring`: built 2026-11-23,** the strings row of 2026-11-23, which adopted a read-only `string` view beside `cstring`. `string` is a predeclared type laid out as `[]u8`, a pointer and a length in 16 bytes, and it travels as a slice does. `x := "text"` now gives a `string`, where it asked for a type, and a literal converts to `string`, `cstring`, `*u8` and `[*]u8`. `s[i]` reads a `u8` with a bounds check, `s[a..b]` gives a `string`, `for c in s` reads its bytes, and `s.len` and `s.ptr` work as on slices. A `[]u8` converts to `string` without a cast, and so does a `[N]u8` variable, as a view of its bytes: nothing is copied, so a write to the array shows through the view. `cstring` is a predeclared pointer to bytes that end in a NUL, laid out as `[*]u8`, for C: `extern fn puts(s: cstring) -> i32` takes a literal, a `cstring` converts to `[*]u8` without a cast, and `cstring(p)` makes one from a `[*]u8`, which states that its bytes end in a NUL. Globals, constant tables and struct fields hold strings, as in `names: [3]string = .["a", "bb", S]`, with each literal's address and length in the data. The IR has no new kind: a `string` is a slice in the IR, so `s := "hello"` is `%0 = data [*]u8 "hello"`, `store string s, %0` and `store int s.len, 5`, and a `cstring` is a pointer, `%0 = data cstring "hi"`. The backends didn't change: the C backend writes a `string` as its slice struct and a `cstring` as `uint8_t *`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - A `string` is read-only. `s[0] = 1`, `s[1] += 2`, `&s[2]` and a conversion to `[]u8`, as in `y: []u8 = s` or `[]u8(s[1..])`, give "a `string` is read-only, so its bytes can't be assigned or pointed to". `s.ptr` gives a `[*]u8` that can write, as the one way out, much as a cast is.
         - `==` and `!=` on strings, with a literal on either side, give "the checker doesn't support this yet": they will compare the bytes, which needs a loop that isn't built. `s == nil` gives "`string` is never nil", and the other operators give "`string` doesn't support `+`".
         - A `cstring` can't be indexed, sliced or compared, with "`cstring` can't be indexed" and "`cstring` doesn't support `==`", and `c + 1` gives "`untyped integer` and `cstring` don't mix without a conversion": to walk its bytes, convert it to `[*]u8` first. `?cstring` holds `nil`, for C functions that return NULL. A `[*]u8` needs `cstring(p)`, and a `*[4]u8`, a `*u8`, a `[*]u16` or a `string` doesn't convert to `cstring`, because nothing says that its bytes end in a NUL. A `cstring` doesn't convert to `string`, because only a scan finds its length.
         - An `extern fn` or an `export fn` can't take or return a `string`, as for other slices: "C has no `string`, so an `extern fn` can't pass or return it". C's `..` refuses one too: "C's `..` takes numbers, `bool`, enums and pointers, not `string`". A literal still passes `..` as a pointer to its bytes.
         - Not supported yet, each with "the checker doesn't support this yet": a conditional, a labeled block or a `switch` whose value is a `string`, as for other slices; `?string`; a typed constant, `const T: string = "typed"`, where an untyped one, `const S := "named"`, works; a field's default, `s: string = "x"`; and `S.len` of a named constant. `"abc"[0]` still gives "`untyped string` can't be indexed".
         - A function without `->` whose `return`s give literals returns a `string`.
         - The fixed types gain `TYPE_ID_STRING` and `TYPE_ID_CSTRING` after `TYPE_ID_I64X2`. The type names gain `NAME_STRING` and `NAME_CSTRING` after `NAME_ERROR`, which moves the id of every later name by 2. `DIAG_STRING_TYPE`, "a string needs a type here", is gone, and `DIAG_STRING_READ_ONLY` follows `DIAG_NOT_INDEXABLE`.
       - Tests: the checker's tests gain a declarations case, with strings in globals, in a `[3]string` table with a named constant, in a `[2]Rec` table of structs and in a struct global that leaves its string out, and with `cstring` globals; two diagnostics cases, which cover each message above at its position, among them `==` with the literal on either side and with `true`, a string value converted to `[]u8`, a `[]int` converted to `string`, `cstring` to and from `[*]u16`, a `?cstring` indexed after `!= nil`, and a conditional of two literals given to a `string`; and an IR case, with a literal, a `[]u8`, a slice of one and an array converted to `string`, `string(b)`, slices, a store into a struct field, a `cstring` literal passed to C, a `string` result, `for` over a string, `.ptr`, and `cstring(p)` and back. In step 3k.3's cases, `h := "global"`, `a := "abc"` and `dd := C` now check, and a field's default, `m := "y"`, and a conditional of two literals give "the checker doesn't support this yet". The shared programs gain `STRING_VIEWS`, 9 functions that the reference interpreter, the arm64 backend under qemu and the C backend share: indexes and slices that trap out of range, views of an array that changes after the view is made, a loop over a string from a global table, strings in struct fields, a string result passed on, and `cstring` conversions with `??` on a `?cstring`. On arm64 they get 22 runs computed by a Python oracle and a check of a global table's pointer and length, and the grid of both backends now covers 379 functions, 7 of them new. A literal `cstring` and `cstring(p)` pass to C's `atoi` in the C interop tests of both backends. The lexer's test of `test/hello.txt` expects each identifier's id 2 higher. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 39 in the checker. A first round caught 29. Of the 10 that passed, 7 got new tests: a `[]int` taken as a `string`, a string value converted to `[]u8`, `cstring` to and from `[*]u16`, two in comparisons of a string value with a literal, and a conditional of two literals given to a `string`. One, a global's length left out, is caught only by the arm64 test's hand-computed runs, and 2 showed dead code, which is gone: a test for the empty type, which no caller passes, and clearing the mark of an operator's result in `cstring(p)`, which only numbers read. The first round also showed that `"x" == s` gave "don't mix" where `s == "x"` gave "the checker doesn't support this yet", and that `s == nil` gave the second for a variable and "`string` is never nil" for a value. Now an untyped string beside a `string` takes its type on either side, and an operator reads a `string` variable as a value. A second round of 38, without the 3 plants whose code is gone and with 2 for the new code, caught all of them: 37 through the checker's tests, 3 of them as a crash or a UBSan error, and the global's length through the arm64 test. Moving the string tests off the main paths, below, rewrote 2 plants and added 5: 6 are caught, one of them after a new test that compares a `string` with `true`, and the 7th, which leaves a literal beside a `string` unconverted, can't fail until an operator on strings is built, because every one of them is refused. Both bugs planted in the translator fail its new sample ([translator.md](translator.md)).
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no `string` or `cstring`: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte, and the C build runs as many instructions as before, 1,865.8 million against 1,865.9 million. A first build ran 0.64 million more, 0.03%, from tests for strings on the main paths of `cast`, `peer_type` and `unify`; `cstring(p)` is now tested after the casts between numbers, and a literal beside a `string` after the other pairs of types that `unify` reconciles. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 1.6 million fewer instructions, 0.08%, all in the checker's `binary`, where clang compiles the inlined `applies` differently, and 3.7% more than the C build, against 3.8% before. The translation has 789 `!!`, 5 more, in code that the corpus doesn't reach: 3 where the checker writes a string literal's address and length, and 2 where the IR printer reads relocations.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind and no new IR shape: a `string` is a slice and a `cstring` a pointer, typed with their own fixed type ids. The ids above move: `TYPE_ID_STRING` and `TYPE_ID_CSTRING` are 33 and 34 and `NFIXED_TYPES` is 35, every name after `NAME_ERROR` moves by 2, and every diagnostic between `DIAG_STRING_TYPE` and `DIAG_NOT_INDEXABLE` moves down by one. The shared test programs gain `STRING_VIEWS` at the end of `BACKEND_PROGRAMS`.
       - Not built yet: tuples (3k.24) with named results, and what the rules above leave unsupported.
     - **3k.24 Tuples: built 2026-11-23,** the tuples row of 2026-11-23 (rank 12), apart from named results, which come next. A tuple type lists two or more types in parentheses, `(int, bool)`, and is an anonymous struct with C layout: the checker makes one hidden struct for each list of element types, so `(int, bool)` is the same type wherever it is written. Tuples nest and sit in arrays, struct fields, globals and constant tables, behind pointers and optionals, and they pass and return as structs do: `[2](u8, bool)`, `?*(Node, int)` inside `Node`, `fn divmod(a: int, b: int) -> (int, int)`. `.{ q, r }` builds one, and `return a, b` is short for `return .{ a, b }`, so it fills a struct result too. `q, r := f()` declares a local for each element, and `a, b = b, a` assigns: a list on the right gives one value to each target, a single value must be a tuple with one element for each target, and `_` skips one. The targets of `=` are names, fields, elements or `p.*`, as in `a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]`. Everything on the right is read before any target is written, as in Go: the targets' places are found first, left to right, then the values, then the stores go left to right, so `a, b = b, a` swaps and `k, a[k] = 2, 0` writes the `a[k]` of the old `k`. The IR has no new kind: a tuple is a struct whose fields are numbered, so the dump writes `field *u8 %2, .0`, and the C backend names a tuple's struct `chibi_<prefix>tuple_<decl>`.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - An element has no name and no index: a tuple is read by giving it to names, as in `q, _ := t`, and `t.x` gives "`(int, bool)` has no member `x`".
         - A tuple needs two or more types: `(int)`, `(int,)` and `()` give "a tuple needs two or more types".
         - A struct that holds itself through a tuple, `struct Bad { t: (Bad, int) }`, gives "`Bad` depends on itself", and a pointer breaks the cycle, as `?*(Node, int)` inside `Node` does. A tuple whose element is a struct declared later, `(Late, i8)`, is laid out when that struct is.
         - `:=` declares names only: `a.x, b := 1, 2` gives "expected a name". A target of `=` that isn't a name, a field, an element or `p.*`, such as `f()` or `x + 1`, gives "expected a name, a field, an element or `p.*`".
         - The counts must match: `x, y := 1, 2, 3`, `e, f := triple()` and `return 1, 2, 3` into a pair give "wrong number of values: expected 2, found 3". A single value that isn't a tuple, a struct too, gives "`S` is not a tuple, so it can't fill several names".
         - In a list given to `=`, each value takes the type of its target, so `s1, s2 = .{ 7, 8 }, .{ 9, 10 }` builds two `S`. A single value takes no type from its targets, and `u, v := .{ 1, 2 }` gives "the type of this literal isn't known here".
         - A struct or array on the right stays in its place until it is stored, as elsewhere. So `=` first copies one to a hidden local when an earlier target may write its place, and a value read before a call in the same list is copied before the call, as arguments are (rank 3).
         - In a switch arm without braces, a comma ends the arm, so `1 => return a, b` and `1 => q, r = r, q` need braces.
         - `==` and `!=` give "`(int, bool)` doesn't support `==`", as for structs. C can't see a tuple: an `extern fn` or `export fn` that takes or returns one gives "C has no `(int, int)`, so an `extern fn` can't pass or return it", and an `extern` global gives "an `extern` global can't hold it". A struct that C reaches may hold one, and `chibi c --layouts` checks that struct's size and offsets but not the tuple's, which has no C name.
         - A function without `->` can't `return a, b`: "the checker doesn't support this yet". `!(int, bool)` parses, as error unions of other types do, and waits for errors (3f).
         - The node kinds gain `NODE_TARGET` and `NODE_ASSIGN_TARGET` after `NODE_VAR_TYPE_INIT`, the lists `NODE_TUPLE_TYPE`, `NODE_DESTRUCTURE_INIT` and `NODE_DESTRUCTURE_ASSIGN` after `NODE_SWITCH_ITEM`, and the markers `NODE_TUPLE_START`, `NODE_TARGETS_START`, `NODE_DESTRUCTURE_VALUE` and `NODE_DESTRUCTURE_VALUES` at the end. The declaration flags gain `DECL_TUPLE`, 1024. The diagnostics gain `DIAG_TUPLE_SIZE`, `DIAG_NOT_TUPLE` and `DIAG_VALUE_COUNT` after `DIAG_FIELD_COUNT`, and `DIAG_NO_ZERO_ELEMENT` after `DIAG_NO_ZERO_FIELD`. A tuple's declaration is made while bodies are checked, so the declaration arrays now grow, and a table keyed by the element types finds the tuple again.
       - Tests: the parser's tests gain trees for tuple types, nested and behind pointers, slices and `!`, for `return a, b` with a conditional, a labeled block and a `switch` among its values, for `:=` and `=` with names, `_`, fields, elements and `p.*` as targets, and for switch arms that a comma ends; errors for an unclosed tuple type, a target that can't be assigned, `:=` with a field and a list without `:=` or `=`; and their postorders. One tree, `a, x: { break x p }.y = 1, 2`, starts a target on a token that two nodes already use, so the parser tests' check that no token serves more than two nodes covers the token of each target's node. The checker's tests gain three declarations cases: tuples in structs, globals, constant tables, parameters and results, a tuple that holds a struct declared after it, an error union of a tuple, and 8 tuples of three types beside 8 pairs with the same first types; three diagnostics cases, which cover each message above at its position; three IR cases: `:=` from a call, a tuple, a field and an element, `_`, swaps of numbers and structs, a value read before a call that writes it, globals as targets, literals that take their targets' types, fields, elements and `p.*` as targets, `k, a[k].x = 2, a[k].y + 5`, and a `return` list with a conditional, a labeled block and a `switch` among its values; and the statement positions of `:=` and `=` with several names. The shared programs gain `TUPLES`, 12 functions that the reference interpreter, the arm64 backend under qemu and the C backend share: a divmod, swaps of numbers and structs, nested tuples, tuples in globals and in constant tables with strings, a value read before a call that writes it, tuples behind pointers, in arrays and in a struct field, a tuple that holds an optional pointer to its own struct, a struct result from `return a, b`, a tuple that holds a struct declared after it, and `=` with elements and fields in Go's order. On arm64 they get 31 runs computed by a Python oracle, and the grid of both backends now covers 391 functions, 12 of them new. `chibi c --layouts` gets a struct that holds a tuple and a pointer to one. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 85, 26 in the parser, 56 in the checker and 3 in the C backend. A first round of 75, before `=` took fields and elements, caught 61, and 7 didn't compile because they left a name unused. Of the 7 that passed, 3 got new tests: a `return` list that marked only its first value, which the IR case with a conditional, a labeled block and a `switch` in the list catches; an entry left in the list of values that aren't read yet, which added 4 `nop`s after `_, _, s = 1, 2, make_s()`; and the statement position of a destructuring. A second round of 85, with 17 for the targets of `=` and the layouts, the 7 rewritten so they compile, and without the 7 whose code is gone, caught 80. Two of the 5 that passed got the new tests above: a target's node on the target's first token in place of its root's, and the tuple table matching a longer tuple whose first types are the same. The declarations case catches the second only because, with today's type ids, `(int, [8]u8)` probes past `(int, [8]u8, bool)`, so a change in the type ids can hide it again. The last 3 change only the hash's quality, the table's load or the reuse of a repeated tuple's members, which no test can see.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no tuples: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 1.3 million more instructions, 0.07%, 1,867.1 million against 1,865.8 million. Assignments now call `assign_to`, which destructuring shares, where clang inlined the whole assignment into `walk` before: 0.9 million. The parser keeps the start of each statement's first expression for a list of targets and tests for a comma after a `return`'s value, 0.3 million; a first build tested for a comma after every statement's first expression, 0.3 million more, and the test now runs only when no `=` follows. Two other layouts were measured: inlining `assign_to` into `walk` runs 1.1 million more in the C build than calling it, and handling `_ = x` in `walk`, with the rest in `assign_to`, runs 2.5 million fewer in the C build but 4.7 million more in the translated build, so `walk` calls `assign_to` for every assignment. Built with `chibi c --release-fast`, the translated compiler runs 2.1 million fewer instructions, 0.11%: its `walk` and `assign_to` together run 2.5 million fewer than its `walk` did. It runs 3.6% more than the C build, against 3.7% before. The translation has 828 `!!`, 39 more, where the new code reads the declarations' arrays and the tuple table; the corpus runs two of them, the test for a tuple at the start of `check_decl`, once per declaration, and the table's allocation, once.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind and no new IR shape: a tuple is a struct, typed with a declaration that has `DECL_TUPLE` and no name, and its members have no names. The ids above move: every node kind after `NODE_VAR_TYPE_INIT` moves by 2 and every one after `NODE_SWITCH_ITEM` by 5, and every diagnostic after `DIAG_FIELD_COUNT` by 3, and after `DIAG_NO_ZERO_FIELD` by 4. The shared test programs gain `TUPLES` at the end of `BACKEND_PROGRAMS`.
       - Not built yet: the two-result overflow checks, and what the rules above leave unsupported. Named results came with step 3k.25.
     - **3k.25 Named results: built 2026-11-23,** the rest of rank 12 (the tuples row of 2026-11-23). A function declaration may name its results, as in Go and Odin: `fn divmod(a: int, b: int) -> (q: int, r: int)`. The names are locals of the body, declared after the parameters, and they start at zero as `x: T` does, with a struct's field defaults. A bare `return` returns them, and `q = a / b; r = a % b; return` builds the same IR as `return q, r`. One name gives the plain type, so `-> (n: int)` returns an `int`, and two or more give a tuple: a caller can't tell named results from unnamed ones. `return a, b` still works in such a function. The IR has no new kind.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Name every result or none: `-> (q: int, bool)` gives "name every result or none". After `->`, a `(` followed by a name and `:` starts named results, so a keyword as a name, `-> (if: int)`, gives "expected a type", as it does in a tuple type.
         - Only a function declaration names its results: `g: fn() -> (q: int, r: int)` gives "only a function declaration can name its results". Go allows names in a function type and ignores them.
         - A type without a zero value can't be a named result: `-> (p: *int, n: int)` gives "a named result starts at zero, and `*int` has no zero value", and so do an enum without a zero member and the structs, tuples and arrays that hold one. Go and Odin can zero every type, because their pointers can be nil; in chibi such a function writes `return p, n`.
         - A result's name follows the rules of a local's: a second `q`, a parameter's name or a local in the body with a result's name gives "`q` is already a local at …", `_` gives "`_` can only discard a value", and a builtin type's name gives "`int` is a builtin type name". A bare `return` reads each result from that result's own local, so a result whose name failed gives no second error.
         - A function with named results must still end in a `return`: "the function can reach its end without returning a value of type `(int, int)`", as in Go. A bare `return` in a function without named results gives "`return` needs a value of type `(int, int)`", as before.
         - `return a, b` returns its values and leaves the names as they are. Go writes the values to the names first, so a deferred function sees them and can change them; step 3c took Zig's rule instead, in which a `return` reads its values before the defers run, so a `defer` can't change them.
         - No default values: `-> (n: int = 1)` gives "expected `,` or `)`".
         - An `extern fn` may name its results, and the names have no effect: one name gives a type that C can see, and two or more give a tuple, which C can't.
         - The node kinds gain `NODE_RESULT` after `NODE_VALUE_IF_ITEM`, the last kind before the lists, and the list `NODE_RESULTS` after `NODE_DESTRUCTURE_ASSIGN`, the last list. The declaration flags gain `DECL_RESULTS`, 2048, which the checker sets while it checks the signature. The diagnostics gain `DIAG_RESULT_ZERO` after `DIAG_MISSING_RETURN`.
       - Tests: the parser's tests gain trees for named results in a declaration, with `=>`, on several lines with a tuple among them and with `!` at the end of a line, and in an `extern fn`; errors for a result without a name, a missing comma, names in a function type and a keyword as a name; and a postorder. The checker's tests gain two diagnostics cases, which cover each message above at its position, with a type that isn't a type and an unknown type in a file without structs; two IR cases: a bare `return` beside the `return q, r` it equals, results of slice, struct, optional and array types, a tuple as one named result, a function with `=>`, and a bare `return` as the arm of a `switch` that gives a value; a function with 1,000 named results; and the statement positions of a body that ends in a bare `return`. The shared programs gain `NAMED_RESULTS`, 15 functions that the reference interpreter, the arm64 backend under qemu and the C backend share: a divmod, a bare `return` from inside a loop, results of struct type with defaults, `string`, optional pointer, array and `int`, a result whose address is taken, a recursive Fibonacci with two results, bare `return`s in `switch` arms, a tuple among the results, and `return a, b` beside a bare `return`. On arm64 they get 32 runs computed by hand, and the grid of both backends now covers 399 functions, 8 of them new. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 47, 14 in the parser and 33 in the checker. A first round of 44 caught 31, and 4 didn't compile because they left a name unused. Of the 9 that passed, 5 got new tests: a keyword as a result's name, which the parser took for named results without the test for a name; `!` at the end of a line inside the list, which needs the list to count as brackets; a result type that isn't a type, whose error must point at the type and not at the `(`; an unknown result type in a file without structs, where a bare `return` without the test for an unknown type read the members of an empty declaration and crashed; and a bare `return` as the arm of a `switch` that gives a value, which left the results on the stack for the arm to take. A sixth showed that a bare `return` gave a second error for a result whose name failed: in `-> (q: int, q: i8)` it read the first `q` for the second result, so it now reads each result from that result's own local. A second round of 45, with the 4 rewritten so they compile and a plant for the new test, caught 41, and a third round of 47 on the final layout below, with 2 plants for the declaration flag, caught 43. The 4 that passed both rounds can't be observed: the token of the list's node, which only the tuple errors read, and a list of names reaches none of them; the token pushed with each result's type, which nothing reads; the test that a name's binding belongs to this body, which fails only for `_` and builtin type names, in functions that already have an error; and the reserve of stack space for the results, which the checker already makes when it checks the declaration, because a function with named results is never checked inside another body.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no named results: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs 0.4 million more instructions, 0.02%, 1,867.5 million against 1,867.1 million: the parser 0.08 million, most of it the test for `(` after `->`; the test for the flag in `check_body`, 0.06 million; and `walk` 0.26 million, where clang places the blocks of the markers' cases differently now that the two new kinds renumber the markers. The translated compiler runs 0.2 million fewer, 1,933.2 million against 1,933.5 million: its `walk` runs 0.34 million fewer and its `check_body` 0.1 million more. It runs 3.5% more than the C build, against 3.6% before. The first build cost more in two places. Its bare `return` with named results took the path of `return_value` that converts and returns a value, and the translated compiler's `walk`, which `return_value` is inlined into, ran 3.9 million more instructions, 1.3%; a bare `return` with named results now goes to a function of its own, `return_results`, and `return_value` keeps its old shape. And its `check_body` read the two nodes before each body, 12 instructions per body: 0.24 million in the C build and 0.28 million in the translation, which the flag `DECL_RESULTS` brings to 0.06 and 0.1 million. Placing the new kinds at the ends of their sections, in place of beside `NODE_FN_RETURN` and `NODE_TUPLE_TYPE`, saved 0.03 million in `parse_range` in both builds. The translation has 838 `!!`, 10 more, where the new code reads the declarations' flags, bodies and members; the corpus runs one of them, the read of the flags in `check_body`, once per body.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind and no new IR shape: a bare `return` builds the IR of `return q, r`. The ids above move: every list kind moves by 1 and every marker by 2, and every diagnostic after `DIAG_MISSING_RETURN` by 1. The shared test programs gain `NAMED_RESULTS` at the end of `BACKEND_PROGRAMS`.
       - Not built yet: top-level code, and what the rules above leave unsupported. The two-result overflow checks came with step 3k.26.
     - **3k.26 Two-result overflow checks: built 2026-11-23,** the overflow row of 2026-11-23 in [../decisions.md](../decisions.md). `bits.add_overflow(a, b)`, `bits.sub_overflow(a, b)` and `bits.mul_overflow(a, b)` give a tuple `(T, bool)`: the result wrapped to `T`, as `+%`, `-%` and `*%` give it, and whether the exact result doesn't fit in `T`, as in Zig, Odin and Swift. They replace step 3i.2's `bits.add_overflows` and its kin, which gave only the `bool`. `s, o := bits.add_overflow(a, b)` declares both, `_, o := bits.add_overflow(a, b)` only the test, and `p, _ := bits.mul_overflow(a, b)` only the product. The tuple is the type that `(T, bool)` names, so `t := bits.add_overflow(a, b)` holds it, `return bits.sub_overflow(a, b)` returns it from a function that returns `(T, bool)`, and a parameter of that type takes it. The IR has no new kind: a call that destructuring takes apart builds the two values straight from the operands, step 3i.2's `add-overflows` for the test and the `add-wrap` of `+%` for the value, and stores no tuple.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - The names end in `_overflow`, as GCC's builtins do. Step 3i.2 chose `_overflows` so that `if add_overflows(a, b)` read as a question, and the call now gives a value as well as the answer.
         - The operands take a common type as step 3i.2's did, as a binary operator's do: two untyped constants become `int`, so `bits.add_overflow(1, 2)` is an `(int, bool)`.
         - Constant operands fold, in a body too: `s, o := bits.add_overflow(i8(127), i8(1))` stores -128 and `true`, and `const K := bits.mul_overflow(3, 4)` is the constant `.{ 12, false }`.
         - When destructuring keeps both values, each operand that isn't a constant is read once into a hidden local, and both values read the locals, because an IR value has one use. With `_` in one place, only the other value is built. `_, _ = bits.add_overflow(a, b)` still builds the test and drops it, because the operands are already read and each read must be used.
         - Only a call that is the whole right side of a destructuring is taken apart. In `s, o := g(bits.add_overflow(a, b))` the call gives a tuple to `g`, which the checker stores in a hidden local and passes as it passes any tuple.
         - A tuple where a `bool` is expected gives "expected a value", as any tuple does, so step 3i.2's `if bits.add_overflows(a, b)` becomes `_, o := bits.add_overflow(a, b)` and then `if o`.
         - On arm64, `s, o := bits.add_overflow(x, y)` on `int` is `add`, then `cmn` and `cset`, besides the moves that the hidden locals cost, where clang writes `adds` and `cset`. And `_, c := bits.add_overflow(x, y)` followed by `if c` on `u64` is `cmn`, `cset` and `cbz`, where step 3i.2's `if bits.add_overflows(x, y)` was `cmn` and `b.lo`: the backend branches on the flags only when the test is the `if`'s own condition, and here the test goes through the local `c`. The C backend writes the wrapping `+` on unsigned operands beside `__builtin_add_overflow`, and clang joins them.
       - Translator: `__builtin_add_overflow(a, b, &r)` and its `sub` and `mul` forms become `r, overflowed = bits.add_overflow(a, b)` before the statement, after `overflowed: bool = undefined`, and `overflowed` in its place ([translator.md](translator.md) rule 13). A `bool` declared from one, `bool o = __builtin_add_overflow(a, b, &r)`, becomes `o: bool = undefined` and `r, o = bits.add_overflow(a, b)`. One call gives both results, so the translation no longer needs `r = a +% b` beside the check, a result that is one of the operands, as in the lexer's `o |= __builtin_add_overflow(sum, c, &sum)`, needs no store after the statement, and a builtin in the condition of a `?:` now translates. Still refused, each with its own message: one on the right of `&&` or `||`, in a branch of `?:`, after a comma or inside `sizeof`; one whose operands or result have side effects; one whose result isn't a local or parameter whose address the function never takes; one whose operands and result differ in type; and one in a `for` condition, whose message now names overflow builtins beside `++`, `--` and `va_arg`.
       - Tests: the checker's tests fold ten constants into tuples, `I` and `J` among them in `i128` and `u128`; the diagnostics case gives each error with the new names, and four more: a tuple as an `if` condition and beside `or`, three names for two values, and a list of two values whose first is a tuple, which is no error. Two IR cases: one with `s, a :=`, `_, b :=`, `c, _ :=`, a tuple local taken apart later, `_, _ =`, a constant tuple taken apart, `=` that reads one of its targets, and a constant operand beside a variable one; and one in which constant operands fold in a body. The shared programs' overflow tests take only the test from destructuring, as `_, o := bits.add_overflow(a, b)`, and gain four functions: a checked add that uses both values, a 32-bit multiply that keeps only the product, a function that returns the tuple and one that takes the tuple apart. On arm64 they get 9 runs computed by hand, and the multiply that keeps only the product has no `umull`. The grid's eight functions, one for each integer type, now hold each value beside its test, as tuple locals taken apart later and as constant checks, so the grid compares the wrapped values too; the grid covers 400 functions, 1 of them new. The 128-bit test and the vector test take both values. A sample of 10 functions translates and prints the same as clang's build of the C under ASan and UBSan, and 8 bodies with the refused forms fail to translate, each with its message. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 44, 32 in the checker and 12 in the translator. Before the round, the first IR case gained a constant operand beside a variable one, which a checker that holds constants in hidden locals needs before it fails. A first round of the checker's 32 caught 27, and 3 didn't compile because they left a name unused. Of the 2 that passed, one got the second IR case: a folded value left unwrapped stored 128 into an `i8`. The other drops the test that the call sits in a body, which only keeps the stack index in range, because destructuring happens only in bodies. A second round, with the 3 rewritten so they compile, caught 31. Without the checker's own cases, the tests under qemu still catch 21 of them, among them every plant that reads an operand twice or swaps the two values, through the 9 runs computed by hand. The other 10 change only folded constants, the IR's shape or a diagnostic, which only the checker's cases can see, because the reference interpreter and both backends run the same IR. Of the translator's 12, the sample caught 7, and 2 didn't compile. Of the 3 that passed, one sent a `bool` declaration through a temporary, which changes only the text, so the sample now checks the text of two such declarations. Another let a declaration of any type take that path, which C's types never allow, because C converts the builtin's `bool` to another type with a cast, so the test of the type is now an `assert`. A second round of 11 caught 10. The last changes a mark on the flag's value that only C's comparisons need, whose C type is `int`; the builtin's C type is `bool`, and C's own casts convert it.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no overflow checks: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte, and every function of the compiler runs as many instructions as before, in the C build and in the translation built with `chibi c --release-fast`. Their totals, 1,867.5 million and 1,908.5 million, differ from before by 151 and 60 instructions, which the C library spends on the harness's paths and on printing its times. The translated compiler still runs 2.2% more than the C build. The translation has 265 `!!`, 1 more, where the new code writes a constant tuple into the IR's data, which the corpus never runs.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind. Two new IR shapes: destructuring builds `add-wrap` and `add-overflows` (and their `sub` and `mul` forms) over the same operands, each reading the operands from hidden locals when both are built; and a tuple local filled by two `store-at`s through `field`. A constant tuple is data, as any constant tuple is. `NAME_ADD_OVERFLOWS` and its kin are now `NAME_ADD_OVERFLOW`, `NAME_SUB_OVERFLOW` and `NAME_MUL_OVERFLOW`, with the same ids. The shared test programs change: `OVERFLOW_TESTS` takes the test from destructuring and gains `checked_add`, `wrapped_mul`, `pair` and `from_pair`; `OVERFLOW_GRID`, the 128-bit `overflows` and the vector test's `mul_both` take both values; the grid has 400 functions.
       - Not built yet: what the rules above leave unsupported. Top-level code came with step 3k.27.
     - **3k.27 Top-level code and the command line: built 2026-11-23,** the Top-level code row of 2026-11-12 and the Compiler row of 2026-11-23 in [../decisions.md](../decisions.md), with the recommendations of [top-level-code.md](top-level-code.md) sections 1 to 4 and 6. The statements at the top level of the entry file, the first file named, run in order as the body of `main`, which the checker declares as `export fn main(argc: i32, argv: [*]cstring) -> i32`. `total := 0` among them declares a local of `main`. `return n` takes an `i32` and gives the exit status, and a bare `return` or the end of the code gives 0. `import "std/os"` gives `os.args`, a `[]cstring` that holds the command line, starting with the program's path, as C's `argv` does. When any file of the program imports `std/os`, `main` starts by storing `argv` and `argc` in a hidden global that `os.args` names, and a program that never imports it stores nothing. A program whose entry file has no code still starts from `export fn main`, as the translated compiler does.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Declarations among the code, such as `fn`, `struct`, `const` and `extern`, stay package declarations wherever they sit. A function can't see the code's locals, even in the entry file: `fn uses() -> int => local` gives "unknown name `local`". The code has no names for `argc` and `argv`, so `os.args` is its only way to the command line.
         - A declaration named `main`, in any file, beside code in the entry file gives "the program starts at the code in the file you run, so it can't declare `main`", at the declaration. Code outside the entry file still gives "only the file you run may contain code".
         - `pub` before a local gives "the checker doesn't support this yet".
         - `os.args` is a global that code can assign, as Go's `os.Args` can, so `os.args = os.args[1..]` drops the program's path for the code after it. A read loads it where it stands, as a read of any slice global does, so `f(os.args, g())` passes the slice from before `g` writes it, in Go's order. It can't appear in a constant: "expected a constant". In a program that starts from `export fn main`, nothing fills it, and reading it gives "`os.args` is set only when the program starts at the code in the file you run". A plain `args` gives "`args` is in the package `os`; write `os.args` after `import "std/os"`", as a builtin's plain name does.
         - `main` takes `argc` and `argv` whether or not the code reads them, so C's start-up code and the arm64 export call it as they call `export fn main`.
         - `main` sits where the code's first statement starts, which the C backend's `#line` before `main` names: at the earlier of the statement's first node and its root, because `x :=` followed by `0` on the next line starts with the node of `0`, and `os.args[` followed by `0] = s` on the next line ends with the `=`.
       - Tests: the checker's entry-file case now runs code where it gave "doesn't support this yet": a call into `bits`, an `if` that returns, a constant between statements and a local that reads it. Four IR cases: two files whose code loops over `os.args[1..]` and calls C's `atoi`, returns bare, returns a call into the other file, whose function assigns `os.args`, and runs a statement after; a file with only code, which stores nothing; an entry that doesn't import `std/os` beside a library that does, which stores `os.args`; and `pick(os.args, skip())`, which loads `os.args` before `skip` writes it. The diagnostics case gives each error above, and a library case gives the `os.args` error in a program without code. The driver builds a two-file program with `chibi build` and runs it with no arguments, one and two, checking the exit status and what `puts` printed; `chibi c` writes `main`'s `#line` at the first statement in two programs, one for each side of the rule above; the message for a program without code or `export fn main` names the entry file; and two new bad files give the errors on `main` and `os.args`. On arm64, the export runs four times with different arguments, `os.args` read whole before a call that assigns it among them, and a function reads `os.args` after each run. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 38 in the checker. A first round caught 31, and 3 didn't compile because they left a name unused. Of the 4 that passed, two found bugs, both fixed with tests: `os.args` gave its address, as a struct does, so the load came where the value was used, and `pick(os.args, skip())` passed the slice that `skip` left; and `main` sat at the first node of its first statement, which in `words :=` followed by `0` on the next line is the `0`, so the C backend's `#line` named the second line. The other two change nothing that a program can see: one leaves the stack index where each statement left it, which costs stack space, and one pushes a plain entry in place of the block marker under the code, which no statement at the top level reads, though a function body has one there. A second round of 39 on the final code, with plants for both fixes and the 3 rewritten so they compile, caught 37; the 2 left are the two that change nothing, and they pass on arm64 under qemu too.
       - Measured on the timing corpus, which has no top-level code: the checker's IR and the arm64 code are the same as before, byte for byte. The C build runs about 2,000 more instructions, 1,867.5 million in all: `declare`, inside `check_package`, runs 2,404 more to check a fourth package's import in each of the 200 files, and the name table interns 5 more names. `check_body`, which now opens and closes its body through `open_body` and `close_body`, runs as many instructions as before, once `finish_ir` is marked `ALWAYS_INLINE` and `open_body` reads the result type where `check_body` read it: with `finish_ir` called from two places, clang stopped inlining it and the C build ran 0.46 million more, and with the result type read before the body's setup, 20,000 more. The translation built with `chibi c --release-fast` runs 1.6 million fewer, 1,906.9 million: its lexer runs 1.6 million fewer, from where clang lays out the code beside the longer table of names, and every other function runs within 20,000 of before. It has 266 `!!`, 1 more, on the allocation of the list of the code's statements in `check_init`, which runs once, and it runs 2.1% more instructions than the C build.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind and no new shape. The entry's `main` is an exported function whose first slots are `argc` and `argv`, and its first statements store them through `global`, `load`, `convert` and `store-pair`, as an assignment of a slice to a global does. The name ids from `assert` on move up by 1, for `args`, and every name a program declares by 5, for `args`, `os`, `main`, `argc` and `argv`; the diagnostics after "only the file you run may contain code" move by 2. `check_body` now opens and closes a body through `open_body` and `close_body`, which `check_entry` shares, `finish_ir` is `ALWAYS_INLINE`, and arm64_test's `check_text` takes a flag for an entry file.
       - Not built yet: `try`, which gives "the function returns `i32`, which can't fail, so `try` can't pass an error up" since step 3f.1, and `defer catch`, which gives "the function returns `i32`, which can't fail, so `defer catch` never runs" since step 3f.6; `defer` came with step 3c. The translator still writes the compiler's entry as `export fn main`, and the switch writes it as top-level code.
     - **3k.28 64-byte vectors: built 2026-11-24,** the Language row of 2026-11-24 in [../decisions.md](../decisions.md), which the owner chose after timing the switch on the M4 Pro. `[64]u8`, `[64]i8`, `[32]u16`, `[32]i16`, `[16]u32`, `[16]i32`, `[8]u64` and `[8]i64` take the lane operators of step 3k.22, as Zig's `@Vector(64, u8)` and Rust's `u8x64` do: the wrapping arithmetic, the bit operators and shifts, `simd.eq` and its siblings, a scalar that stands for every lane, `bitcast` between them, `mem.unaligned_load` and `mem.unaligned_store`, and the values of conditionals, labeled blocks and `switch`, arguments and results. `simd.top_bits(v)` gives one bit for each lane in the smallest unsigned integer that holds them: `u64` for 64 lanes, `u32` for 32, `u16` for 16 and `u8` for 8. `simd.lookup(t, i)` takes a `[16]u8` table with `[16]u8` or `[64]u8` indexes, or the same in `i8`, and gives the indexes' type, so `simd.lookup(t, v & 15)` of a `[64]u8` gives a `[64]u8`.
       - IR: no new kind. Eight vector types follow the 16-byte ones inside the checker, `u8x64` to `i64x8`, in the same order, so each 64-byte type is its 16-byte type plus 8, and the type ids from `string` on move up by 8. `lookup` takes its indexes' type, `top-bits` gives the type that `check_top_bits_type` in `check.h` names, and `check_type_size` of a vector is its lanes times its lane's size. The checker never puts a vector in a slot of its own: a local of 64 bytes is an array slot read with `load-at u8x64` and written with `store-at u8x64`, as step 3k.22 reads and writes 16-byte arrays.
       - The C backend writes the 64-byte types as GNU vectors of 64 bytes, `chibi_u8x64` and its kin, and passes them to its `lookup` and `top_bits` helpers through pointers: on x86-64, GCC and clang warn that a 64-byte vector passed by value changes the ABI when AVX-512 is on, and `src/simd.h` turns off that warning, `-Wpsabi`, for the C build. On NEON, `chibi_u8x64_top_bits` takes the AND of each quarter with the weights 1 to 128, adds pairs four times and moves the result out with `fmov`, 9 instructions for a mask of comparisons, where clang drops the shifts that make all-ones lanes; elsewhere it joins four 16-byte masks. The helpers took names that say their type: `chibi_lookup` is now `chibi_u8x16_lookup`, and `chibi_top_bits_x16` to `chibi_top_bits_x2` are `chibi_u8x16_top_bits`, `chibi_u16x8_top_bits`, `chibi_u32x4_top_bits` and `chibi_u64x2_top_bits`.
       - The arm64 backend keeps each 64-byte value in a 64-byte home on the stack and computes it a quarter at a time in V29 to V31: one 16-byte instruction per quarter, between loads and stores of the quarters. A home goes back to a free list after its one use. The value of an `if`, `else`, `switch` or block `exit` goes through one 64-byte home per function that each branch writes, and `bitcast` takes over its operand's home and emits nothing. `top_bits` gathers each quarter with step 3i.5's 16-byte sequence and joins the four, about 35 instructions for a `[64]u8`. A 64-byte array of integers gets a 16-byte-aligned frame slot, as a 16-byte one does, so each quarter loads with one `ldr q` at an offset.
       - The translator writes `simd_lookup64` as `simd.lookup`, with step 3t.7's `& 15`, and `simd_mask64` as `simd.top_bits`, as it writes `simd_mask16`.
