       - The lexer: `src/simd.h`'s `u8x64` is a 64-byte vector on every path, and the predicates that `block_masks` calls, `is_ident`, `is_ws`, `pair_firsts`, `is_rare`, `is_comment_start` and `is_line_end`, take and give one `u8x64`, where the macros `MASK64` and `MASK64_PAIR` called them on four `u8x16`; `simd_lookup64` replaces `simd_lookup16`. The NEON C build keeps its deinterleaving `vld4q_u8` load and its `sri` mask, since a lane-wise operation doesn't depend on the order of the lanes, and the generic path, which the translator reads, loads the 64 bytes in order. `is_ident` ORs the test for `_` before the digit range: with the range last, clang 20, unlike clang 18, shared the range's compare with the `.digit` mask and sign-extended the OR of the 64 lanes with `shl` and `cmlt`, 8 more instructions per block.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Only arrays of 16 and 64 bytes are vectors. An operator on a `[32]u8` still gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", and `mem.unaligned_load([32]u8, p)` gives "`mem.unaligned_load` reads and writes numbers and 16-byte and 64-byte arrays of integers, found `[32]u8`".
         - `simd.lookup` keeps a 16-byte table for 64-byte indexes, as NEON's `tbl` and x86's `pshufb` look up 16 bytes, and its result takes the indexes' type.
         - `simd.top_bits` gives the smallest unsigned integer with a bit for each lane, so `[16]u32` gives a `u16` and `[8]u64` a `u8`, as `[16]u8` gave a `u16` in step 3i.5.
         - A 64-byte array aligns as its lanes do, as a 16-byte one does; only the arm64 backend's frame aligns it to 16, for speed.
       - Tests: a shared program of 16 functions, `VECTOR_WIDE_RUNS`, runs 75 cases whose results a Python model computed: loads and stores at offsets into a 160-byte array, `[16]u32` loads and stores, lookups whose `[64]u8` and `[64]i8` indexes pass 15, `top_bits` of each lane width, conditionals, labeled blocks and `switch` that give 64-byte arrays, calls that take and return them, `+%=` and `^=` on rows of a `[2][64]u8`, a struct field that holds a 64-byte array, `bitcast` through four types, shifts with checked counts, 64-bit lanes multiplied, an array read before a call that changes it, and a lookup whose table loads after its indexes, so the table arrives in a register. A grid of 8 functions, one for each 64-byte type, runs 20 operators against oracles in C, on a quarter of the argument pairs of step 3k.22's grid, since each call does four times the work. The reference interpreter, the C backend with SSE2, SSSE3 and generic helpers on x86-64 and with NEON and generic helpers on arm64, and the arm64 backend under qemu run both. They sit in arrays of their own, `VECTOR_WIDE_RUNS` and `VECTOR_WIDE_GRID`, so the LowIR spike's tests, which share `VECTOR_RUNS` and `VECTOR_GRID`, run the same programs as before. The builtins' diagnostics case gains 8 errors, and an IR dump shows a 64-byte load, a lookup, a `bitcast`, a store and two `top-bits`. `make test` passes on x86-64, with the generic SIMD and with the bulk lexer, and on arm64 under qemu, and so do `make translated` and the bootstrap check on both targets.
       - Planted bugs: 57 in the checker, the C backend and its helpers, the arm64 backend, `src/simd.h` and the lexer. A first round caught 50, and 1 didn't compile until rewritten. Of the 6 that passed, traps planted in their place showed that 4 sit on paths that nothing reaches. The checker never puts a vector in a slot of its own, in any test, in the translated compiler or in a program that runs the 64-byte tests through the C backend on arm64, so the arm64 backend's 64-byte `load` and `store` of a slot are gone; the 16-byte ones stay, because `u128` and `i128` slots use them. A 64-byte `bitcast` never sees a constant, which an assert now states, so its copy of one is gone. A case that gave the 64-byte vector types an alignment of 16 changed nothing, since only IR values have those types, so it is gone too. One plant found a gap: no lookup had its table arrive in a register, which happens when the indexes come from an operator, as in `simd.lookup(t, v ^ 3)`, so that the backend must copy the table out of V29 before the lookup writes it; `wide_lookup_late` now covers it. The last changes only the frame alignment of 64-byte arrays, which costs an `add` before each quarter's load and nothing that a program can see. A second round of 54 on the final code, with the rewritten plant and one for `bitcast`'s home, caught 53; the 1 left is the frame alignment. After the step, a plant in the LowIR spike that built a mask from one quarter twice passed every case, because `wide_top_bits` sets byte i's top bit from bit i of its argument, and the arguments of its 16-, 32- and 64-bit cases gave quarters 0 and 1 the same bits. Four cases whose quarters all differ, the spike's and one for each of those lane widths, now catch that plant and 5 of its kind planted here, 3 in the C helpers and 2 in the arm64 backend's `top_bits`; 4 of the 5 had passed the old cases.
       - Measured: the owner's M4 Pro timed the switch on 2026-11-24, before this step ([translator.md](translator.md#speed-of-the-translated-compiler)). The translated build took 5.8% more cycles per line than the C build, and its lexer and parser 13.2% more cycles and 17.5% more instructions, because the generic `simd.h` built each of the 9 masks of a 64-byte block from four 16-byte masks, about 30 instructions where the NEON C build's `sri` takes about 6. Counted under qemu with clang 20 on the corpus's 34,601 lines, which matches the Mac's counts within 3 instructions per line, the translated lexer and parser now run 793.94 instructions per line against 886.97 before, 41.93 more than the C build's 752.01 where they ran 132.00 more. Checking and arm64 code generation run as before, so the three phases together run 3.64% more instructions than the C build, against 7.67%. What remains in the lexer, 23.8 instructions per line, comes to about 65 per 64-byte block: 28.5 in the masks, whose `and` and pairwise adds take about 9 instructions where `sri` takes about 6; 13.5 in loads, since the translation loads the block and the bytes after it in order where the C build deinterleaves them with `ld4`; about 7 where the translation tests bits with `cmeq` and `bic` and the C build with `cmtst`; and the rest in spills and moves. On x86-64, callgrind counts 1,866.69 million instructions on the corpus for the C build, 5.60 million or 0.30% more than before, from 69 more `movdqa` spills in `lexer_tokenize`, since each 64-byte value takes 4 of the 16 XMM registers; the release-fast translation runs 1,870.55 million, 0.06 million more, 0.21% more than the C build. The checker's IR and the arm64 code for the corpus are the same as before, byte for byte, and the translation has 320 `!!`, 2 more, in `arm64.chibi`.
       - Hand-off: no new IR kind. The eight 64-byte vector types follow `TYPE_ID_I64X2`, so `TYPE_ID_STRING`, `TYPE_ID_CSTRING` and every later type id move up by 8. `lookup` takes its indexes' type; `top-bits` gives `check_top_bits_type` (new in `check.h`), which the spike's `value_type` needs in place of its `lanes == 16 ? U16 : U8`; and `check_type_size` of a vector is its lanes times its lane's size, where it was 16. The C backend's helpers were renamed as above. `GridFunction` gained the lane width, `bits`, and the 64-byte programs sit in `VECTOR_WIDE_RUNS` and `VECTOR_WIDE_GRID` with their results, so the spike's tests run the same programs as before and can add these when it lowers 64-byte vectors. The translated lexer now uses 64-byte vectors, so the compiler built through LowIR needs them, where the spike maps every vector type to `v128`.
       - Not built yet: 32-byte vectors; 64-byte tables for `simd.lookup`, which NEON's four-register `tbl` and AVX-512's `vpermb` give; a 64-byte `top_bits` on arm64 shaped like the C helper's, about 9 instructions where the backend's takes 35; and 64-byte values in registers in the arm64 backend, which keeps them on the stack.
     - **3k.29 `@cold`: built 2026-11-25,** after the owner chose on 2026-11-24 to chase the checker's gap before the switch, when the M4 Pro timed the translated build's checking at 2.0% more cycles per line than the C build's. The compiler's C marks 8 of the checker's functions `NOINLINE COLD`: `report`, `report_from`, and the six that open, close and leave `defer` scopes, bodies and loops. The translation gave them `@noinline` alone (rule 14 of [translator.md](translator.md)), so clang weighed a branch into one as it weighs any branch. In `walk`, `assign_to`, `or_else_left` and `result_value`, into which clang inlines `for_range` and `assign_at`, the calls to `store_local`, `load_local` and `add_slot` then sat on paths that clang priced at under 2% of the function's runs, where it inlines a callee only up to a cost of 45 in place of 250, and they stayed calls: 314 thousand instructions on the corpus under qemu, which the C build inlines (step 3t.18 in [translator.md](translator.md)).
       - What works: `@cold` before a function with a body, where `@inline` and `@noinline` stand, as in `@cold fn report(c: *Checker, file: u32, token: u32)`. It says that the function rarely runs, as GCC's and clang's `cold`, Rust's `#[cold]` and Odin's `@(cold)` do, and the function never inlines. The C backend writes `__attribute__((noinline, cold))` after `CHIBI_FN`, on the function's declaration and on its definition. The arm64 backend ignores it, as it ignores the other marks. The checker's declaration dump prints it first, as in `@cold pub fn m() -> void`.
       - `cold` is a builtin name, interned after `noinline`, so the parser compares a token's id and no keyword is added. It stays an ordinary name everywhere else: `cold := inline` parses.
       - Errors, all from the parser: any other name after `@` gives "expected `@inline`, `@noinline` or `@cold`", and a mark before anything but `fn name`, after an optional `pub` and `export`, gives "`@inline`, `@noinline` and `@cold` must come before a function with a body", a second mark among them, as before. `@cold` on a function inside a body gives "the checker doesn't support this yet", as the other marks do.
       - Parser and checker: `NODE_COLD` follows `NODE_NOINLINE` and holds its declaration as the other marks do, and `declare` reads it into a new declaration flag, `DECL_COLD`.
       - IR: no change.
       - The checker's `emit` reads the IR's four arrays into locals before it writes them, as the parser's `emit` reads its `kinds`. The translation reads a struct's fields through bytes, so clang can't tell that a store into `types` leaves the pointers `as` and `bs` alone, and it read each one again after the store before it; the C build's type-based aliasing let it read two of them with one `ldp`. `emit` runs 138,914 times on the corpus, one load fewer each.
       - Tests: the parser reads `@cold pub fn` and `cold` as a name, pins `NODE_COLD`'s kind, and gives the errors above for `@cold extern fn` and `@noinline @cold fn`; the 10 rows of the old errors take the new wording. The checker dumps a `@cold pub fn` and reports `@cold` inside a body. `INLINE_MARKS` gains `inl_rare`, an `@cold` function, and `inl_guard`, which calls it on one branch. The C backend test finds `noinline, cold` on `inl_rare`'s declaration and definition and no mark on `inl_guard`'s, the arm64 backend runs both on arm64 under qemu with 7 hand-computed cases, 1 of which traps on overflow, and both join the grid, now 498 functions. `test/hello.txt` has every name's id one higher.
       - Planted bugs: 13 in the parser, the checker and the C backend, all caught: refusing `@cold`, reading it as `@inline` or as `@noinline`, skipping `NODE_COLD` in `declare`, giving it `DECL_NOINLINE` or no flag, dumping it as `@noinline` or not at all, writing `cold` without `noinline`, `noinline` without `cold` or neither, and two in `emit`'s new loads. The 5 planted in the translator are in step 3t.18 of [translator.md](translator.md).
       - Measured: under qemu with clang 20, on the corpus's 34,601 lines, the translated checker runs 890.37 instructions per line against 900.52, 1.53 fewer than the C build's 891.90, with 4.38 fewer loads per line, of which `emit` gives 4.01; the C build runs 0.03 more per line, and both builds' lexers, parsers and arm64 backends run as before. On x86-64 the translated compiler runs 0.14% fewer instructions than before, 0.04% more than the C build ([translator.md](translator.md#speed-of-the-translated-compiler)). The IR and the arm64 code are byte for byte the same.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - `@cold` also means `@noinline`, so a function still takes one mark. Every `COLD` in the compiler's C is `NOINLINE COLD`, and clang seldom inlines a cold function anyway: a call to one gets the threshold of 45.
         - The translator stops at a C function marked `COLD` without `NOINLINE`, which `@cold` would change.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. `NODE_COLD` follows `NODE_NOINLINE`, so the node kinds after it move up by one, and `NAME_COLD` follows `NAME_NOINLINE`, so the builtin names after it, and every program's names, move up by one. `INLINE_MARKS` has two more functions, `inl_rare` and `inl_guard`, both on the grid.
       - Not built yet: branch hints. The C's 30 `LIKELY` and `UNLIKELY`, 20 of them in the lexer, still translate to their bare conditions.
     - **3k.30 The lexer's masks on NEON: built and reverted 2026-11-25,** after the owner chose on 2026-11-25 to chase the lexer's gap before the switch, when GitHub's M1 runner timed step 3k.29's translated build at 0.8% to 1.8% more time per line than the C build, most of it in the lexer and parser. The C build's lexer loads each 64-byte block with NEON's `vld4q_u8`, which deinterleaves it: its register k holds bytes k, k + 4, k + 8 and on. The predicates that `block_masks` calls work lane by lane, so the order doesn't change them, and `simd_mask64` packs a mask's four registers with three `sri` and one `shrn`, about 6 instructions. The translation loaded the bytes in order, and its `simd.top_bits` took about 9: an `and` with the bit weights, four `addp` and an `fmov`.
       - What works: on NEON the C backend keeps the lanes of a `[64]u8` or `[64]i8` value in the order that `ld4` gives, lane 4j + k at byte 16k + j of the C vector, and puts them back in order wherever code can see the order: in memory, and in a `bitcast` to or from a 64-byte vector of wider lanes. A program's results don't change. Lane-wise operators, constants, which hold one value in every lane, `simd.lookup` and the values of conditionals work in either order. `simd.top_bits` of a `[64]u8` makes its 64 bits with `sshr`, three `sri` and one `shrn`, as `src/simd.h` does, and clang drops the `sshr` when the lanes are the results of comparisons, as all of the lexer's are. On x86-64, and on arm64 with `CHIBI_SIMD_GENERIC`, the lanes stay in memory order and the generated code is the same as before; on x86-64 the translated compiler's object code is the same byte for byte.
       - Loads and stores: a load from memory outside the function's locals is `vld4q_u8`, and a store `vst4q_u8`. A local, which the backend finds by following an address back to a slot through constant indexes, fields and the copies of whole structs and arrays, loads with a 64-byte copy and a `__builtin_shufflevector` that deinterleaves, and stores with one that interleaves. Clang keeps such a local in registers and cancels each store's shuffle against the next load's, where an `ld4` or `st4` on the local's address would keep it in memory. The generated C's helpers are `chibi_u8x64_load`, `chibi_u8x64_store`, `chibi_u8x64_load_local` and `chibi_u8x64_store_local`, and the C backend now writes its vector helpers for every program with a 64-byte vector.
       - Measured: under qemu with clang 20, on the corpus's 34,601 lines, the translated lexer and parser run 784.36 instructions per line against 793.95 before, 32.35 more than the C build's 752.01 where they ran 41.94 more. In `lexer_tokenize`, four `ld4` replace the in-order loads, 84 `sri` replace 84 `addp`, and no shuffle is left: the `tbl` count stays at the 48 of the lookups. Checking and arm64 code generation run as before, so the three phases together run 2.75% more instructions than the C build, against 3.18% before. The C build makes the bytes one on from the block, `simd_next64`, out of the loaded registers with one `ext` and a load of one byte, and the translation loads them with a second `ld4`, so clang can't share the 3 registers of 4 that the two have in common: a hand patch of the generated C with the C build's `ext` measured 779.92 per line, 4.44 fewer.
       - Timed and reverted: GitHub's timing run of 064952c found it slower, and it was reverted the same day. On the M1 runner the translated lexer and parser took 6.3% more time than the C build's, beside 3.1% for 2b0c6d4 in the same run, and on the Neoverse N2 4.1% more cycles, where step 3k.29's took 2.1%, while running 4.1% more instructions, where step 3k.29's ran 5.5% more. The step changed two things, and the run can't tell them apart: the second `ld4`, which does the work of several instructions in one, and the `sri` chain in place of `and` and `addp`, which ran more instructions per cycle on the N2. A lane shift removes the first; after the switch it is chibi work that needs no translator.
       - Tests: the C backend's vector programs, among them `VECTOR_WIDE_RUNS` and `VECTOR_WIDE_GRID`, also ran on arm64 under qemu with the generated C compiled for arm64, which `make test` does only on x86-64 (CI runs them natively on arm64 Linux and macOS), and the arm64 bootstrap of `make translated` lexes the whole compiler with the new masks.
       - Planted bugs: 4, all caught on arm64: `sri` with its operands swapped, two lanes swapped in the interleave, a load without `ld4`, and a `bitcast` to `[64]u8` without the deinterleave.
       - Rules chosen while building, open to review:
         - Only `[64]u8` and `[64]i8` change order. The 64-byte vectors of wider lanes stay in memory order: nothing in the compiler masks them, and an `ld4` of wider lanes gives another order.
         - The order is chosen where the C compiles, by the same `CHIBI_NEON` test as the other helpers, so one generated file still serves every target.
       - Hand-off: no IR change. The generated C changes only for programs with 64-byte vectors.
       - Not built yet: a lane shift, such as `simd.shift_in(v, x)`, which the translator could write for `simd_next64` and which the C backend could lower to `ext` on NEON (the 4.44 instructions per line above); the same order in the arm64 backend.
4. **Threads:** declaration jobs queued before one job per function body, with dependencies claimed on demand (section 3).
5. **Lowering to bytecode,** so the tests' programs run in the interpreter. Each later backend runs the same programs, which makes one differential test.

   - **3x. The x86-64 development backend,** which the owner chose on 2026-11-25 to build before the switch, with debug info, so that x86-64 machines get the JIT that arm64 has ([../decisions.md](../decisions.md), Compiler). It shares one skeleton with the arm64 backend, `src/jit.c`, and the compiler builds in the file of its own architecture, as tcc, LuaJIT and V8's Liftoff do. Planned parts: the shared JIT memory and `native_*` API (3x.0); the split of `src/arm64.c` into the skeleton and the arm64 file, with the same arm64 code byte for byte and the same speed, and an allocator that can place a value in a given register (3x.1); integers and control flow on x86-64, with System V calls, the context in `rdi`, locals in registers by use count (only `rbx` and `r12`–`r15` survive a call) and traps that a signal handler can read (3x.2); debug info for both JIT backends through gdb's JIT interface, with a perf map (3x.3); division, shifts and bit counts, where x86-64-v2 has `popcnt` but not `tzcnt` or `lzcnt`, then floats (3x.4); pointers, arrays, structs, slices, bounds checks and stack probes (3x.5); calls into C, with System V's classes for structs, variadic calls, callbacks, exports and C globals (3x.6); optionals, errors and tuples, then 16- and 64-byte vectors and 128-bit integers (3x.7); and timing against the arm64 backend's code generation on the corpus (3x.8).
     - **3x.0 The JIT's memory and `native_*` API in their own file: built 2026-11-25.** `src/native.c` maps the function table, the data and the code, maps and fills the globals, turns writing to JIT memory on and off on Apple silicon and flushes the instruction cache on arm64; `arm64_compile` asks it for `code_size` bytes and says whether the host can run them. `Native` holds the code as bytes, `code` and `code_size`, in place of words, and `data`, the address of the program's data. `src/native.c` joins the translated files. The arm64 backend's code is the same, byte for byte, and on hosts that aren't arm64 its memory still maps without `PROT_EXEC`.
     - **3x.1 One skeleton for both backends, split from `src/arm64.c`: built 2026-11-25,** in the shape of LuaJIT's `lj_asm.c`, which includes `lj_target_*.h`, `lj_emit_*.h` and `lj_asm_*.h` for one architecture. `src/jit.c` is the backend's one translation unit. It holds what any architecture shares: the type summaries, which register or stack slot holds each value, spilling, moving live values into registers that survive a call, fixup lists, the open constructs of structured control flow and the values they hand over, giving locals registers by use count, frame layout, division's magic numbers, the loop over the IR and the driver. Between its parts it includes three arm64 files in `src/jit/`: `arm64_target.h` (registers, pools, the code unit and size limits), `arm64_encode.h` (the encoder, with the primitives the skeleton calls: store and load a spill, load an immediate, move a register, jump to a fixup, jump back, skip unless a register or the flags allow) and `arm64_select.h` (instruction selection, AAPCS64 and Apple's variant, prologues, epilogues, traps and adapters). Everything stays `static` in one unit, so clang inlines as before, and the translator writes each part to its own chibi file. The files sit in `src/jit/` because they don't stand alone, and `make translated` includes every `src/*.h` to check layouts. The public API is unchanged (`arm64_compile`, `src/arm64.h`); the shared allocator can't yet place a value in a given register, which x86-64 brings in 3x.2.
       - Tests: `make codehash` in `experiments/arm64-backend` made 18,370 compilations before and after, in both calling conventions: the 38 backend test programs and 3,000 random programs, each plain, release-fast and as an entry file; 32 programs taken from the C tests' string literals; the program with 33,000 error names; and the 694,816-line corpus, plain and release-fast. The code and data are the same, byte for byte, under `setarch -R`. `make test` passes on x86-64, and `make translated` passes its tests and rebuilds itself byte for byte.
       - Speed: on the Ryzen 9 9900X, pinned to one core, arm64 code generation takes 303.9 cycles per line against 303.0 before (three runs each, taking turns) and runs 163.2 instructions per IR instruction against 168.7, 3.3% fewer. The first order of the split took 308.8 cycles with the same instructions, and moving `signed_magic` and `unsigned_magic` to the end of the shared layer, beside their caller, gave the time back; the refactored code in the old file's order also ran in 303.6 cycles. Almost the whole backend inlines into `emit_function`, about 41 KB, so where clang places its blocks follows the order of the functions. On this machine, changes of up to 2% in code generation's cycles with the same instructions are that placement, and instruction counts are the signal to trust.

**Order, changed 2026-11-16 (owner):** self-hosting comes after 3b and 3d: globals and constant tables, calling C, SIMD and builtins, the C backend, the C-to-chibi translator and the bootstrap check (decisions.md). Then 3c, 3e, 3f, 4 and 5. On 2026-11-23 the owner chose to keep the C as the source while 3c, 3e and 3f are built in it and translated, and to revisit the switch after them (decisions.md).

**Tests.** Pure transforms are tested with tables:
- source and the expected IR dump;
- source and the expected diagnostic, with its line, column and message, so every rejection has a test;
- programs that run in the interpreter and check their results.

**Benchmark.** The parser's generated programs are valid syntax but don't type-check, so the checker needs a generator of well-typed programs. The parser thread proposes where things live:
- the generator in `test/random/`, which the Makefile already collects for tests and the bench;
- the benchmark as a function in a new file in `bench/`, with no `main`, called from `bench/bench.c` by the parser thread as one more phase of `make bench`.

The benchmark reports:
- cycles per line on 1 and 14 threads (H5, H3);
- IR bytes per line (H17);
- the time spent waiting on claims held by other threads (H14).

## 9. Questions for the owner

Questions 1, 4 and 5 shape the checker's structure, so they came before any checker code; all three are decided. Questions 6, 7 and 9 come up in steps 2 and 3 of section 8, or wait for their own syntax decisions, and their recommended answers are cheap to change later.

Decided by the owner on 2026-11-12, and recorded in decisions.md:
- **1. What the checker reads** (section 1): one forward pass over the postorder nodes, plus two parser additions, a list of top-level nodes and builtin names with fixed ids.
- **2. One id per name** (section 2.1): per-thread name tables, merged after parsing.
- **3. Shadowing** (section 2.2): option C, the rule of D, C# and Java. A local may not hide another local or parameter of the same function, may reuse package and predeclared names, and builtin type names such as `int` stay reserved.
- **4. Order of checking** (section 3): each phase of a declaration claimed on demand, with declaration jobs queued before bodies and no barrier.
- **5. The IR's shape** (sections 5 and 5.5): explicit operands in program order, locals in slots, and every value used once.
- **8. Guaranteed tail calls** (section 6.2): written `@tail return f(x)`. Their rules are still open (question 8 below).
- **10. Lengths** (section 4.4): arrays, slices and strings have a read-only `.len` field, and there is no predeclared `len`.

Decided by the owner on 2026-11-15 ("All of the recommendations look great"), and recorded in decisions.md: questions 11 to 16 below, each as recommended.

Decided by the owner on 2026-11-14, from section 8's review list: discarding a value takes `_ =`, and parameters and `for` variables are read-only, both as in Zig.

Decided by the owner on 2026-11-13:
- **6. Untyped constants** (section 4.2): integers in 64 bits with a big-integer fallback up to 512 bits, and floats as exact fractions, both as in Go. The recommendation was revised on 2026-11-13 from 128-bit integers and f64 floats, after measuring how often f64 folding differs.
- **7. Literals that take their type from context** (section 4.3): option C, the expected type where a marker gives one, and a provisional value until the operator that uses it otherwise.

Open:
8. **Tail-call rules** (section 6.2). The callee's type equals the caller's; a pending `defer` or `defer catch`, the address of a local in the arguments, varargs and GPU code are errors; and an opt-in register-heavy convention for handlers, spelled once attributes are decided? Recommended: yes.
9. **Computed `goto`** (section 6.3). Leave out labels as values. Instead, chibi's own backends promise one indirect jump per `continue name value`, and a direct jump when the value is known at compile time. Recommended: yes.


**Step 3 questions (2026-11-15), all decided as recommended on 2026-11-15.** The language surface of step 3 is mostly decided; these are the choices of representation and lowering it still needs. Each is cheap to state now and expensive to change once three backends read it.

11. **`switch` in the IR.**
    - **A. A `switch` instruction** with its cases in the side array, each case a value or range and an arm. Zig's AIR (`switch_br`), LLVM (`switch`), WebAssembly (`br_table`) and SPIR-V (`OpSwitch`) all have one. Each backend picks a jump table, a binary search or compares. The labeled-switch dispatch that question 9 recommends (`continue sw value`) needs it: one indirect jump per site is only possible if the backend sees the whole switch.
    - **B. Nested `if`s.** No new instruction, but a backend can't build a jump table without recognizing the chain again, and the GPU writers lose `OpSwitch`.
    - Recommended: A.
12. **Pointer kinds** (adopt-or-settle.md 2.5, rank 5, still open). Recommended there, as in Zig: `*T` is never nil and has no indexing or arithmetic; `[*]T` points into an array, indexes without checks and supports `p + n`; `?*T` may be nil, is the same size, and nil is address 0. Odin has the same split (`^T` and `[^]T`); C, Go and C3 have one nullable kind. Step 3 can start with `*T` and `?*T` only, and add `[*]T` with calls to C.
13. **Bounds checks** (adopt-or-settle.md 2.9, rank 9, still open). Recommended there: indexing and slicing check bounds in every build, and a block opts out with an attribute, as Odin's `#no_bounds_check` does. Rust, Go and Swift always check; Zig checks only in its safe build modes.
    - Measured 2026-11-15 on the M4 Pro with clang (-O1 and -O2, vectorizing off): a sequential sum and a random gather over 4,096 `i64`s ran within ±5% of the unchecked loops, about 0.24 ns per element either way. The cost of checks is not the compare and branch but the vectorizing they can prevent in release builds, which the opt-out covers.
14. **Structs, arrays and slices in the IR.**
    - **A. In memory only.** A struct, array or slice local lives in a slot; the IR gets `addr` (of a slot), `field` and `index` (typed addresses that keep field numbers for the GPU writers), and `load`/`store` through an address. Calls pass them through a hidden pointer. This is WebAssembly's and Cranelift's model, and Rust's MIR places; it maps directly onto SPIR-V's access chains. Simple for every backend, but a slice's pointer and length are reloaded on every use.
    - **B. As values.** Aggregates are IR values with field reads and builds, as in LLVM's first-class aggregates and Zig's AIR. Each backend must keep values that need two or more registers.
    - **C. A, plus pairs.** Values of two scalars, a slice, `?T` and `E!T` for a scalar `T`, are values held in two registers; everything else is in memory. Rust lowers such types as a scalar pair, and Go's SSA backend splits structs of up to four fields into scalars.
    - Recommended: C, after prototyping A and C on a slice loop and timing both, as with `and`/`or`. Locals of a struct type whose address is never taken could later be split into scalar slots by the checker, so the register choice of 2026-11-15 applies to their fields.
    - **Timed on 2026-11-19, on x86-64** ([experiments/slice-lowering](../../experiments/slice-lowering/README.md)), with machine code written as a one-pass backend would emit it. Indexing a slice that doesn't change took 1.5 cycles per element in both forms. A loop that reads the first byte and drops it, as a lexer does, took 2.0–2.1 cycles per byte with C and 2.7–2.9 with A, which loads and stores both halves of the slice at every step. A call per byte that passes the remaining slice took 4.4–5.1 cycles with C and 5.0–5.5 with A. The owner's M4 Pro was not available; llvm-mca can't stand in for it, because LLVM 18 models Apple cores with the 2013 Cyclone core and doesn't model a store forwarded to a later load.
15. **`defer`.**
    - **A. Copied to each exit.** The deferred code is checked once into a side buffer and copied, with its values renumbered, at every exit from its scope. Zig stores a defer body once and analyzes it at each exit; Odin and C3 also expand defers at each exit. No run-time state, so it is the fastest; the code grows with exits times defers.
    - **B. One cleanup block per scope** that each exit jumps to, with a variable saying where to go after it, as C code does with `goto cleanup`. Smaller code, one extra branch and store per exit.
    - **C. A run-time list of deferred calls,** as Go did before 1.14; Go then moved simple cases to open-coded defers, which is A.
    - Recommended: A.
16. **Optionals and error unions in memory and in calls.**
    - `?*T` is the pointer, with nil at address 0 (Zig guarantees this; Rust's `Option<&T>` does the same).
    - `?T` for other `T` is `{ T value; bool present }` in C layout, as adopt-or-settle.md 2.5 proposes.
    - `E!T` is `{ T value; u16 code }`, with code 0 for success, as the error-code row proposes (Zig's error type defaults to `u16`).
    - In calls, `T` returns in `x0` or `d0` and the code in `w1`, so `try f()` is `cbnz w1, error_path` after the call. Swift returns its error in a dedicated register (`x21` on arm64); Rust returns a two-scalar `Result` in two registers.
    - Recommended: as listed.

The memory model stays open; step 3 needs no heap, only pointers to locals, arrays and slices of them.

## 10. Not verified

- **Jai.** Its checker, its message loop and how it waits on dependencies have no public primary source, so this sheet doesn't rely on Jai.
- **Go.** No published lines-per-second figure for its type checker was found.
- **TypeScript.** Which release first ships the import-graph partitioning of PR #4313 was not checked.
- **rustc.** Its module-level name tables were not read.
- **Real code.** Every per-line figure in this sheet comes from the parser's generated programs. H1 asks whether those predict real code, and is still untested.
- **Dispatch.** These were not confirmed in a primary source:
  - what Zig does with a `defer` pending at `.always_tail`;
  - whether GCC's and Clang's AArch64 `preserve_none` are the same convention;
  - which MSVC version added `__preserve_none`;
  - why Cranelift gave its `tail` convention callee-saved registers back in 2024;
  - whether C3's `@jump` gives each `nextcase` its own jump;
  - Metal's rules on recursion and function pointers;
  - whether WG14 has published the Technical Specification that N3266 and N3582 propose (`return goto f(x)`).
- **IR sizes.** No compiler publishes IR instructions per source line, and none publishes a backend's cost per IR instruction (TPDE, Cranelift, Liftoff).
- **This sheet's own measurements** ran while other sessions kept the load average at 10–13. The cycle counters are per process, but the ranges are wider than on an idle machine.
